W1 Exact words: Negligence

Use this page when…

  • The question says "claim for damages under Tort Law", "negligence" or "Advise [P]", and someone was physically hurt (a fall, a car crash, a swimming accident, bad surgery) because somebody else was careless.
  • The person who caused it is a venue / festival / shop / event organiser, a council or "Authority" (beach, lake, road, park), a driver, a doctor, an employer or a manufacturer.
  • Someone lost money because they relied on careless advice, a report or a reference (negligent misstatement), and the question asks about tort.
  • The facts list losses: medical bills, lost wages, rehab for an addiction, PTSD/depression therapy, sometimes a strange extra loss (failed exam, lost holiday). Negligence was in 7 of the 11 past papers, usually as Q1.
THE ORDER — your booklet headings
  1. Issue (with the opening line above it)
  2. Law: Negligence generally
  3. 1. Duty of care — then three mini-headings: Law · Application · Conclusion
  4. 2. Breach of duty — Law · Application · Conclusion
  5. 3. Causation — Law · Application with ONE sub-heading per loss: (a) …, (b) …, (c) … · Conclusion
  6. 4. Defences — Law · Application · Conclusion
  7. 5. Remedies — Law · Application (the $ sum) · Conclusion
  8. Overall conclusion

Then: Full mini-answer (Oliver) · Glossary. Spend the most words on Breach and Causation (about 1,500 words in 55 min).

Issue

What this means:

The Issue is one question that names the injured person (the plaintiff, "P"), the person you say is to blame (the defendant, "D") and the claim ("the tort of negligence"). Do NOT write "Did D owe P a duty of care?" — the markers called that "misguided".

WRITE — Opening line (above the heading)

Whether [P] can recover damages from [D] depends on whether each element of the tort of negligence (duty, breach and causation) can be established, and whether any defences apply.

WRITE — Issue

Can [P] bring an action against [D] for the tort of negligence?

Example (2026 S1 Oliver): Whether Oliver can recover damages from Skyline Events ('Skyline') depends on whether each element of the tort of negligence (duty, breach and causation) can be established, and whether any defences apply. Issue: Can Oliver bring an action against Skyline for the tort of negligence?

Pick the ONE row that matches your facts (add its sentence straight after the Issue):

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
Only one person could be to blame, and the question names them.
(Nothing extra — the Issue above is enough.)
—
Someone else may also be to blame (e.g. SoundCo's speaker, a drunk driver, a surgeon), but the question asks about one D.
[P] may also have a separate claim against [SoundCo / the driver / the surgeon], but this advice is confined to [D].
—

Law: Negligence generally

What this means:

A tort is a "civil wrong": something one person does to another that lets the victim sue for money (damages), as opposed to a crime. Negligence is the tort of carelessly hurting someone. On the balance of probabilities means "more likely than not" — that is how much P has to prove. The CLA is the Civil Liability Act 2003 (Qld), the Queensland statute that sets the rules for breach (s 9) and causation (s 11).

WRITE — Law

A tort is a civil wrong that allows the injured person to sue the wrongdoer for damages. A person commits the tort of negligence if they carelessly cause harm to another. To succeed, [P] must establish on the balance of probabilities that: (1) [D] owed them a duty of care; (2) [D] breached that duty by failing to meet the standard of the reasonable person; and (3) the breach caused [P]'s loss, which is within the scope of [D]'s liability. Any defences and the remedies available must then be considered. The common law applies together with the Civil Liability Act 2003 (Qld) ('CLA').

Example (2026 S1 Oliver): the same paragraph with P = Oliver and D = Skyline.

1. Duty of care

What this means:

A duty of care is a legal obligation to take reasonable care not to hurt someone. An established category is a relationship the courts have ALREADY decided always carries a duty of care, so you don't have to argue it — e.g. a driver always owes a duty to other people on the road. A public authority (a council or government "Authority") is different: it only owes a duty if the danger was hidden, not if it was obvious.

WRITE — Law (always write this first)

The onus is on [P] to establish that [D] owed them a duty of care. In Donoghue v Stevenson [1932] AC 562, Mrs Donoghue drank a ginger beer that her friend had bought, a decomposed snail floated out of the opaque bottle, and the manufacturer was held to owe her a duty of care even though there was no contract between them. Lord Atkin's 'neighbour principle' requires a person to 'take reasonable care to avoid acts or omissions which you can reasonably foresee would be likely to injure your neighbour', being persons 'so closely and directly affected' by the act that they ought reasonably to be in contemplation. Later cases recognised established categories of relationship in which a duty of care automatically arises.

Pick the ONE row that matches your facts. Copy its Law sentence straight after the paragraph above. Copy its Application sentences under your "Application" mini-heading.

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
Occupier → visitor. D runs or controls the place where P got hurt: shop, supermarket, restaurant, pub, hotel, gym, festival, event, venue, private leisure centre, landlord's building. P was allowed to be there.
Law: An occupier of premises owes a duty of care to all persons entering the premises to ensure that the premises are safe: Australian Safeway Stores Pty Ltd v Zaluzna (1987), where a supermarket occupier was held to owe a duty to people entering its store.
Application: [D] was the occupier of [the premises] because it [organised / managed / had control over] [the site / the flooring / the building]. [P] entered as [a paying patron / customer / guest]. Similar to the supermarket in Zaluzna, [D] controlled the premises that [P] was invited onto, so the relationship falls within the established occupier–entrant category. It was also reasonably foreseeable that carelessness about [the flooring] could injure someone in [P]'s position, so [P] was [D]'s 'neighbour'.
 Australian Safeway Stores v Zaluzna (1987) + Donoghue
Occupier → trespasser. P was somewhere they were NOT allowed (storeroom, staff-only area, after hours, climbed a fence).
Law: An occupier owes a duty of care to persons entering its premises (Zaluzna), and a duty may be owed even to persons who are on the premises without permission: Hackshaw v Shaw (1984).
Application: Although [P] was [in the storeroom / on the site after hours] without permission, similar to Hackshaw v Shaw, where a duty was owed to a person on the land without permission, [D] as occupier still owed [P] a duty of care, because it was reasonably foreseeable that [people would enter that area].
 Hackshaw v Shaw (1984); Zaluzna
Motorist → road user. D was driving (car, motorbike, truck) and P was another driver, a passenger, a cyclist or a pedestrian.
Law: It is an established category that motorists owe a duty of care to other road users.
Application: [D], as a motorist driving on [the freeway], owed [P], [another driver / a pedestrian on the footpath], a duty of care. Applying the neighbour principle in Donoghue, it is plainly foreseeable that careless driving could injure [other road users / people beside the road].
 Established category (motorist); Donoghue
Doctor → patient. D is a doctor, surgeon or psychiatrist who treated P (e.g. a surgeon who made mistakes while drunk).
Law: It is an established category that doctors owe a duty of care to their patients.
Application: [Dr Phillips] was [P]'s [surgeon / treating doctor], so the relationship falls within the established doctor–patient category and a duty of care was owed. [The hospital] is also likely to be vicariously liable, because [Dr Phillips] performed [the surgery] in the course of [his] employment (Deatons v Flew (1949)).
 Established category (doctor); Deatons v Flew (1949)
Manufacturer → consumer. P was hurt by a product D made (food, drink, appliance), even if someone else bought it.
Law: It is an established category that manufacturers owe a duty of care to the ultimate consumers of their products: Donoghue v Stevenson, where the maker of the ginger beer owed a duty to Mrs Donoghue although she had no contract with it.
Application: [D] manufactured [the product] and [P] was its ultimate consumer. Similar to Donoghue, where the drink was bought by a friend, [D] owed [P] a duty of care even though [P] did not buy [the product] from [D].
 Donoghue v Stevenson [1932]
Employer → employee. P was hurt while working for D.
Law: It is an established category that employers owe a duty of care to their employees, as in Paris v Stepney Borough Council (1951), where an employer owed a duty to a worker doing dangerous work.
Application: [P] was employed by [D] as [a warehouse worker] and was injured while [doing his job]. The relationship falls within the established employer–employee category, so [D] owed [P] a duty of care.
 Established category (employer); Paris v Stepney
A staff member was the careless one and P sues the business (vicarious liability). NOT for an independent contractor like SoundCo — that's a separate defendant.
Law: An employer may be vicariously liable for the acts or omissions of its employees if they occurred during authorised work or in the course of employment: Century v Northern Ireland (1942); Deatons v Flew (1949).
Application: The careless [act / omission] was done by [a Skyline staff member] while [inspecting the flooring], which was part of [his] authorised work. Therefore [D] is vicariously liable for it, in addition to owing [P] its own duty as [occupier].
 Century v Northern Ireland (1942); Deatons v Flew (1949)
Professional → client (an act, not advice). D is P's accountant or banker and caused the harm by something D did.
Law: It is an established category that [accountants / bankers] owe a duty of care to their clients.
Application: [D] acted as [P]'s [accountant] when [lodging the documents], so the relationship falls within an established category and [D] owed [P] a duty of care.
 Established category (accountant/banker → client)
Public authority — HIDDEN risk. A council / "Authority" / government body controls a beach, lake, pool, river or park. The danger could not be seen (underwater rock, sandbar, exposed pipe, current). D invited people to use it (a "Swimming area" or "Safe" sign, flags, patrols, a designated zone, a festival).
Law: Public authorities, such as councils and government bodies, often control public spaces such as parks, roads and waterways and may be occupiers of them. However, it is questionable whether they automatically owe a duty of care. This depends on whether there was a hidden risk that required a warning or prevention, or whether the risk was so obvious to a reasonable person that no duty would exist. In Nagle v Rottnest Island Authority (1993), a submerged rock in an area signposted as a 'Swimming area' was a hidden risk, and because the sign encouraged people to swim there a duty to warn was owed. Similarly, in Swain v Waverley (2005), a submerged sandbar between the flags at a patrolled beach was a hidden risk giving rise to a duty to warn. By contrast, in Romeo v Conservation Commission (1998) (an unfenced cliff top) and Vairy v Wyong Shire Council (2005) (diving from a natural rock platform at an unpatrolled, unsigned beach) the risks were obvious and no liability arose.
Application: [D] is a public authority that [maintains and controls] [the lake], so it is in the position of an occupier. Similar to Nagle, where a 'Swimming area' sign encouraged swimming over a submerged rock, [D] encouraged [swimming] by [designating a swimming zone / putting up flags / holding a festival]. Like the rock in Nagle and the sandbar in Swain, the [exposed pipe and current] was [underwater and invisible], so it was a hidden risk. [D] will argue, relying on Romeo and Vairy, that [deep water] is an obvious danger. However, those cases concerned natural features whose dangers anyone could see, whereas the danger that actually happened here was [a concealed current] that a reasonable person would not have known about. Therefore [D] owed [P] a duty to warn of, or prevent, the hidden risk.
 Nagle v Rottnest (1993); Swain v Waverley (2005); distinguish Romeo (1998), Vairy (2005)
Public authority — hidden ROAD hazard. A council or road authority maintains a road or footpath; a pothole or defect was hard to see; often residents complained.
Law: (Write the public authority paragraph from the row above.)
Application: [D] [manages and maintains] [Freshman Street], so as the public authority in control of the road it is in the position of an occupier. The pothole's [angle and position] made it [not easily visible to road users]. Similar to the submerged rock in Nagle and the sandbar in Swain, it was a hidden risk, unlike the obvious cliff edge in Romeo. Applying Donoghue, it is reasonably foreseeable that a car hitting a hidden pothole could lose control and strike [a pedestrian walking along that street], so [P] was [D]'s neighbour and [D] owed [P] a duty of care.
 Nagle; Swain; distinguish Romeo; Donoghue
Public authority — OBVIOUS risk. The danger was a natural feature anyone could see (cliff edge, rock platform, rocks in shallow water), the place was unpatrolled, had no signs, and D did not invite the activity. (Then still do Breach etc., starting "If a duty were owed…".)
Law: (Write the public authority paragraph from the "hidden risk" row.) Under s 15 CLA there is no duty to warn of an obvious risk.
Application: Similar to Romeo, where the danger of an unfenced cliff top was obvious, and Vairy, where the danger of diving from a natural rock platform at an unpatrolled, unsigned beach was obvious, the danger of [diving off the rocks] was [plainly visible / a natural feature]. [D] did nothing to invite people to [dive there]: unlike Nagle, there was no sign encouraging the activity, and unlike Swain, there were no flags or patrols. Under s 15 CLA, [D] had no duty to warn of an obvious risk. Therefore [D] probably did not owe [P] a duty of care. If a duty were owed, the following analysis applies.
 Romeo (1998); Vairy (2005); s 15 CLA; distinguish Nagle, Swain
Negligent misstatement — advice to P. The harm came from WORDS: advice, a report, an inspection, a reference, a recommendation. P said something like "I'm relying on you". P usually lost money.
Law: A person giving advice may owe a duty of care where: (1) the advice is of a business or serious nature; (2) they know or ought to know that the recipient intends to rely on it; and (3) it is reasonable in the circumstances for the recipient to rely on it. The adviser need not be a professional, but reliance is more easily reasonable where the adviser holds themselves out as having special skill or expertise: Hedley Byrne v Heller (1964), where a bank gave a careless credit reference; Rentokill v Channon (1990), where an incorrect pest inspection report was relied on to buy a house that later had to be demolished, and a duty was held to be owed and breached.
Application: First, the advice concerned [the settlement deadline for the sale of his house], which is a serious business matter, not a casual social conversation. Second, [D] knew that [P] would rely on it because [P told him so / giving that advice was his job]. Third, reliance was reasonable because [D] [is a qualified conveyancer / held himself out as an expert in …]. Similar to Rentokill, where the buyer relied on a professional pest report, [P] relied on [D]'s expertise. Therefore [D] owed [P] a duty of care in giving the advice.
 Hedley Byrne v Heller (1964); Rentokill v Channon (1990)
Advice + a disclaimer. The advice came with words like "without responsibility" / "no liability accepted".
Law: In Hedley Byrne v Heller, the House of Lords said a duty was owed because of the bank's expertise and its knowledge that the reference would be relied on, but the bank was not liable only because the reference carried a disclaimer.
Application: [D] will rely on the statement '[wording of disclaimer]'. Similar to Hedley Byrne, where the disclaimer protected the bank, this is likely to defeat [P]'s claim if it covered the advice [P] relied on.
 Hedley Byrne v Heller (1964)
Advice relied on by a THIRD party. D advised someone else (its client), and P — not the client — relied on it (e.g. a buyer, a lender, an investor).
Law: An adviser generally owes no duty of care to a third party who relies on the advice, unless the adviser knew or ought to have known that (i) the advice would be passed on to the third party, (ii) it was likely to lead them into a particular type of transaction, and (iii) they would be likely to suffer financial loss if it was wrong: Esanda Finance v Peat Marwick (1997), where an auditor's advice was relied on by a third party.
Application: [P] was not [D]'s client; [D] gave the [report] to [client]. Similar to Esanda, [D] generally owes [P] no duty. However, [D] knew that [the report would be shown to buyers], that it would lead [P] to [buy the business], and that [P] would lose money if it was wrong, so the exception applies and a duty is owed. [OR: Nothing suggests [D] knew [P] would see it, so no duty is owed.]
 Esanda v Peat Marwick (1997)
A family member / bystander who WATCHED P get hurt and now has a psychiatric illness (e.g. a child who saw his father injured).
Law: Nervous shock, or mental and psychiatric injury, is a recognised type of injury in negligence; Mrs Donoghue herself suffered nervous shock in Donoghue v Stevenson. Whether a duty is owed to [Bill] depends on whether it was reasonably foreseeable that [D]'s conduct could harm someone in his position (the neighbour principle).
Application: [Bill] was [in the water beside his father], at a place [D] invited the public to use. It is reasonably foreseeable that if [a swimmer] is seriously injured by a hidden hazard, a family member beside him, particularly [a 10-year-old child], would witness it and suffer psychiatric harm. [Bill] is a person so closely and directly affected that [D] ought to have had him in contemplation, so a duty is likely owed. As the course does not teach separate mental-harm rules, this advice applies the neighbour principle and s 11 CLA.
 Donoghue v Stevenson (neighbour principle)
NO established category fits (novel duty). ONLY if no row above fits — markers penalised students who used this for an occupier, driver or employer.
Law: Where no established category applies, two tests must be satisfied: (1) whether it was reasonably foreseeable that [D]'s conduct could cause harm to someone in [P]'s position (Donoghue v Stevenson); and (2) whether the salient features of the case, including the relationship between the parties, control, relative knowledge, experience, vulnerability and reliance, and personal responsibility, are consistent with a duty of care.
Application: First, it was reasonably foreseeable that [D]'s [conduct] could harm someone in [P]'s position because […]. Second, the salient features support a duty: [D] had control over […]; [D] knew more than [P] about […]; and [P] was vulnerable because […] and relied on [D] to […]. Against a duty, [P] could have taken personal responsibility by […]. On balance, the salient features are consistent with a duty of care, so a duty is owed.
 Donoghue + salient features

WRITE — Application (after your row's sentences)

[Your row's Application sentences.] Because the relationship falls within an established category, it is not necessary to consider the salient features of a novel duty. [If D is private: [D] is a private [event organiser], not a public authority, so the hidden-risk analysis applicable to public authorities is not required.]

Example (2026 S1 Oliver): Skyline was the occupier of the festival site because it organised the festival and controlled the site, including the temporary flooring near the main stage. Oliver entered as a festival patron (it is assumed he held a valid ticket). Similar to the supermarket in Zaluzna, Skyline controlled the premises that Oliver was invited onto, so the relationship falls within the established occupier–entrant category. It was also reasonably foreseeable that carelessness about the flooring could injure someone in Oliver's position, so Oliver was Skyline's 'neighbour'. Because the relationship falls within an established category, it is not necessary to consider the salient features of a novel duty. Skyline is a private event organiser, not a public authority, so the hidden-risk analysis applicable to public authorities is not required.

WRITE — Conclusion

Therefore, [D] owed [P] a duty of care.

2. Breach of duty

What this means:

Breach means D did not take the care a sensible ("reasonable") person in D's position would have taken. A precaution is a step that stops the harm (a sign, a barrier, a repair, closing the area). Section 9(1) of the CLA has three requirements, (a), (b) and (c); s 9(2) gives four factors the court weighs to decide (c). Write a sentence for EVERY one — markers noted students "missed the factors in s 9(2)".

WRITE — Law

The existence of a duty does not automatically mean it was breached. Under s 9(1) CLA, a person breaches their duty by failing to take precautions against a risk of harm if three requirements are met. Under s 9(1)(a), the risk must have been foreseeable, meaning a risk the person knew or ought reasonably to have known about. Under s 9(1)(b), the risk must have been not insignificant. Under s 9(1)(c), in the circumstances a reasonable person in their position would have taken those precautions. At common law, the reasonable person is someone of normal intelligence, credited with the perception of the surrounding circumstances and the knowledge that the relevant person would normally possess.
Under s 9(2) CLA, in deciding whether a reasonable person would have taken precautions, the court considers four factors. Under s 9(2)(a), it considers the probability that the harm would occur if care were not taken. Under s 9(2)(b), it considers the likely seriousness of the harm; in Paris v Stepney Borough Council (1951), an employer breached its duty by failing to give goggles to a worker who was already blind in one eye, because harm to him would be so serious. Under s 9(2)(c), it considers the burden of taking precautions to avoid the risk. Under s 9(2)(d), it considers the social utility of the activity that creates the risk; in Watt v Hertfordshire County Council (1954), there was no breach because the urgent rescue of a life justified carrying unsecured equipment in a vehicle, which then injured a fireman.
In Bolton v Stone (1951), a cricket ball was hit out of the ground and struck a woman standing on the road outside. Balls had very rarely cleared the ground in many years, so the probability of harm was extremely low, and the burden of eliminating that risk, such as building a much higher fence or closing the ground, was high. The court held that a reasonable person would not have taken further precautions, so there was no breach.

Add every row that matches D's situation or excuse (put the sentence under the matching s 9 point in your Application):

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
D actually knew about the danger (complaints, reports, an inspection, an internal report, a customer warned them).
The risk was foreseeable under s 9(1)(a) CLA because [D] actually knew of it: [two attendees reported the unstable flooring and a staff member inspected it at about 2pm].
s 9(1)(a)
D didn't know, but should have (a routine check would have found it; it had been there for months; visible wear).
Even if [D] did not actually know of the risk, it ought reasonably to have known of it under s 9(1)(a) CLA, because [the pothole had existed for months on a main road that it was responsible for maintaining], and a reasonable [council] carrying out routine inspections would have found it.
s 9(1)(a)
D says "nobody was hurt before" / "it's rare", but the danger was known and cheap to fix.
[D] may rely on Bolton v Stone, where the club was not liable because a ball had only rarely left the ground in many years. That case is distinguishable: here, [three warnings were received within a month], which shows the risk was likely rather than remote, and it could have been avoided at [little cost].
Bolton v Stone (distinguish)
It truly was a freak, rare event that would have been very expensive to prevent (D's best argument).
Similar to Bolton v Stone, where a ball had very rarely left the ground and the burden of preventing it was high, the risk of […] was [extremely remote] and could only have been eliminated by […], which would be a heavy burden. A reasonable person would not have taken further precautions, so [D] did not breach its duty.
Bolton v Stone (apply); s 9(2)(a), (c)
D gave a weak warning: only online (Facebook), a generic sign, a general verbal warning ("don't swim too far").
[D] will argue that it took reasonable precautions by [posting a warning on its Facebook page]. However, [patrons already on site, watching performances in the evening, are unlikely to read Facebook], and the warning [did not identify the exact location / did not mention the specific danger of the current] and left the hazard in place. It was therefore not what a reasonable person would have done under s 9(1)(c).
s 9(1)(c); s 9(2)(c)
D gave a clear, specific warning at the spot (e.g. "Uneven Surface – Take Care!" sign right at the hazard).
[D] placed a sign reading '[Uneven Surface – Take Care!]' at the hazard. [P] will argue a sign alone was not enough because [the hazard remained and could have been repaired or cordoned off cheaply]. [D] will argue the sign was a reasonable precaution. On balance, [because the hazard was serious and cheap to fix, a warning alone was not enough / the sign was a reasonable precaution]. (Any argument that [P] saw the sign also goes to contributory negligence.)
s 9(1)(c); s 9(2)(b), (c)
D's excuse: staff shortage, cost, closing would be expensive.
Under s 9(2)(c) CLA, the burden of taking precautions was low. [D] may argue that [a shortage of staff / the cost of closing the lake] made precautions burdensome. However, [staffing levels were within D's own control], and even if [repairs had to wait], [placing a physical sign or barrier tape] required minimal resources.
s 9(2)(c)
D's excuse: the activity was useful (a festival, a business, community event).
Under s 9(2)(d) CLA, [the festival] had [community and recreational] value. However, unlike the emergency rescue in Watt v Hertfordshire, it was not urgent, and its [commercial] purpose does not justify exposing [patrons] to a known danger.
Watt v Hertfordshire (distinguish); s 9(2)(d)
D really was dealing with an emergency (ambulance, fire service, rescue of a life).
Similar to Watt v Hertfordshire, where the urgent need to save a life justified the risk of carrying unsecured equipment, [D] was [rushing to rescue …]. The high social utility of that activity under s 9(2)(d) CLA justified the risk, so [D] did not breach its duty.
Watt v Hertfordshire (apply); s 9(2)(d)
P was especially vulnerable, or D knew vulnerable people would be there (child, elderly person, someone with a disability, a crowd of drinkers).
Similar to Paris v Stepney, where the employer had to provide goggles because the worker was already blind in one eye, [D] knew that [children / intoxicated patrons] would be [using the area], so the likely seriousness of harm under s 9(2)(b) was greater and a reasonable person would have done more.
Paris v Stepney; s 9(2)(b)
D was a careless driver (speeding, on the phone, drunk, changed lanes).
[Driving over the legal alcohol limit / speeding on the freeway] created a foreseeable and not insignificant risk of a collision. Under s 9(2), the probability and seriousness of harm were high and the burden of [not drinking / keeping to the speed limit] was nil, so a reasonable driver would have [not driven / driven more slowly]. [D] breached the duty owed to [P].
s 9(1), s 9(2)(a)–(c)
D was a doctor who made mistakes (operated drunk, "critical mistakes").
A reasonable surgeon would not operate while [intoxicated by alcohol]. The risk of [permanent injury to the patient] was foreseeable and not insignificant, the harm was likely to be serious (s 9(2)(b)), and the burden of [not operating / handing over to another surgeon] was low. [Dr Phillips] therefore breached the duty owed to [P].
s 9(1), s 9(2)
D gave wrong advice without checking (negligent misstatement).
A reasonable [conveyancer] in [D]'s position would have checked [the settlement date in the contract] before advising [P]. The risk that [P] would lose [the sale] if the advice was wrong was foreseeable and not insignificant, and the burden of checking was very low (s 9(2)(c)). Similar to Rentokill, where the incorrect pest report was a breach, [D] breached the duty owed to [P].
Rentokill v Channon; s 9

WRITE — Application (one sentence per point, in this order)

s 9(1)(a) Foreseeable: The risk of [patrons falling on unstable flooring] was foreseeable because [D] [actually knew of it: two attendees reported it and a staff member inspected it at 2pm].
s 9(1)(b) Not insignificant: The risk was not insignificant because [the flooring shifted when walked on and was near the main stage, the busiest part of the festival].
s 9(1)(c) Reasonable person: A reasonable [festival organiser] in [D]'s position would have [cordoned off the area, placed physical warning signs and repaired the flooring]. Instead, [D] only [posted a warning on Facebook]. The s 9(2) factors support this.
s 9(2)(a) Probability: The probability of harm was high because [large crowds would walk across the area into the evening, looking at the stage rather than the ground, many after drinking alcohol].
s 9(2)(b) Seriousness: The likely seriousness of harm was high because [a fall on shifting flooring in a crowd can cause serious back or head injuries]. As in Paris v Stepney, the more serious the potential harm, the more a reasonable person would do.
s 9(2)(c) Burden: The burden of taking precautions was low because [barrier tape, a physical sign or rerouting patrons is quick and inexpensive]. [D] may argue that [a staff shortage] made precautions burdensome, but [staffing was within its control and simpler measures were available].
s 9(2)(d) Social utility: [D]'s [festival] has [entertainment and commercial] value, but unlike the emergency rescue in Watt v Hertfordshire, nothing urgent justified leaving a known hazard in place. [OR: The social utility of [D]'s activity is not relevant here.]
Bolton comparison: This case is distinguishable from Bolton v Stone. There, the risk was remote because balls rarely left the ground, and the burden of eliminating it was high. Here, the risk was [known, reported and likely to eventuate] and could have been avoided at [little cost].
D's best argument and answer: [D] may argue it took reasonable steps by [posting a warning]. However, […], so it was not what a reasonable person would have done.

Example (2026 S1 Oliver): The risk of patrons falling on unstable flooring was foreseeable because Skyline actually knew of it: by about 2pm two attendees had reported it, and a Skyline staff member inspected it and posted a warning about 'uneven flooring'. The risk was not insignificant because the flooring shifted when walked on and was near the main stage, the busiest part of a large festival. A reasonable festival organiser would have cordoned off the area, placed physical warning signs and repaired the section. Instead, Skyline only posted a warning on Facebook. The probability of harm was high because crowds would cross the area into the evening, looking at the stage, many after drinking. The likely seriousness was high because a fall in a crowd can cause serious back or head injuries; as in Paris v Stepney, this demanded more than minimal precautions. The burden was low because barrier tape or a sign is quick and cheap; Skyline may argue a staff shortage prevented repairs, but staffing was within its control and a sign needed minimal resources. Unlike the emergency in Watt v Hertfordshire, nothing urgent justified leaving a known hazard in place. Unlike Bolton v Stone, the risk was specific, reported and likely, and cheap to prevent. Skyline's Facebook post was unlikely to reach patrons already on site at night, so it was not what a reasonable person would have done.

WRITE — Conclusion

Therefore, [D] breached its duty of care to [P] under s 9 CLA.

3. Causation

What this means:

Causation asks whether D's carelessness actually caused EACH loss, and whether that loss is not too far-fetched. A head of damage is one separate loss (the medical bill, the lost wages, the rehab). Give each one its own sub-heading — (a), (b), (c) — and run BOTH tests under each: factual causation ("but for D's carelessness, would this have happened?") and scope of liability (was this kind of loss foreseeable, or too remote?). The feedback said this is what separated HD answers from the rest.

WRITE — Law

Merely creating a risk is not actionable: the harm must actually occur, must flow from the breach and must not be too remote. Under s 11(1) CLA, a decision that a breach caused harm has two elements. Under s 11(1)(a), factual causation requires that the breach was a necessary condition of the occurrence of the harm. Under s 11(1)(b), scope of liability requires that it is appropriate for the scope of the liability of the person in breach to extend to the harm so caused.
Factual causation is tested by asking whether, 'but for' [D]'s carelessness, [P] would still have suffered the harm. In Yates v Jones [1990], Yates was injured in a car accident caused by Jones and, while in hospital, a friend suggested she take heroin for the pain. She became addicted, but the addiction was held not to have been caused by the accident: it was caused by the friend's actions, which broke the chain of causation.
[Only if there are 2+ causes:] The 'but for' test is not appropriate where harm has several causes. The court then applies the 'material contribution' test: whether the defendant's conduct increased the risk of injury and that risk eventuated, whether or not other factors also contributed. The breach need not be the sole cause: Chappel v Hart (1998) (McHugh J). In Cook v ACT Racing Club Inc [2001], an overheated sauna dehydrated a jockey, who then suffered a heart attack. Although dehydration was only one cause, the sauna significantly increased the risk of injury, so causation was established.
Under the scope of liability test, the type of harm suffered must have been a reasonably foreseeable consequence of the breach and not too remote: Overseas Tankships v Morts [1961], where oil spilt into Sydney Harbour later caught fire and burned a wharf, and because fire damage was not a foreseeable type of harm the defendant was not liable. In Schneider v Smith & Anor (2016), the plaintiff was injured in a motorcycle accident and turned to illegal drugs to cope with his pain without following medical advice. This was a deliberate, voluntary and unreasonable act that was not part of prescribed or recommended treatment, so causation was not established for the drug intake. However, his medical and psychological treatment costs were recoverable as the 'ordinary and natural consequences' of the accident, and lost income from his resignation was not recoverable because there were other causes unrelated to the accident.

Pick ONE row for EACH loss in your question (one sub-heading per loss). Each row gives the factual sentence, the scope sentence and the mini-conclusion.

If… (the type of loss)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
The physical injury itself, and pain and suffering (broken arm, back injury, lung damage).
([a]) The [back injury]. But for [D]'s failure to [repair, cordon off or signpost the flooring], [P] would not have [stepped onto the unstable section and fallen], so the breach was a necessary condition of the injury under s 11(1)(a). A [serious back injury] is exactly the type of harm that is reasonably foreseeable from [a patron falling on unstable flooring], so under s 11(1)(b) it is within [D]'s scope of liability. Unlike the unforeseeable fire damage in Overseas Tankships, this harm is not remote.

Consistent with Schneider, where treatment costs were recoverable as ordinary and natural consequences, the rehabilitation cost is a reasonably foreseeable consequence of the injury and is within [D]'s scope of liability.

[D] will argue that [P] broke the chain by taking them 'beyond the prescribed dose', relying on Schneider. However, this case can be distinguished from Schneider: the [opioids] were prescribed as part of [her] treatment, and [she] began exceeding the dose only 'as a result of the addiction', which itself developed from the prescribed use. It is also unlike Yates v Jones, because no third person suggested [she] take the drugs. Addiction is a reasonably foreseeable consequence of a strong prescribed painkiller, so on balance it is within [D]'s scope of liability and the $[10,500] is likely to be recoverable.

Addiction — P CHOSE to keep taking it beyond the prescription, without the doctor knowing (not recoverable). E.g. Matt 2022 S2.
([d]) Addiction therapy ($[7,500]). But for the accident, [P] would not have been prescribed the medication, and [he] took it as prescribed for [two months]. However, [P] chose to continue taking it for [a further three months] without [his psychiatrist] knowing. Similar to Schneider v Smith, this was a deliberate, voluntary act that was not part of prescribed or recommended treatment, and the law of torts is built on individual responsibility. The addiction is therefore outside the scope of [D]'s liability under s 11(1)(b), and the $[7,500] is not recoverable.

Similar to Yates v Jones, where the friend's suggestion of heroin, not the car accident, was held to cause the addiction, [her husband]'s suggestion is an intervening act that breaks the chain of causation. On balance, the addiction is outside [D]'s scope of liability and the $[20,000] is unlikely to be recoverable.

Example (2026 S1 Oliver): (a) Back injury, medical expenses ($36,000) and lost income. But for Skyline's failure to repair, cordon off or signpost the flooring, Oliver would not have stepped onto the unstable section and begun to fall. However, the medical evidence shows his injuries were caused by a combination of the unstable flooring and his sudden reaction to the noise from SoundCo's equipment, so the material contribution test applies: Skyline's breach increased the risk of injury and that risk eventuated, because Oliver was already unbalanced and falling when the noise occurred. WRITE — Conclusion

Therefore, [D]'s breach caused [the injury, medical expenses, lost income and rehabilitation costs], all of which are within the scope of its liability[, but not [the course fees / the addiction therapy]].

4. Defences

What this means:

A defence is a reason D pays less, or nothing, even though D was negligent. Contributory negligence = P was partly to blame for their own injury, so P's money is cut by a percentage. Voluntary assumption of risk = P knew the exact danger and freely accepted it, so D pays nothing; it is very hard to prove, and markers penalised students who raised it when P did not know of the danger. An obvious risk is one a reasonable person in P's position would have seen.

WRITE — Law

Even where all the elements of negligence are established, a defendant may reduce its liability by proving a defence. If the plaintiff contributed in some way to their own loss or injury, liability will be apportioned between the defendant and the plaintiff and damages reduced accordingly (contributory negligence): Ingram v Britten [1994], where the plaintiff drove a tractor at speed but the defendant had failed to secure a metal frame to it; Manley v Alexander, where the plaintiff drank 12 stubbies and slept in the middle of the road before the defendant negligently drove over him.
[Only if P knew the specific danger, or the danger was obvious:] Voluntary assumption of risk, which can relieve the defendant of all liability, is very difficult to establish. The plaintiff must have had full knowledge and appreciation of the precise risk that eventuated and freely and willingly accepted it: Agar v Hyde (2000), where rugby players injured in a collapsed scrum were held to have voluntarily assumed the risks inherent in the game. Under ss 13–14 CLA, where a risk is obvious to a reasonable person, the plaintiff is presumed to have been aware of it unless they prove on the balance of probabilities that they were not; under s 15 there is no duty to warn of an obvious risk; and under ss 17–19 a defendant is not liable for harm from an obvious risk of a dangerous recreational activity.

Pick the row(s) that match P's behaviour. If P did nothing careless, use the last row.

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
P had been drinking alcohol (intoxication).
[D] will argue contributory negligence because [P] had consumed [several alcoholic drinks]. Similar to Manley v Alexander, where the plaintiff drank 12 stubbies and fell asleep in the middle of the road before the defendant negligently drove over him, [P]'s intoxication likely reduced [his] awareness and balance and contributed to [his] injury. Liability is therefore likely to be apportioned between [D] and [P], although [D] remains liable for its own share.
Manley v Alexander
P was not looking / on their phone / distracted (pedestrian or driver).
Contributory negligence is relevant because [P] was [using her phone / looking towards the stage] and not paying attention to [the road / the ground in front of him]. Like the plaintiff in Ingram v Britten, [P] failed to take reasonable care for [his] own safety, so liability is apportioned between the parties. On one hand, [D] will argue that an attentive [pedestrian] may have [seen the hazard / reacted in time]. On the other hand, [P] will argue that [the flooring shifted unexpectedly / the car hit her suddenly], so that even with full attention [he] could not have avoided it. Any reduction is therefore likely to be [small / moderate].

P ignored signs or staff warnings (swam outside the flags, went past the barrier, swam too far out).

Against this, [he started in a designated zone and could not have known of the hidden current]. A significant reduction, perhaps around [30]%, is likely.

[P]'s damages are likely to be reduced by around [20]%.

Ingram v Britten
D raises voluntary assumption of risk, but P did NOT know the precise danger (it was hidden, or the warning was general). Use to dismiss it in 2–3 lines.
[D] is unlikely to succeed with voluntary assumption of risk. Unlike the rugby players in Agar v Hyde, who knew the risks inherent in their sport, there is no evidence that [P] knew of the precise risk [that the flooring was unstable / of the hidden current]; [he was warned only about swimming too far out / the warning was only online]. The precise risk that eventuated was not obvious under ss 13–15 CLA because [it only shifted when stepped on / it was underwater]. This defence is likely to fail.
Agar v Hyde (2000) (distinguish); ss 13–15 CLA
Voluntary assumption of risk genuinely applies: P chose a risky sport/activity and knew the exact danger.
Similar to the rugby players in Agar v Hyde, who were fully aware of the risks inherent in a scrum, [P] [had played … for years] and knew of the precise risk that […], which is the risk that eventuated. [P] freely and willingly accepted it by […]. Therefore [D] is likely to succeed with voluntary assumption of risk, which is a complete defence.
Agar v Hyde (apply)
The danger was obvious to a reasonable person.
Under ss 13–14 CLA, because the risk of […] would have been obvious to a reasonable person in [P]'s position, [P] is presumed to have been aware of it unless [he] proves on the balance of probabilities that [he] was not. Under s 15 CLA, [D] had no duty to warn of that obvious risk. [P] is unlikely to rebut the presumption because […].
ss 13–15 CLA
P was doing a dangerous recreational activity (extreme sport, diving off rocks, high-risk adventure).
[D] may argue that […] was a dangerous recreational activity and that the harm came from an obvious risk of it, so under ss 17–19 CLA [D] is not liable. However, [recreational swimming in a managed lake is unlikely to be a 'dangerous' recreational activity, and the risk that eventuated was hidden, not obvious]. This argument is likely to fail.
ss 17–19 CLA
P is an innocent witness or child who did nothing careless.
Contributory negligence does not apply to [Bill], who did nothing careless.
—
No defence applies — P did nothing careless and did not know of the danger.
There is no evidence that [P] contributed to [his] own injury: [he was walking normally where he was entitled to be]. Nor did [P] know of the precise risk, so voluntary assumption of risk does not arise. No defence applies, and [D] is liable for the full amount of [P]'s damages.

WRITE — Application

Contributory negligence: [D] will argue that [P] contributed to [his] injury by [consuming several alcoholic drinks and not watching where he was walking]. Similar to Manley v Alexander, where the plaintiff's intoxication contributed to his injury, [P]'s [intoxication] likely reduced [his] awareness and balance. Like the plaintiff in Ingram v Britten, [P] failed to take reasonable care for [his] own safety. On the other hand, […], so [D]'s failure remains the primary cause. On balance, a reduction of around [25]% is likely.
Voluntary assumption of risk (only if the facts invite it): [Use the "did NOT know the precise danger" row above.]

Example (2026 S1 Oliver): Skyline will argue that Oliver contributed to his injury by consuming several alcoholic drinks and looking towards the stage rather than at the ground in front of him. Similar to Manley v Alexander, where the plaintiff's intoxication contributed to his injury, Oliver's drinking likely affected his balance and awareness. Like the plaintiff in Ingram v Britten, he failed to take reasonable care for his own safety. On the other hand, the flooring shifted unexpectedly and patrons at a music festival can be expected to look at the stage, so Skyline's failure to address a known hazard remains the primary cause. It is assumed Oliver had not read the Facebook warning. On balance, a reduction of around 25% is likely. Skyline is unlikely to succeed with voluntary assumption of risk: unlike the rugby players in Agar v Hyde, there is no evidence Oliver knew the flooring was unstable, and the instability was not an obvious risk under ss 13–15 CLA because it only shifted when stepped on.

WRITE — Conclusion

Therefore, [P]'s damages are likely to be reduced for contributory negligence, but [D] has no complete defence. [OR: No defence applies.]

5. Remedies

What this means:

Damages = the money the court orders D to pay. Ordinary (compensatory) damages try to put P back where they would have been without the accident. They are assessed "once and for all" — P can't come back later — so future losses must be claimed now. Add up only the losses you said were caused (Causation), then cut the total by P's contributory negligence %. No calculators: pick round percentages.

WRITE — Law

The typical remedy for a tort is an award of damages, the primary purpose of which is to compensate the plaintiff, not to punish the defendant. Ordinary damages aim to restore the plaintiff, so far as money can do so, to the position they would have been in had the tort not been committed. They are assessed 'once and for all', so future losses must be estimated at the time of the award. Exemplary damages may be awarded where the defendant deliberately and maliciously disregarded the plaintiff's rights.

Use every row that matches a loss in your question:

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
Medical / hospital bills are given.
[P] may claim ordinary damages for [medical / hospital] expenses of $[36,000].
Ordinary damages
Rehab or therapy costs you found recoverable (prescribed addiction, PTSD therapy).
[P] may also claim the [rehabilitation program] cost of $[5,000], which was found to be within [D]'s scope of liability.
Ordinary damages
Lost wages for some MONTHS (annual salary given).
[P] lost [six] months' income on an annual salary of $[95,000], calculated as $[95,000] ÷ 12 × [6] = $[47,500].
Ordinary damages
Lost wages for WEEKS or DAYS (weekly or daily rate given).
[P] lost [6] weeks' income at $[5,000] per week, calculated as [6] × $[5,000] = $[30,000]. [OR: [P] may claim [2] days' lost wages of $[…].]
Ordinary damages
P can't work / compete the same in future.
Because damages are assessed once and for all, [P] should also claim an estimated amount for future loss of earning capacity, since [his chronic respiratory issues affect his ability to train and compete].
"Once and for all"
Pain and suffering (always add one line for a real injury).
[P] may also claim an amount for pain and suffering arising from [a serious back injury and extended hospitalisation].
Ordinary damages
A loss you decided was NOT caused / too remote.
The [course fees / addiction therapy] of $[…] is not included, as it falls outside [D]'s scope of liability.
—
D deliberately chose to ignore the danger (e.g. put a festival ahead of safety advice).
[P] may argue for exemplary damages because [D] consciously chose to [put the festival ahead of known safety recommendations]. However, as [D]'s conduct was careless rather than a deliberate and malicious disregard of [P]'s rights, such an award is unlikely.
Exemplary damages
An amount is missing from the facts.
The facts do not state the cost of [the psychiatric treatment], so it is assumed [P] can prove this amount with receipts.
—

WRITE — Application

[P] may claim ordinary damages for: [medical expenses of $36,000]; [rehabilitation costs of $5,000]; and [lost income for six months], calculated as [$95,000 ÷ 12 × 6 = $47,500].
Sum-up sentence: These total $[36,000] + $[5,000] + $[47,500] = $[88,500]. [P] may also claim an amount for pain and suffering and, because damages are assessed once and for all, for any future medical costs and loss of earning capacity caused by [the injury].
% reduction sentence: Applying a [25]% reduction for contributory negligence, [P] would recover [75]% of $[88,500] = $[66,375], plus [75]% of any general damages awarded.

Example (2026 S1 Oliver): Oliver may claim ordinary damages for medical expenses of $36,000; the rehabilitation program of $5,000; and lost income for six months, calculated as $95,000 ÷ 12 × 6 = $47,500. These total $36,000 + $5,000 + $47,500 = $88,500. Oliver may also claim an amount for pain and suffering from a serious back injury and extended hospitalisation and, because damages are assessed once and for all, any future medical costs or loss of earning capacity supported by medical evidence. Applying a 25% reduction for contributory negligence, Oliver would recover 75% of $88,500 = $66,375, plus 75% of any general damages awarded.

Quick maths: 6 months = annual ÷ 2 · 3 months = annual ÷ 4 · 12 months = annual · 25% off = × ¾ · 30% off = × 0.7 (e.g. 70% of $257,000 = $179,900) · 10% = move the decimal point.

WRITE — Conclusion

Therefore, [P] is entitled to ordinary damages of approximately $[66,375], plus a reduced amount for pain and suffering and any future losses.

Overall conclusion

What this means:

One sentence that answers the Issue question, starting "On the balance of probabilities" (= more likely than not), naming the losses and the % cut.

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
P wins (the usual answer).
On the balance of probabilities, [P] can bring an action against [D] in the tort of negligence and is likely to recover damages for [his medical expenses, lost income and rehabilitation costs] (approximately $[66,375]), reduced by [25]% for contributory negligence.
—
P loses (no duty because the risk was obvious, Watt emergency, Bolton freak event, or VAR applies).
On the balance of probabilities, [P] is unlikely to succeed in an action against [D] in the tort of negligence, because [the risk was obvious and no duty was owed / D did not breach its duty / P voluntarily assumed the risk].
—

Example (2026 S1 Oliver): On the balance of probabilities, Oliver can bring an action against Skyline in the tort of negligence and is likely to recover damages for his medical expenses, lost income and rehabilitation costs (approximately $66,375 after a 25% reduction for contributory negligence).

Full mini-answer: 2026 S1 Oliver v Skyline

Open the whole answer, stitched together (≈1,250 words)

Whether Oliver can recover damages from Skyline Events ('Skyline') depends on whether each element of the tort of negligence (duty, breach and causation) can be established, and whether any defences apply.

Issue

Can Oliver bring an action against Skyline for the tort of negligence?

Law: Negligence generally

A tort is a civil wrong that allows the injured person to sue the wrongdoer for damages. A person commits the tort of negligence if they carelessly cause harm to another. Oliver must establish on the balance of probabilities that (1) Skyline owed him a duty of care; (2) Skyline breached that duty; and (3) the breach caused his loss, which is within the scope of Skyline's liability. Defences and remedies must then be considered under the common law and the Civil Liability Act 2003 (Qld) ('CLA').

1. Duty of care

Law. In Donoghue v Stevenson [1932] AC 562, a manufacturer owed a duty to the consumer of a ginger beer containing a decomposed snail, despite no contract. Lord Atkin's neighbour principle requires reasonable care to avoid acts or omissions that one can reasonably foresee would injure persons so closely and directly affected that they ought to be in contemplation. An occupier of premises owes a duty to all persons entering to ensure the premises are safe: Australian Safeway Stores Pty Ltd v Zaluzna (1987).

Application. Skyline organised the festival and controlled the site, including the temporary flooring, so it was the occupier. Oliver entered as a patron (it is assumed he held a ticket). Similar to the supermarket in Zaluzna, this falls within the established occupier–entrant category, and it was reasonably foreseeable that carelessness about the flooring could injure a patron. Skyline is a private organiser, not a public authority.

Conclusion. Skyline owed Oliver a duty of care.

2. Breach of duty

Law. Under s 9(1) CLA, a person breaches their duty by failing to take precautions if (a) the risk was foreseeable; (b) it was not insignificant; and (c) a reasonable person in their position would have taken those precautions. Under s 9(2), the court considers (a) the probability of harm; (b) its likely seriousness (Paris v Stepney Borough Council (1951): no goggles for a one-eyed worker, breach); (c) the burden of precautions; and (d) social utility (Watt v Hertfordshire County Council (1954): rushing to save a life justified the risk, no breach). In Bolton v Stone (1951), a cricket ball very rarely left the ground, so the low probability and high burden meant no breach.

Application. The risk was foreseeable because Skyline actually knew of it: two attendees reported it and a staff member inspected it at 2pm. It was not insignificant because the flooring shifted when walked on near the busy main stage. A reasonable organiser would have cordoned off the area, placed physical signs and repaired it; Skyline only posted on Facebook. The probability of harm was high, as crowds would cross the area into the night, looking at the stage, many after drinking. The likely harm was serious; as in Paris v Stepney, this demanded more than minimal precautions. The burden was low: barrier tape or a sign is quick and cheap. Skyline may argue a staff shortage prevented repairs, but staffing was within its control and a sign needed minimal resources. Unlike the emergency in Watt, nothing urgent justified leaving a known hazard. Unlike Bolton v Stone, the risk was reported, likely and cheap to prevent. A Facebook post is unlikely to reach patrons already on site at night, so it was not reasonable.

Conclusion. Skyline breached its duty under s 9 CLA.

3. Causation

Law. Under s 11(1) CLA, (a) the breach must be a necessary condition of the harm (factual causation), and (b) it must be appropriate for liability to extend to the harm (scope of liability). Factual causation uses the 'but for' test; in Yates v Jones [1990], an addiction to heroin suggested by a friend was caused by the friend, not the car accident. Where there are several causes, the material contribution test asks whether the breach increased the risk and the risk eventuated: Chappel v Hart (1998); in Cook v ACT Racing Club Inc [2001], an overheated sauna was only one cause of a jockey's heart attack, but causation was established. The harm must be a reasonably foreseeable type, not remote: Overseas Tankships v Morts [1961]. In Schneider v Smith & Anor (2016), illegal drug use against medical advice was a voluntary act breaking the chain, but medical and psychological treatment costs were 'ordinary and natural consequences'.

Application. (a) Back injury, medical expenses ($36,000) and lost income. But for Skyline's failure to repair or cordon off the flooring, Oliver would not have begun to fall. The medical evidence shows the injury was caused by the flooring combined with his reaction to SoundCo's noise, so the material contribution test applies: Skyline's breach increased the risk of injury and it eventuated, because Oliver was already falling when the noise occurred. Similar to Cook, the breach need not be the sole cause. Unlike Yates, the noise was not an independent later choice; it combined with the existing danger at that moment. A serious back injury is exactly the harm foreseeable from unstable flooring, and medical costs and lost income are its ordinary and natural consequences (Schneider). Unlike Overseas Tankships, it is not remote.

(b) Addiction and rehabilitation ($5,000). But for the injury, Oliver would not have been prescribed strong pain medication or become addicted. Unlike Yates (heroin suggested by a friend) and Schneider (illegal drugs against medical advice), Oliver took the medication 'in accordance with medical advice', so no voluntary, unreasonable act broke the chain. The rehabilitation cost is a reasonably foreseeable consequence and within scope.

Conclusion. Skyline's breach caused all of Oliver's losses, which are within its scope of liability.

4. Defences

Law. If a plaintiff contributed to their own injury, liability is apportioned and damages reduced: Ingram v Britten [1994] (tractor driven at speed, frame unsecured); Manley v Alexander (12 stubbies, slept on the road). Voluntary assumption of risk requires full knowledge of the precise risk, freely accepted: Agar v Hyde (2000); under ss 13–15 CLA, a plaintiff is presumed aware of an obvious risk.

Application. Oliver had consumed several drinks and was looking at the stage, not the ground. Similar to Manley, his intoxication likely affected his balance; like the plaintiff in Ingram, he failed to take reasonable care for his own safety. However, the floor shifted unexpectedly and patrons are expected to watch the stage, so Skyline remains primarily responsible. It is assumed he had not read the Facebook post. A reduction of around 25% is likely. Unlike the rugby players in Agar v Hyde, Oliver did not know the flooring was unstable, and it was not obvious, so voluntary assumption of risk fails.

Conclusion. Oliver's damages are likely to be reduced for contributory negligence, but Skyline has no complete defence.

5. Remedies

Law. Damages compensate, not punish. Ordinary damages restore the plaintiff, so far as money can, to his position had the tort not occurred, and are assessed once and for all.

Application. Oliver may claim medical expenses of $36,000; rehabilitation of $5,000; and lost income of $95,000 ÷ 12 × 6 = $47,500. These total $88,500. He may also claim for pain and suffering and any future losses. Applying a 25% reduction, Oliver would recover 75% of $88,500 = $66,375, plus 75% of any general damages.

Conclusion. Oliver is entitled to ordinary damages of approximately $66,375 plus reduced general damages.

Overall conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, Oliver can bring an action against Skyline in the tort of negligence and is likely to recover damages for his medical expenses, lost income and rehabilitation costs (approximately $66,375 after a 25% reduction for contributory negligence).

Glossary

TermPlain English
TortA civil wrong (not a crime) that lets the victim sue for money.
NegligenceThe tort of carelessly causing harm to someone.
Plaintiff (P)The injured person who sues.
Defendant (D)The person or business being sued.
DamagesMoney the court orders D to pay P.
On the balance of probabilitiesMore likely than not (over 50%) — how much P must prove.
CLAThe Civil Liability Act 2003 (Qld): the Queensland law with the breach (s 9) and causation (s 11) rules.
Common lawLaw made by judges in cases (e.g. Donoghue v Stevenson), as opposed to an Act of Parliament.
OnusThe job of proving something. "The onus is on P" = P has to prove it.
Duty of careA legal obligation to take reasonable care not to hurt someone.
Neighbour principleLord Atkin's test: you must take care not to harm people you can foresee would be directly affected by what you do.
Reasonably foreseeableA sensible person could have predicted it might happen.
Established categoryA relationship the courts have ALREADY decided always carries a duty (driver → road users, doctor → patient, occupier → visitors, employer → employee, manufacturer → consumer).
OccupierWhoever controls a place (a shop owner, festival organiser, venue, council for a park).
EntrantAnyone who comes onto the occupier's premises.
TrespasserSomeone who is somewhere without permission.
Public authorityA government body, e.g. a council or a "Recreation Authority".
Hidden risk / obvious riskA danger a reasonable person couldn't see (underwater rock) vs. one anyone could see (a cliff edge).
Novel duty / salient featuresA new kind of relationship with no established category; the court looks at its key features (control, knowledge, vulnerability, reliance) to decide if there's a duty.
Negligent misstatementCareless advice or information that someone relied on and lost money.
DisclaimerA statement like "no responsibility accepted" attached to advice.
Third partySomeone outside the main relationship (e.g. not the adviser's client).
Vicarious liabilityAn employer being responsible for what its employee did at work.
Independent contractorA separate business hired to do a job (e.g. SoundCo) — not an employee.
BreachFailing to take the care a reasonable person would have taken.
Reasonable personAn imaginary sensible person of normal intelligence in D's position.
PrecautionA step to stop the harm: a sign, barrier, repair, closure, safety gear.
Not insignificantA real risk, not a far-fetched one.
Probability / seriousness / burden / social utilityThe four s 9(2) factors: how likely the harm was; how bad it would be; how hard or costly it was to prevent; how useful D's activity was.
CausationWhether D's carelessness actually caused P's loss.
Head of damageOne separate loss (medical bill, wages, rehab) — each gets its own causation sub-heading.
Factual causation / "but for" testWould the harm have happened anyway without D's carelessness? If not, D caused it.
Necessary conditionSomething without which the harm would not have happened.
Material contributionUsed when there are several causes: D is liable if D's carelessness increased the risk and that harm happened.
Scope of liability / remoteWhether it is fair to make D pay for this kind of loss; "remote" = too far-fetched to foresee.
Break in the chain of causation (intervening act)A later, independent act (someone else's or P's own deliberate choice) that becomes the real cause, so D isn't responsible for what follows.
Ordinary and natural consequenceSomething that normally follows from an injury (medical bills, time off work).
Nervous shock / psychiatric injuryA recognised mental illness caused by the event (PTSD, depression, acute stress disorder).
Economic lossLosing money (as opposed to a physical injury).
DefenceA reason D pays less or nothing even though D was negligent.
Contributory negligenceP was partly to blame, so P's damages are cut by a percentage.
ApportionedBlame (and so the money) is split between D and P by percentage.
Voluntary assumption of riskP knew the exact danger and freely accepted it, so D pays nothing (very hard to prove).
Dangerous recreational activityA risky leisure activity; under ss 17–19 CLA D isn't liable for its obvious risks.
Ordinary (compensatory) damagesMoney to put P back where they would have been without the accident.
"Once and for all"P gets one payment only, so future losses must be claimed now.
Future loss of earning capacityMoney P won't be able to earn in future because of the injury.
Pain and suffering / general damagesMoney for the injury itself and its pain, with no receipt.
Exemplary damagesExtra money to punish D who deliberately and maliciously ignored P's rights (rare).
W2 Exact words: Australian Consumer Law

Use this page when…

  • The question says "under the Australian Consumer Law (ACL)" or "Competition and Consumer Act" (in 9 of 11 past papers).
  • A business said something untrue or gave a false impression — a label, ad, flyer, sign, TV commercial, or a salesperson's words ("free range", "most reliable", "will mix all day", "guaranteed to lose weight", "ONLY fresh seafood").
  • A product or service broke, didn't work, or wasn't what the buyer asked for, and the business says "warranty void" / "no refunds" / "not liable for any delay".
  • A vulnerable customer was pressured (elderly, poor eyesight, "sign today"), or a contract has one-sided terms (exit fee, forfeited deposit, business can cancel any time), or the business took money it couldn't deliver on.
THE ORDER — your booklet headings (skip any heading your facts don't raise — markers penalise irrelevant law)
  1. Issue (opener + one Issue question per cause of action)
  2. Preliminary: Is [P] a consumer, and is [D] in trade or commerce?
  3. Issue 1: Misleading or deceptive conduct (s 18)
  4. Issue 2: False or misleading representations (s 29)
  5. Issue 3: Consumer guarantees (ss 54, 55, 56 and 64)
  6. Issue 4: Unconscionable conduct (ss 20, 21, 22)
  7. Issue 5: Unfair contract terms (ss 23, 24, 25, 27)
  8. Issue 6: Bait advertising, wrongly accepting payment, unsolicited goods (ss 35, 36, 40)
  9. Remedies (always) and defences
  10. Overall conclusion
Each Issue = I (one question ending "?") · L (rule + case facts) · A (one sub-heading per element, both sides, "Similar to… / Unlike…") · C ("On the balance of probabilities, …").

Issue

What this means: Start with one opening sentence that lists every claim you will run, then put a separate one-sentence Issue question (ending in "?") at the top of each heading below. An "issue" = the legal question the court has to answer. A "cause of action" = a legal reason you can sue (e.g. "misleading conduct" is one; "breach of a consumer guarantee" is another).

The nature and extent of the available remedies is then considered.

Example (2026 S1 Emma v GreenPastures): "Whether Emma has causes of action against GreenPastures (GP) under the Australian Consumer Law (ACL), a Schedule to the Competition and Consumer Act 2010 (Cth), depends upon four issues: whether Emma is a "consumer"; misleading or deceptive conduct (s 18); false or misleading representations (s 29); and the consumer guarantees (ss 54–56). The nature and extent of the available remedies is then considered."

Preliminary: Is [P] a consumer, and is [D] in trade or commerce?

What this means: The consumer guarantees (promises the law automatically puts into every sale) only protect a "consumer" — and under s 3 that is much wider than "a shopper": anyone who paid $100,000 or less counts, even a business, unless they bought the goods to resell. "In trade or commerce" = the defendant was acting as a business (selling, advertising), not in a private/social setting. Note: ss 18 and 29 don't need a consumer — anyone, even a competitor, can sue.

WRITE — Law:

Under s 3 of the ACL, a person acquires goods or services as a "consumer" if the amount paid was not more than $100,000; or the goods or services were of a kind ordinarily acquired for personal, domestic or household use or consumption; or the goods were a vehicle or trailer acquired principally for transporting goods on public roads. Only one limb need be satisfied. The definition is wide enough to include businesses, to the extent they are not acquiring goods for resale: for example, a restaurant owner buying cutlery, furniture or food items for the menu is a consumer: Star Kingdom Investments v Camatic [2024] FCA 329. Separately, the ACL only applies to conduct "in trade or commerce", which under s 2(1) means trade or commerce within Australia or between Australia and places outside Australia, and includes any business or professional activity, whether or not carried on for profit: Re Ku-Ring-Gai Co-operative Building Society (1978).

WRITE — Application:
