Example (2026 S1 Emma): "Emma paid $10,000 upfront, well below the $100,000 threshold, so she is a consumer on the price limb alone even though the chicken is acquired for her business. Further, packaged chicken is plainly of a kind ordinarily acquired for household consumption. GP may argue that Emma acquired the chicken to re-supply it to diners. However, Emma does not resell the packaged chicken; she uses it as an ingredient in her meals, exactly like the restaurant owner buying food for the menu in Star Kingdom v Camatic. GP supplies packaged meat across Queensland for profit, which is trade or commerce under s 2(1)."

WRITE — Conclusion:

On the balance of probabilities, [P] is a consumer under s 3 of the ACL. In any event, ss 18 and 29 do not require [P] to be a consumer.

Issue 1: Misleading or deceptive conduct (s 18)

What this means: A business must not, while doing business, give people a false impression. You must walk through four requirements one by one (person → in trade or commerce → engaged in conduct → misleading), and test "misleading" from the point of view of the target audience including the gullible (the Taco Bell tests + Annand) — NOT your own view. "Intention is irrelevant" = it doesn't matter if they didn't mean to mislead. Students who skipped Taco Bell/Annand got half marks.

WRITE — Law:

Section 18(1) of the ACL provides that "a person must not, in trade or commerce, engage in conduct that is misleading or deceptive or is likely to mislead or deceive". Intention or fault is irrelevant. There are four requirements. (1) A "person" is interpreted broadly and includes individuals and corporations. (2) "Trade or commerce" includes any business or professional activity, whether or not for profit: s 2(1); Re Ku-Ring-Gai Co-operative Building Society (1978). (3) "Engaging in conduct" includes doing or refusing to do an act, such as making a statement, claim or promise: s 2(2). (4) To mislead is to lead into error; to deceive is to cause a person to believe something false: Weitmann v Katies (1977). The test is objective and it is unnecessary to prove anyone was actually misled.
In Taco Co of Australia v Taco Bell (1982), a Sydney restaurant already trading as "Taco Bell" sued the US Taco Bell chain when it opened in Sydney under the same name; the conduct was held misleading because customers would believe the businesses were connected. The court set two tests: first, identify the relevant section of the public at which the conduct is directed; second, consider the effect on all who come within it, "including the astute and the gullible, the intelligent and the not so intelligent". In Annand & Thompson v TPC (1979), the second test was applied by reference to a person "not particularly intelligent or well-informed, but perhaps of somewhat less than average intelligence and background knowledge", though not someone "quite unusually stupid". Only a small percentage of the audience need be misled: ACCC v Powerballwin.com (2010).

Pick the row(s) that match each statement/act — then add its Law sentence to your Law and its Application sentence to your Application

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
False statement of fact — a checkable claim that is untrue ("Free Range", "highest protein content", "ONLY fresh seafood")L: To mislead is to lead into error: Weitmann v Katies (1977). A: "[statement]" is a specific, factual claim that [the chicken was raised outdoors]. In fact [a substantial proportion was caged]. A member of the audience of somewhat less than average intelligence (Annand) would take it at face value and be led into error.Weitmann v Katies; Taco Bell; Annand
Silence — seller dodged a question, changed the subject, or said nothing about a known defectL: Silence can amount to conduct where failing to disclose information creates or maintains a misleading impression: Henjo Investments v Collins (1988). A: When [P asked whether there was any customer feedback], [D's staff member] [avoided the question and changed the subject], although [he knew of the reported faults]. Similar to Henjo, this silence maintained the misleading impression that [there were no known problems], so it is conduct capable of breaching s 18.s 2(2); Henjo v Collins
Half-truth / "it's literally true" — partly true, but the overall impression is falseL: A statement that is literally true may still mislead through its overall impression: Henderson v Pioneer Homes (1980), where the information was technically correct but the advertisement's overall impression was misleading. A: [D] may argue that "[statement]" is true because [some chickens were free range]. However, as in Henderson v Pioneer Homes, the overall impression — that [this product is free range] — is false for [a substantial part of the product].Henderson v Pioneer Homes
Puffery — vague boasting ("best", "revolutionary", "really decent machine", "highest welfare standards")L: Mere sales puffery is not necessarily misleading. However, in Given v Pryor (1980), although a phrase alone might have been puffery, the advertisement as a whole conveyed the misleading impression that homes could be built on the land. In Optus Mobile v Telstra [2018] FCA 745, Telstra argued that "unlimited" in its advertising conveyed "nothing definitive"; the argument failed and the ads contravened ss 18 and 29. A: [D] may argue that "[best / highest standards]" is mere puffery that no member of the audience would take literally. That has some force for that phrase alone. However, similar to Given v Pryor, read as a whole with [the images / the specific claim "Certified"], the [packaging] conveys the factual impression that […]. Like Telstra's "unlimited" in Optus v Telstra, "[reliable / Certified]" carries a definite meaning to the audience.Given v Pryor; Optus v Telstra
Prediction / promise about the future ("it will mix all day", "guaranteed to lose weight", "installed within a week")L: Under s 4, a representation about a future matter is taken to be misleading if the person did not have reasonable grounds for making it: ACCC v Powerballwin.com (2010). A: "[It will…]" is a representation about a future matter. [D] had no reasonable grounds for it because [the staff member had no training and relied on his mum's mixer / testers had reported the faults / the bars are 95% sugar]. It is therefore taken to be misleading under s 4.s 4; ACCC v Powerballwin.com
Opinion — "Mum thinks it's great", "Polex is the best brand", "I've tasted it, it's disgusting"A: "[opinion]" is a subjective opinion rather than a specific, checkable claim, and even a viewer of less than average intelligence (Annand) would likely see it as sales talk, so on its own it is likely puffery. However, where the opinion is given alongside [specific promises such as "it will do the job you want"], the conduct as a whole conveys a factual impression, as in Given v Pryor. [If the opinion predicts the future:] To the extent it predicts future performance, [D] needed reasonable grounds (s 4).Given v Pryor; Annand; s 4 (opinion isn't a separate course category — treat it like puffery)
Pictures / packaging create the impression (chickens on grass; the delicate watch shown throughout the ad)A: The [images of chickens roaming on green grass] are part of the conduct. As in Given v Pryor, the [package] must be read as a whole, and together with "[words]" it conveys that […].Given v Pryor; Henderson v Pioneer Homes
Fine print / disclaimer / manual says something differentL: Fine print or disclaimers will not necessarily correct a misleading impression: ACCC v Telstra (2007). A: The limitation in [the manual], which [P] had not seen, does not undo the impression created by [the staff member's statements at the point of sale].ACCC v Telstra (2007)
Staff member said it (salesperson, rep, manager)A: [Staff member] made the statements while [working on the shop floor in the store's uniform / as Senior Sales Manager], so the statements are treated as [D]'s conduct. (I assume [he] was acting in the course of [his] employment.)"person" includes a corporation (agency on T8)
D says "we didn't mean it" / "no-one would believe it" / "they can't tell the difference"A: It is irrelevant that [D] [did not intend to mislead / thought no-one would believe it], since intention is irrelevant under s 18, and the test is the effect on the target audience including the gullible (Annand), not [D]'s own view. [If deliberate:] In fact, [management approved it believing consumers could not tell], which aggravates the conduct.s 18 (intention irrelevant); Annand
"Only a few people would be fooled"A: Even if only some [customers] were misled, a small percentage of the audience is sufficient: ACCC v Powerballwin.com (2010).Powerballwin.com
Similar names/logos — customers "might wonder" if connectedL: Mere confusion is not enough: McWilliam's Wines v McDonald's (1980), where some consumers might wonder about a connection but that was insufficient. A: Unlike McWilliam's, and similar to Taco Bell, customers would [positively believe the businesses were connected]. / OR: Similar to McWilliam's, customers might merely wonder, which is insufficient.McWilliam's v McDonald's; Taco Bell
Ranking / "Top Offer" / comparison is riggedA: Similar to Trivago N.V. v ACCC [2020], where the "Top Offer" was whichever hotel paid Trivago most rather than the cheapest, [D]'s [ranking] leads users to think [it is the best deal] when it is not.Trivago v ACCC
"Specially made for X" but identical to the cheaper productA: Similar to ACCC v Reckitt Benckiser, where Nurofen "specific pain" products were identical to standard Nurofen but sold at up to double the price, [D] sold [X] at a premium on the basis of a quality it did not have.ACCC v Reckitt Benckiser

WRITE — Application (one sub-heading per element):

Person. [D] is a [company] and therefore a legal person.
In trade or commerce. [D] was [selling/advertising X] to the public for profit, which is a business activity in trade or commerce within Australia under s 2(1).
Engaged in conduct. By [labelling / advertising / telling P that…], [D] made a statement or claim and so engaged in conduct (s 2(2)). [Further, when P asked…, D said nothing; similar to Henjo, this silence maintained the impression that…]
Misleading — Taco Bell test 1. The relevant section of the public is [e.g. Queensland purchasers of packaged chicken who choose free range for animal-welfare reasons].
Taco Bell test 2 and Annand. Considering all within that group, including a person of somewhat less than average intelligence and background knowledge (Annand), they would understand "[words]" to mean […]. In fact [the truth]. They would therefore be led into error (Weitmann v Katies).
For D: [D] may argue that "[phrase]" is mere puffery / literally true. Against: However, similar to Given v Pryor, read as a whole with [the images / specific promises], the conduct conveys the impression that […].
Intention. It is irrelevant that [D] [believed…], as intention is irrelevant under s 18; and only a small percentage need be misled (Powerballwin.com).

Example (2026 S1 Emma): "GP is a business supplying packaged meat across Queensland for profit, so it is a person in trade or commerce (s 2(1)). GP engaged in conduct by labelling the chicken 'Farm Fresh – Free Range Certified' with images of chickens on green grass, and through its representative's assurance to Emma (s 2(2)). By saying nothing about the caged birds, GP maintained a misleading impression, similar to Henjo. Test 1: the audience is Queensland purchasers of packaged chicken who choose free range for animal-welfare reasons, including restaurant owners. Test 2/Annand: a person of somewhat less than average intelligence would understand 'Free Range Certified' with those images to mean every chicken had outdoor access. In fact a substantial proportion were barn raised or caged. GP may argue some chickens were free range; but as in Henderson v Pioneer Homes the overall impression is false. GP may argue 'highest animal welfare standards' is puffery; but similar to Given v Pryor, read as a whole with 'Certified' and the images, the package conveys a clear factual impression. Intention is irrelevant, and in fact management approved the labelling believing consumers 'would not be able to tell the difference'."

WRITE — Conclusion:

On the balance of probabilities, [D] has engaged in misleading or deceptive conduct in contravention of s 18 of the ACL.

Issue 2: False or misleading representations (s 29)

What this means: s 29 is a list of specific kinds of false claims (about quality, newness, reviews, approval, price, origin…). Name the exact paragraph (e.g. s 29(1)(a)). It overlaps with s 18 — you can say the s 18 analysis applies equally. It matters because the ACCC can get pecuniary penalties (big fines) for s 29 but not for s 18 alone. A "representation" = a statement or impression given about something.

WRITE — Law:

Section 29 provides that a person must not, in trade or commerce, in connection with the supply or possible supply of goods or services, or the promotion of goods or services, make certain false or misleading representations. Unlike contract law, there is no need to classify the statement as fraudulent, negligent or innocent. There is no meaningful difference between "false or misleading" in s 29 and "misleading or deceptive" in s 18: ACCC v Coles Supermarkets Australia Pty Ltd [2014] FCA 634 at [40]; Optus Mobile v Telstra [2018] FCA 745. In ACCC v C.I. & Co Pty Ltd [2010] FCA 1511 (the Egg Case), cage eggs were sold labelled "free range" and later "Fresh Range – Omega 3"; consumers had no practical way of telling once the eggs were in the carton. The court held this misleading and a false representation of the eggs' quality and characteristics, describing it as a "cruel deception" involving a "high level of dishonesty".

Pick EVERY paragraph that matches — one row per paragraph taught

If… (the claim was about…)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
Quality, standard, grade, composition, style, model, or history/previous use of GOODS ("free range", "highest protein", "most reliable", "excellent colour")L: Section 29(1)(a) prohibits false or misleading representations that goods are of a particular standard, quality, value, grade, composition, style or model, or have had a particular history or previous use: ACCC v Cadbury (2004). A: "[words]" represents that the [goods] are of a particular [standard / quality / composition / history]. This is false because […], so s 29(1)(a) is contravened.s 29(1)(a); ACCC v Cadbury; C.I. & Co
Quality or standard of a SERVICE (dining, repairs, internet plan)L: Section 29(1)(b) prohibits false or misleading representations that services are of a particular standard, quality, value or grade. A: "[words]" represents that [D]'s [dining / service] is of a particular standard or quality, which is false because […].s 29(1)(b)
Says goods are NEW but they're used / refurbishedL: Section 29(1)(c) prohibits false or misleading representations that goods are new. A: [D] represented that the [goods] were new, but they had [been used / returned], contravening s 29(1)(c).s 29(1)(c)
Fake reviews / made-up testimonialsL: Section 29(1)(e) prohibits a false or misleading representation that purports to be a testimonial by any person. A: The [reviews] purported to be testimonials by [customers], but were [written by D], contravening s 29(1)(e).s 29(1)(e)
Approval / certification / sponsorship, OR performance, uses, benefits ("Certified", "designed for high-demand environments", "will mix all day", "guaranteed to lose weight")L: Section 29(1)(g) prohibits false or misleading representations that goods or services have sponsorship, approval, performance characteristics, accessories, uses or benefits: Hartnell v Sharp (1975). A: "[Certified / will mix all day]" represents that the [goods] have [an approval / performance characteristics / uses / benefits] they do not have, because […], contravening s 29(1)(g).s 29(1)(g); Hartnell v Sharp
Wrong price / hidden fees / fake "was $X"L: Section 29(1)(i) prohibits false or misleading representations with respect to the price of goods or services. A: [D] represented the price as [$X], but [P] was charged [$Y / hidden fees], contravening s 29(1)(i).s 29(1)(i)
Place of origin ("Made in Australia", "manufactured in London")L: Section 29(1)(k) prohibits false or misleading representations concerning the place of origin of goods. A: "[Made in…]" is a representation about place of origin. [It is false because… / The question does not say whether this is untrue; assuming it is true, there is no breach of s 29(1)(k).]s 29(1)(k)

WRITE — Application:

The representations were made in connection with the [supply / promotion] of [goods], as they appeared [on the packaging / in the advertisement / in the sales conversation]. s 29(1)(a). "[words]" represents that the goods are of a particular [standard / quality / composition / history]; this is false because […]. s 29(1)(g). "[words]" represents that the goods have [an approval / performance characteristics] they do not have. Case comparison. Similar to the egg producers in C.I. & Co, [D] [labelled…] and [P] had no practical way of detecting the truth once […]. As the analysis under s 18 applies equally (ACCC v Coles), these representations are false or misleading.

Example (2026 S1 Emma): "The labels and the representative's assurance were made in connection with the supply of chicken to Emma. s 29(1)(a): 'Free Range' and 'raised in natural open environments' represent that the chicken is of a particular standard, quality and composition, and has a particular history (how it was raised); for a substantial proportion this was false. s 29(1)(g): 'Certified' represents an approval the product could not truly have. The facts are strikingly similar to C.I. & Co: as there, GP sold non-free-range animal products as free range, and once packaged, purchasers like Emma had no practical way of detecting the difference — exactly what GP's management relied on. As under s 18 (ACCC v Coles), these representations are false or misleading."

WRITE — Conclusion:

On the balance of probabilities, [D] has contravened s 29(1)[(a) and (g)] of the ACL.

Issue 3: Consumer guarantees (ss 54, 55, 56 and 64)

What this means: The ACL automatically puts guarantees (legal promises) into every sale to a consumer — e.g. the goods will work properly (s 54), will do the job you told the seller you needed (s 55), and will match how they were described (s 56). The business cannot cancel them by any clause, sign or manual (s 64 — the clause is "void" = has no legal effect). Only for consumers who bought something — never for a competitor.

WRITE — Law:

The ACL contains statutory guarantees that protect consumers who acquire goods in trade or commerce, and any term that seeks to exclude, restrict or modify them is void (s 64). Under s 54, goods supplied to a consumer in trade or commerce (other than by auction) must be of acceptable quality: fit for all the purposes for which goods of that kind are commonly supplied, acceptable in appearance and finish, free from defects, safe and durable, as a reasonable consumer fully acquainted with the state and condition of the goods (including hidden defects) would regard as acceptable: Licciardo v Plush Think Sofas Pty Ltd [2015] VCAT 2044; Mitchell v Oz Design Furniture Pty Ltd [2016] VCAT 108 (both defective-furniture claims applying s 54). Under s 55, goods must be reasonably fit for any purpose the consumer makes known, expressly or by implication, to the supplier or a person who conducted the prior negotiations (s 55(2)), and for any purpose the supplier represents they are fit for; the guarantee does not apply if the consumer did not rely, or it was unreasonable to rely, on the supplier's skill or judgment. Under s 56, goods supplied by description must correspond with that description.

Pick EVERY guarantee that matches your facts

s 54 — it broke, overheated, jammed, stopped working, was unsafe, didn't lastWithin [weeks], the [goods] [overheated and stopped working]. A reasonable consumer, fully acquainted with this [defect], would not regard a [$X item] that fails so quickly as free from defects or durable. [D] may argue the goods are fit for the purposes for which they are commonly supplied ([domestic use]) and failed only because of [P]'s unusual use. However, […]. The s 54 guarantee is [likely / arguably] breached.s 54; Licciardo v Plush Think Sofas; Mitchell v Oz Design
s 54 weak — product works, the problem is something else (chicken is safe and edible but not free range)This is the weakest guarantee. A delay of [up to eight months] for [a monthly subscription service] is unlikely to be reasonable. s 64 — "warranty void", "no refunds", "not liable for any delay", limitation only in the manual[D]'s reliance on [the manual's statement that commercial use voids the warranty / clause X] fails. Example (2026 S1 Emma): "s 56: The chicken was supplied by the description 'Free Range Certified'; a substantial proportion did not correspond with it. s 55: Before contracting, Emma expressly told GP's representative it was essential the chicken be genuinely free range — a particular purpose made known to the person who conducted the negotiations (s 55(2)(a)). The representative assured her it met those requirements and she relied on that by entering the agreement. Her reliance was reasonable: she had no way to test how processed chicken was raised. Chicken that is not free range is not fit for her purpose. s 54: GP will argue the chicken was safe and edible; on balance s 54 adds little. s 64: any term limiting GP's liability for these guarantees is void."

WRITE — Conclusion:

On the balance of probabilities, [D] has breached the guarantees in ss [55 and 56], [though a claim under s 54 is less likely to succeed], and cannot rely on [the limitation] because of s 64.

Issue 4: Unconscionable conduct (ss 20, 21, 22)

What this means: "Unconscionable" = so unfair it's against good conscience — a stronger business unfairly taking advantage of its bargaining power or of a customer's weakness (old age, poor eyesight, poor English, grief, pressure). Use s 21 (business dealings); s 20 is the backup that adopts the old case law (Amadio). s 22 is a checklist of factors: procedural = how the deal was made (pressure, couldn't read it); substantive = how harsh the terms are.

WRITE — Law:

Unconscionable conduct is conduct that unfairly takes advantage of a superior bargaining position or of another person's weakness or disadvantage. Section 20 prohibits conduct in trade or commerce that is unconscionable within the meaning of the unwritten law, as in Commercial Bank of Australia v Amadio, which requires that (1) the weaker party was under a special disability or disadvantage, (2) the stronger party knew or should have known of it, and (3) the stronger party took unfair advantage of it; Kitto J in Blomley v Ryan (1956) described special disadvantage as arising from "illness, ignorance, impaired faculties". Section 20 does not apply where conduct falls within s 21. Section 21 provides that a person must not, in trade or commerce, in connection with the supply or possible supply of goods or services to a person (other than a listed public company), engage in conduct that is, in all the circumstances, unconscionable; the court may consider the terms of the contract and the manner in which it is carried out (s 21(4)(c)). Section 22 lists matters the court may consider, and no single factor is determinative: procedural matters (s 22(1)(a), (c), (d)) and substantive matters (s 22(1)(b), (j), (k)). In ACCC v Coles [2014] FCA, Coles breached s 21 by misusing its bargaining power against its suppliers in a way that was "serious, deliberate and repeated".

Pick EVERY factor that matches

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
P is elderly, ill, grieving, isolated, relies on family for money mattersThere was a significant inequality of bargaining power (s 22(1)(a)): [P] is [84, lives alone and relies on his grandson to manage his finances], while [D] is a [company with trained sales staff]. These are the "impaired faculties" described in Blomley v Ryan, placing [P] under a special disadvantage, which [D's salesperson] knew of because [P told him…] (Amadio).s 22(1)(a); Amadio; Blomley v Ryan
P couldn't understand the documents (poor eyesight, limited English, 35 pages of legalese)[P] could not understand the [35-page] contract because of [poor eyesight / limited English], which [P] told [D] (s 22(1)(c)).s 22(1)(c)
Pressure / unfair tactics ("sign today", "once-in-a-lifetime", wouldn't wait for family/adviser)[D] used pressure and unfair tactics: "[once-in-a-lifetime deal, sign immediately]", despite knowing [P's grandson usually helps with financial decisions] (s 22(1)(d)).s 22(1)(d)
Harsh one-sided terms (P pays exit fee; D can walk away free; D excludes its own liability)The terms are substantively one-sided (s 22(1)(b), (j)): [P must pay a $2,000 exit fee, while D may terminate at any time without penalty and excludes its own liability for non-supply]. These conditions are not reasonably necessary to protect [D]'s legitimate interests.s 22(1)(b), (j)

P is an ordinary adult who read the terms — no special disadvantage (claim likely FAILS)Against [P], there is little procedural unconscionability: [P] is not elderly, ill or otherwise at a special disadvantage, [read the terms before paying] and could have [hired elsewhere]. Unlike ACCC v Coles, there is no evidence the conduct was repeated or deliberate. A court is unlikely to find the conduct unconscionable; [P]'s stronger claim is under the unfair terms provisions.s 21; ACCC v Coles (Ben v BEW, Tim v BTH)

WRITE — Application:

Elements 1–4. [D] engaged in conduct in trade or commerce by [selling X] to [P], an individual and not a listed public company.
Procedural (s 22(1)(a), (c), (d)). [Inequality / couldn't read / pressure facts].
Substantive (s 22(1)(b), (j), (k)). [One-sided terms].
D's argument. [D] may argue that [P] voluntarily signed a standard contract offered to all. However, the question is whether the conduct was unconscionable "in all the circumstances", and [the circumstances of the sale] are strongly against [D]. Unlike ACCC v Coles, there is no evidence of repetition, but s 21 does not require it.

Example (2023 S1 John v Spectra — Emma's facts don't raise this): "John is 84, lives alone, has poor eyesight so cannot read the fine print, and relies on his grandson to manage his finances — the 'impaired faculties' in Blomley v Ryan and s 22(1)(a). The salesman urged him to sign immediately (s 22(1)(d)). The terms are one-sided: John pays a $2,000 exit fee while Spectra may terminate at any time without penalty (s 22(1)(b), (j)). In carrying out the contract (s 21(4)(c)), Spectra insists John keep paying $120 a month for a service it cannot supply."

WRITE — Conclusion:

On the balance of probabilities, [D]'s conduct was, in all the circumstances, unconscionable in contravention of s 21 of the ACL, and [P] can seek to have the contract set aside.

Issue 5: Unfair contract terms (ss 23, 24, 25, 27)

What this means: A one-sided clause in a standard form contract (same pre-printed terms for everyone, "take it or leave it") is void (cancelled — it has no effect) if it is unfair. Three steps: (1) consumer contract (an individual buying for personal/home use) or small business contract; (2) standard form; (3) the term is unfair under s 24 (big imbalance + not needed to protect the business + causes harm). s 25 is a list of examples. Do ONE ILAC per clause.

WRITE — Law:

Under s 23 of the ACL, a term of a contract is unfair and therefore void if (1) the contract is a consumer contract or a small business contract, (2) it is a standard form contract, and (3) the term is unfair. A consumer contract is a contract for the supply of goods, services or an interest in land to an individual whose acquisition is wholly or predominantly for personal, domestic or household use or consumption: s 23(3). A standard form contract is one that is not individually negotiated, having regard to the matters in s 27(2), such as whether one party prepared it in advance and the other had to "take it or leave it". Under s 24(1), a term is unfair if it (a) would cause a significant imbalance in the parties' rights and obligations, (b) is not reasonably necessary to protect the legitimate interests of the party advantaged by it, and (c) would cause detriment, financial or otherwise, to a party if relied on. The court must consider the extent to which the term is transparent — in reasonably plain language, legible, presented clearly and readily available — and the contract as a whole: s 24(2)–(3). Section 25 gives examples of unfair terms, including terms permitting one party (but not the other) to terminate the contract (Director v AAPT (2006)), to penalise the other for breach, or to vary the terms (Director v Trainstation (2006)).

Pick the ONE row that matches each clause

If… (the clause says…)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
One-sided termination — D can cancel "for any reason without penalty"; P can'tClause [Y] lets [D] terminate "for any reason without penalty", while [P] cannot terminate without [paying $2,000]. This is the s 25 example of a term allowing one party but not the other to terminate, as in Director v AAPT. The imbalance is significant, and [D]'s unrestricted right to walk away protects no legitimate interest.s 25; Director v AAPT
Exit fee — P must pay $X to leaveClause [X] requires [P] to pay a [$2,000] exit fee for terminating "for any reason". [D] may argue the fee protects its legitimate interest in recovering [installation costs]. However, a [$2,000] fee payable even where [D itself cannot supply the service] is not reasonably necessary to protect that interest. The detriment to [P] is [$2,000 or four years of payments].ss 24(1), 25 (penalise for breach / terminate)
Forfeit the whole deposit for any lateness "for whatever reason"Clause [11] requires a complete forfeiture of the [$1,000] deposit for any late return "for whatever reason", even [a two-hour delay caused by flooding], while clause [10] means [D] bears no consequence when it is late. This is a s 25 term penalising one party for breach, and the imbalance is significant. [D] has a legitimate interest in prompt return, but a late fee proportionate to its actual loss would protect it; forfeiting the whole deposit regardless of reason or loss operates as a penalty, not protection. The detriment is [$1,000].ss 24(1), 25 (Ben v BEW; Tim v BTH)
Unilateral variation — D can change the price/terms whenever it likesClause [X] allows [D], but not [P], to vary [the price / terms] at any time. This is a s 25 example of an unfair term, as in Director v Trainstation, and causes a significant imbalance because [P] is bound to terms [D] can rewrite. [Also relevant to unconscionability: s 22(1)(k).]s 25; Director v Trainstation
"Not liable for any delay/failure" — D limits its own performanceClause [10] lets [D] avoid or limit its own performance and limits [P]'s right to sue — both s 25 examples. Excluding liability for delays "however they may arise", including [D's own understaffing], is not reasonably necessary to protect any legitimate interest. It is also void under s 64 to the extent it excludes the guarantees (e.g. s 60).s 25; s 64
Limits P's right to sue / D alone decides if there's been a breach or what the contract meansClause [X] [limits P's right to sue D / allows D alone to decide whether P has breached], which is an example of an unfair term in s 25. It creates a significant imbalance because […].s 25
D alone can renew / not renew; D limits its liability for its agentsClause [X] permits [D], but not [P], to [renew or not renew the contract / limit its vicarious liability for its agents], an s 25 example of an unfair term.s 25
D's defence: "it was plain and they read it"[D]'s strongest argument is that the term is transparent: it is in plain language, readily available [online], and [P read the terms before paying] (s 24(2)(a), (3)). However, transparency is only one matter; a transparent term can still be unfair. Looking at the contract as a whole (s 24(2)(b)), clause [X] sits alongside clause [Y], which [excuses D from all liability for its own delay].s 24(2)–(3)
Clause hidden on page 30 / legalese / P couldn't read itThe clause was buried [at clause 10(a)(ii) of 35 pages] and [P could not read it], so it was not transparent (s 24(3)), making it more likely to be unfair.s 24(3)
P is a business, not an individual buying for home[P] is not an individual acquiring for personal or household use, so the contract is not a consumer contract under s 23(3), which is narrower than "consumer" in s 3. [P] must instead argue that it is a small business contract.s 23(3) (note: small business thresholds not in course notes)

WRITE — Application:

Consumer contract. [P] is an individual acquiring […] for [his home], which is wholly for personal or household use (s 23(3)).
Standard form. The contract was prepared by [D] in advance [as online terms] and presented to [P] without any opportunity to negotiate; the same terms apply to every customer (s 27).
Significant imbalance. [row sentence].
Legitimate interests. [D] may argue the clause protects […]; however [a smaller fee would fully protect it].
Detriment. If relied on, [P] would lose [$X].
Transparency / contract as a whole. [row sentence].

Example (2024 S1 Ben v BEW — Emma's facts don't raise this): "The hire is for an excavator to build a fence at Ben's home, so it is a consumer contract (s 23(3)). Ben accepted BEW's online terms with no negotiation (s 27). Clause 11 forfeits the whole $1,000 deposit for any lateness 'for whatever reason', while clause 10 excuses BEW's own five-hour delay — a significant imbalance and a s 25 penalty term. A proportionate late fee would protect BEW's interest in prompt return. Ben loses $1,000. Although the term was plain and Ben read it, the contract as a whole puts all the risk of delay on Ben."

WRITE — Conclusion:

On the balance of probabilities, clause [X] is an unfair term in a standard form [consumer] contract and is void under s 23 of the ACL; the rest of the contract may continue if it can operate without it.

Issue 6: Bait advertising, wrongly accepting payment, unsolicited goods (ss 35, 36, 40)

What this means: Three short specific bans. Bait advertising (s 35) = advertising a cheap deal to lure people in, knowing stock will run out almost straight away. Wrongly accepting payment (s 36) = taking money when you don't intend to, or know you can't, supply on time. Inertia selling / unsolicited goods (s 40) = sending things nobody ordered and then demanding payment. Only use the one(s) your facts raise.

WRITE — Law (copy only the sentence(s) for your provision):

Under s 35 of the ACL, a person must not engage in bait advertising — advertising goods or services at a price likely to attract buyers when it knows or should know that it is likely to run out of stock very quickly, as in Repco's "$1 Million Sizzling Sound Sellout". Under s 36, a person must not accept payment for goods or services where it does not intend to supply them, or knows or should know that it will be unable to supply them within the specified time or a reasonable time: Dawson v World Travel (1981); in ACCC v Ozdirect (2009), the ACCC sued a retailer that took payment for electronics it knew or ought reasonably to have known it could not supply within a reasonable time. Under s 40, a person must not assert a right to payment for unsolicited goods unless it reasonably believes it has that right; the recipient need not pay, is not liable for loss or damage to the goods unless wilful and unlawful, and after a period the goods become theirs free of charge.

Pick the ONE row that matches

Like Repco's "$1 Million Sizzling Sound Sellout", [D] knew or should have known the stock was likely to run out very quickly, so it contravened s 35.s 35; Repco

WRITE — Conclusion:

On the balance of probabilities, [D] has contravened s [36] of the ACL.

Remedies (always) and defences

What this means: ALWAYS finish with remedies, with section numbers. Damages = money for your loss — but you must show the loss was caused "because of" the breach. Injunction = court order to stop doing something (e.g. take the label down). Rescission = cancelling the contract and putting both sides back where they started. Pecuniary penalty = a fine paid to the government, sought by the ACCC (the national consumer watchdog). "Nature and extent" = say what remedy, how much, and why it's big or small.

WRITE — Law:

The ACL provides a broader range of remedies than contract or misrepresentation law. A person who suffers loss "because of" a contravention may recover damages (s 236); the court may grant an injunction (ss 232–235), make compensation orders (ss 237–241), and declare a contract void, vary it, refuse to enforce it, or order a refund or repair. The ACCC may seek pecuniary penalties (ss 224–231), which are available for s 29 and s 21 but not s 18 alone, and an adverse publicity order (s 247). In setting a penalty, the court has regard to the nature and extent of the contravention, the loss caused, the circumstances and any previous similar conduct; in ACCC v C.I. & Co, the deliberate, hard-to-detect mislabelling attracted a penalty designed to punish and send a clear message to the industry.

Full mini-answer — 2026 S1 Q2 Emma v GreenPastures (the whole script, blanks filled)

Whether Emma has causes of action against GreenPastures (GP) under the Australian Consumer Law (ACL), a Schedule to the Competition and Consumer Act 2010 (Cth), depends upon four issues: whether Emma is a "consumer"; misleading or deceptive conduct (s 18); false or misleading representations (s 29); and the consumer guarantees (ss 54–56). The nature and extent of the available remedies is then considered.

Preliminary: Is Emma a consumer, and is GP in trade or commerce?

Issue: Is Emma, as a restaurant owner, a "consumer" entitled to rely on the consumer guarantees in the ACL?

Law: Under s 3, a person acquires goods as a "consumer" if the amount paid was not more than $100,000, or the goods were of a kind ordinarily acquired for personal, domestic or household use or consumption. Only one limb need be satisfied. The definition includes businesses not acquiring goods for resale; a restaurant owner buying food for the menu is a consumer: Star Kingdom Investments v Camatic [2024] FCA 329.

Application: Emma paid $10,000, well below $100,000, so she is a consumer on the price limb alone. Packaged chicken is also ordinarily acquired for household consumption. GP may argue she re-supplies it to diners; however, she uses it as an ingredient, exactly like the restaurant owner in Star Kingdom.

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, Emma is a consumer under s 3. In any event, ss 18 and 29 do not require it.

Issue 1: Misleading or deceptive conduct (s 18)

Issue: Can Emma bring an action against GP for misleading or deceptive conduct under s 18 of the ACL?

Law: Section 18(1) provides that "a person must not, in trade or commerce, engage in conduct that is misleading or deceptive or is likely to mislead or deceive". Intention is irrelevant. "Person" includes corporations. "Trade or commerce" includes any business activity: s 2(1); Re Ku-Ring-Gai (1978). Conduct includes making a statement, and silence that maintains a misleading impression: s 2(2); Henjo Investments v Collins (1988). To mislead is to lead into error: Weitmann v Katies (1977). In Taco Bell (1982), the US chain's use of an existing Sydney restaurant's name was misleading; the court asked (1) who is the relevant section of the public and (2) the effect on all within it, "including the astute and the gullible". Annand & Thompson v TPC (1979) uses a person of "somewhat less than average intelligence". In Given v Pryor (1980) an ad read as a whole was misleading though one phrase alone might be puffery; a partly true statement may mislead by its overall impression: Henderson v Pioneer Homes (1980).

Application:

Person and trade or commerce. GP is a business supplying meat across Queensland for profit (s 2(1)).

Conduct. GP labelled the chicken "Farm Fresh – Free Range Certified" with images of chickens on grass, and its representative assured Emma it was genuinely free range. Saying nothing about the caged birds maintained the impression, similar to Henjo.

Taco Bell test 1. The audience is Queensland purchasers of packaged chicken who choose free range for animal-welfare reasons, including restaurant owners like Emma.

Test 2 and Annand. A person of somewhat less than average intelligence would understand "Free Range Certified" with those images to mean every chicken had outdoor access. In fact a substantial proportion were barn raised or caged, so purchasers were led into error. GP may argue some chickens were free range; however, as in Henderson, the overall impression is false.

Puffery. GP may argue "highest animal welfare standards" is puffery. That has some force alone, but similar to Given v Pryor, read with "Certified" and the images, the package conveys a factual impression. "Certified" is not puffery.

Intention. Intention is irrelevant; in fact management approved the labelling believing consumers "would not be able to tell the difference".

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, GP has contravened s 18.

Issue 2: False or misleading representations (s 29)

Issue: Has GP made false or misleading representations to Emma in contravention of s 29 of the ACL?

Law: Section 29 prohibits, in trade or commerce and in connection with the supply or promotion of goods, false or misleading representations that goods are of a particular standard, quality or composition, or have a particular history (s 29(1)(a); ACCC v Cadbury (2004)), or have an approval or performance characteristics (s 29(1)(g); Hartnell v Sharp (1975)). There is no meaningful difference from s 18: ACCC v Coles [2014] FCA 634 at [40]. In ACCC v C.I. & Co [2010] FCA 1511, cage eggs labelled "free range" were a false representation of quality; consumers could not detect it, and North J called it a "cruel deception".

Application: "Free Range" and "raised in natural open environments" represent a standard, composition and history (how the chicken was raised) — s 29(1)(a). "Certified" represents an approval it could not have — s 29(1)(g). The facts are strikingly similar to C.I. & Co: non-free-range product sold as free range, undetectable once packaged, bought because of animal-welfare concerns.

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, GP has contravened s 29(1)(a) and (g).

Issue 3: Consumer guarantees (ss 54, 55, 56 and 64)

Issue: Has GP breached the consumer guarantees in ss 54–56 of the ACL in supplying chicken to Emma?

Law: Goods supplied by description must correspond with it (s 56). Goods must be fit for any purpose made known to the supplier or a person who conducted the negotiations (s 55(2)), unless reliance was absent or unreasonable. Goods must be of acceptable quality (s 54; Licciardo v Plush Think Sofas [2015] VCAT 2044). Terms excluding the guarantees are void (s 64).

Application: s 56: the chicken did not correspond with "Free Range Certified". s 55: Emma told GP's representative it was essential the chicken be genuinely free range (s 55(2)(a)); he assured her it was, and she relied on that by signing. Reliance was reasonable — she could not test how processed chicken was raised. s 54: GP will argue the chicken was safe and edible; on balance s 54 adds little. s 64: any term limiting these guarantees is void.

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, GP has breached ss 55 and 56; s 54 is less likely.

Remedies

Emma may recover damages under s 236 for her decline in business and reputational harm, provided she proves they were caused by GP's mislabelling; this is likely, as the loss followed directly from the media investigation. She may seek rescission of the two-year agreement and a refund of the portion of the $10,000 for undelivered chicken, and an injunction (ss 232–235). The ACCC may seek pecuniary penalties for the s 29 contraventions (ss 224–231) and an adverse publicity order (s 247). As the conduct was deliberate, widespread and undetectable, as in C.I. & Co, a substantial penalty is likely. GP cannot rely on s 207 or s 208, as management knowingly approved the labelling.

Overall conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, Emma is likely to succeed against GP under ss 18, 29(1)(a) and (g), 55 and 56, and is entitled to damages, rescission with a refund, and an injunction, with significant ACCC penalties likely.

Glossary

TermPlain English
ACL (Australian Consumer Law)The national consumer protection law — a Schedule at the back of the Competition and Consumer Act 2010 (Cth).
ACCCThe Australian Competition and Consumer Commission — the government watchdog that can sue businesses and ask for fines.
Cause of actionA legal reason to sue (e.g. misleading conduct; breach of a guarantee).
Consumer (s 3)Anyone who paid $100,000 or less, or bought household-type goods, or a goods truck/trailer — even a business, unless buying to resell.
Consumer contract (s 23(3))Narrower: an individual buying wholly or mainly for personal/home use. Needed for unfair terms.
In trade or commerce (s 2(1))Done as part of business or professional activity (profit or not) — not a private chat.
Engage in conduct (s 2(2))Doing something (saying, labelling, advertising) or deliberately not doing something (staying silent).
Misleading / deceptiveLeading someone into a mistake / making them believe something false.
Objective testJudged by how the audience would understand it — not by what the business meant.
Target audience / section of the publicThe group the ad or label was aimed at (Taco Bell test 1).
Puffery / puffVague sales boasting nobody takes literally ("best on Planet Earth!!!").
Future matter (s 4)A promise or prediction ("it will…"); misleading unless the business had reasonable grounds.
RepresentationA statement or impression given about something.
Consumer guaranteeA promise the law automatically adds to every sale to a consumer (works properly, fit for purpose, matches description…).
Acceptable quality (s 54)Fit for normal uses, looks OK, no defects, safe, lasts — as a fully informed reasonable buyer would expect.
Disclosed purpose (s 55)The particular job the buyer told the seller they needed the goods for.
RelianceThe buyer actually depended on the seller's advice or skill when buying.
VoidHas no legal effect at all — treated as if it was never written.
Exclude, restrict or modify (s 64)Cancel, limit or change the guarantees — not allowed.
UnconscionableSo unfair it is against good conscience — taking advantage of someone's weakness or your stronger position.
Special disadvantage / disabilityA weakness (age, illness, poor eyesight, poor English, grief) stopping someone protecting their own interests (Amadio; Blomley v Ryan).
Procedural / substantive unconscionabilityProcedural = unfair HOW the deal was made (pressure); substantive = unfair WHAT the terms say (harsh terms).
Listed public companyA company on the stock exchange — s 21 doesn't protect them.
Standard form contract (s 27)Pre-written terms, same for everyone, "take it or leave it", no negotiation.
Significant imbalanceThe contract gives one side much more rights than the other — not a trivial difference.
Legitimate interestsGenuine business needs the clause protects (e.g. getting equipment back on time).
DetrimentHarm — money, inconvenience, delay or distress.
Transparent (s 24(3))Plain language, readable, clearly presented, easy to get hold of.
Exit fee / forfeitureMoney you must pay to leave a contract / losing your deposit.
Bait advertising (s 35)Advertising a bargain to lure buyers, knowing stock will run out almost at once.
Unsolicited goods / inertia selling (s 40)Goods sent that nobody ordered, followed by a bill.
Damages (s 236)Money to make up for loss caused by the breach.
CausationProof that the breach actually caused the loss.
Injunction (ss 232–235)A court order to stop (or do) something.
RescissionCancelling the contract and putting both sides back where they started.
Compensation orders (ss 237–241)Court orders to pay back or compensate injured people.
Pecuniary penalty (ss 224–231)A large fine paid to the government (ACCC action) — available for s 29, not s 18 alone.
Adverse publicity order (s 247)The business is ordered to publish that it broke the law.
s 207 / s 208 defencess 207 = honest reasonable mistake of fact (e.g. relied on a supplier's info); s 208 = caused by someone else / something outside its control, and it took reasonable precautions.
On the balance of probabilitiesMore likely than not — the civil standard of proof.
W3 Exact words: Contract 3: vitiating factors

Use this page when…

  • The question says "Contract Law 3", "vitiating factors", "set aside", "rules of equity", or "can [client] get out of the contract / get the house back?"
  • Someone weak (very old, sick, depressed, grieving, poor English, poor eyesight, dependent on a carer) signs something or gives something away to someone stronger (salesperson, carer, family member, adviser).
  • Someone said something false before the signing, someone misunderstood what they signed, someone was threatened, or a disaster happened after signing.

Which factor(s) apply? Go down this table and tick EVERY row that matches your facts

What this means: each ticked row becomes its own heading in your booklet with its own Issue, Law, Application and Conclusion. Most questions tick 2–4 rows. Write the strongest one first (usually the order of this table). If a factor clearly fails, write ONE sentence saying why (shown in each section) instead of a full answer.

If your facts say…The factor is…Jump to
The weaker person is old, sick, depressed, grieving, has poor English or poor eyesight, is illiterate or drunk, or had no adviser — and the other side knew and pushed the deal throughUnconscionable conduct (Amadio)→ Unconscionable conduct
A carer, family member, spouse, doctor, lawyer, priest, trustee, bank manager, accountant or employer; "dependent on", "manages his affairs", "emotional dependency"; a house given away or a will changed in their favourUndue influence (Johnson v Buttress, Allcard v Skinner)→ Undue influence
Someone said something untrue about a present fact before signing ("it's risk-free", "no fees", "never been in an accident") and the client believed itMisrepresentation (fraudulent / negligent / innocent)→ Misrepresentation
The client could not read what they signed; or got the price or the thing wrong; or BOTH sides were wrong about the same thing; or each side meant a DIFFERENT thingMistake (common / mutual / unilateral / non est factum)→ Mistake
A threat: violence, keeping the client's goods until they pay, or refusing to perform unless paid moreDuress→ Duress
Something happens after signing that nobody caused: fire, flood, bushfire, destruction, event cancelled, new lawFrustration→ Frustration
Only sales pressure ("sign now", "limited-time deal") with no threatNOT duress — use it as "unfair advantage" in unconscionable conduct; dismiss duress in one line→ Duress (dismiss row)
The two people met for the first time that day (e.g. door-to-door salesperson)NOT undue influence — dismiss in one line→ Undue influence (stranger row)
Client wants out, money back, house back (always)Remedies→ Remedies
THE ORDER — your booklet headings (write only the factors you ticked, in this order)
  1. Opening (overview + general law)
  2. Issue 1: Unconscionable conduct
  3. Issue 2: Undue influence
  4. Issue 3: Misrepresentation
  5. Issue 4: Mistake
  6. Issue 5: Duress
  7. Issue 6: Frustration
  8. Remedies
  9. Overall conclusion

Renumber as you go: if you only tick three factors they become Issue 1, Issue 2, Issue 3. In a "carer gets the house" question undue influence is often the strongest, so you may write it first.

Opening

What this means: two short paragraphs at the very top. The first lists what you will discuss. The second explains the basic idea: a contract only binds someone who agreed freely and genuinely. A vitiating factor = something that spoils that agreement (a lie, a mistake, a threat, a dominating relationship, someone exploiting a weakness). Void = the contract never existed. Voidable = the contract is valid until the innocent person chooses to cancel it.

Write — overview

Whether [Client] can have the [contract with X / transfer of the property / will] set aside depends on whether [his/her] consent was vitiated by [unconscionable conduct, undue influence, misrepresentation and/or mistake][, and whether the contract has been discharged by frustration]. Each is considered in turn, followed by remedies.

Write — Law (general)

A contract will only be enforceable against a party if that party entered into it willingly, with free and voluntary consent. Factors that affect genuine consent are called vitiating factors, and include misrepresentation, mistake, duress, undue influence and unconscionable conduct. Where a vitiating factor is established, the contract may be void (of no contractual effect) or voidable (valid until set aside at the option of the innocent party).

If the client is dead and family are suing, add: "It is assumed that [the sons / the daughter] may bring the claim on behalf of [David]'s estate, as the question does not say." If it is a will, add: "Although a will is not a contract, the question directs that the principles of Contract Law 3 be applied."

Example (2025 S1 Juan): "Whether Juan can have his contract with Evergreen Solar Pty Ltd (ES) set aside depends on whether his consent was vitiated by unconscionable conduct, misrepresentation or mistake, and whether the contract has in any event been discharged by frustration. Each is considered in turn, followed by remedies."

Issue 1: Unconscionable conduct

What this means: Unconscionable conduct = the stronger person took unfair advantage of a weakness in the other person that they knew about. Special disadvantage = something about the weaker person (old age, illness, poor English, grief, can't see) that stops them looking after their own interests. Equity = court rules based on fairness that sit beside ordinary contract rules. No relationship is needed before the deal, so this works even for strangers like a door-to-door salesperson. There are 3 elements: (1) special disadvantage, (2) the stronger party knew, (3) they took unfair advantage.

Write — Issue

Can [Client] have the [contract/transfer] with [Stronger party] set aside on the basis of unconscionable conduct?

Write — Law

Unconscionable conduct is conduct that is unfair, unjust and against good conscience. While mere harshness of a term is not enough at common law, equity will intervene where there is no "reasonable degree of equality between the contracting parties" (Longmate v Ledger). As Kitto J stated in Blomley v Ryan, equity intervenes "whenever one party to a transaction is at a special disadvantage in dealing with the other party because illness, ignorance, impaired faculties… affect his ability to conserve his own interests, and the other party unconscientiously takes advantage of the opportunity thus placed in his hands."
Under Commercial Bank of Australia v Amadio, a contract will be set aside where: (1) the weaker party was under a special disability or disadvantage in relation to the stronger party; (2) the stronger party knew or ought to have known of that disadvantage; and (3) the stronger party took unfair advantage of it, so that it was unconscientious to procure the agreement in the circumstances. In Amadio, Mr and Mrs Amadio, elderly Italian migrants with limited written English, signed a guarantee and mortgage over their property for their son's company's debts, believing it was limited to $50,000 and six months when it was unlimited. The bank manager brought the document to their home, knew of their limited English and the company's financial difficulties, and did not explain it. The High Court set the guarantee aside.

Element (1) special disadvantage — pick EVERY row that matches (use more than one if you can)

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case to name
Poor English / migrant[Client] has [difficulty understanding English], so [he] could not properly understand the [contract]. This is very similar to the Amadios, elderly Italian migrants with limited written English, who did not understand that the guarantee they signed was unlimited.Amadio
Old age[Client] is [85] years old. Like the elderly Amadios, [his] age left [him] less able to protect [his] own interests.Amadio
Illness, depression, ADD, disability[Client] suffers from [depression / attention-deficit disorder / a disability]. This is the kind of "illness… impaired faculties" that Kitto J in Blomley v Ryan described as affecting a person's ability to conserve his own interests.Blomley v Ryan
Grief, recently widowed, emotionally dependent[Client] was grieving the recent death of [his wife], which affected [his] ability to make careful decisions and added to [his] special disadvantage.Blomley v Ryan
Can't read it: poor eyesight, blind, illiterate[Client] could not read the [35-page] contract because of [poor eyesight / illiteracy]. Unlike the Amadios, who could at least read some English, [Client] could not read the document at all, so [his] disadvantage is even greater.Amadio
Alone / remote / usual helper absent / no adviser[Client] [lives on a remote property / relies on his stepson, who was away,] and had no one to advise [him] when signing, which increased [his] disadvantage.Amadio
Drunk when signing[Client] was intoxicated when [he] signed. Intoxication is an "impaired faculty" that affected [his] ability to conserve [his] own interests.Blomley v Ryan

Nobody said it, but it was obvious / they live together / they manage the client's affairs[X] ought to have known of the disadvantage because [she lives with him and manages his personal and financial affairs]. [X] is in a far better position to know of [Client]'s weakness than the bank manager in Amadio, who was still held to know of the Amadios' limited English.Amadio

Against. [X] may argue that [Client] is an adult who chose to sign [and that the price was fair / a solicitor was present]. However, the doctrine in Amadio looks at how the agreement was procured, and [X's pressure after being told Client could not read the contract / the solicitor being X's choice] means this argument carries little weight.

Example (2025 S1 Juan): "Juan was under a serious special disadvantage. He is 85, an immigrant who 'still has difficulty understanding English', has poor eyesight, and is suffering from depression following the recent death of his wife. These are exactly the 'illness, ignorance, impaired faculties' described in Blomley v Ryan. Similar to the Amadios, who were elderly migrants with limited English, Juan's position is arguably worse, as his poor eyesight meant he could not read the 35-page contract at all. Jake knew of the disadvantage because Juan told him directly. Like the bank manager in Amadio, Jake had actual knowledge. It was unconscientious for Jake to procure the agreement because he insisted Juan sign immediately for a 'once-in-a-lifetime deal' instead of waiting for his stepson, called the contract 'risk-free', and did not explain any terms, including the one-sided clauses 10(a)(ii) ($4,000 exit fee for Juan) and 22(b)(vi) (ES may terminate free)."

Write — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, [Client] can have the [contract/transfer] set aside on the basis of unconscionable conduct, as all three elements in Amadio are satisfied.

Issue 2: Undue influence

What this means: Undue influence = one person has so much power over another, because of a relationship of trust or dependence, that the weaker person's decision is really the stronger person's. It needs a relationship that existed before the deal, so strangers don't count. Presumed = for some relationships (parent–child, doctor–patient etc.) the court automatically assumes influence, so you don't have to prove it. For every other relationship (carer–patient, spouses…) you must prove the stronger person dominated. Onus shifts = once that is shown, the stronger person has to prove the gift was the weaker person's own free, well-understood choice.

Write — Issue

Can [Client] have the [transfer/will/contract] in favour of [X] set aside on the basis of undue influence?

Write — Law

Undue influence occurs where a party has a degree of controlling influence and power over another person and dominates that person's will — "every case where influence is acquired and abused, where confidence is reposed and betrayed". A transaction is voidable at the election of the weaker party where: (1) the parties are in a pre-existing relationship; (2) one party has a controlling influence over the other; (3) the stronger party takes advantage of that influence for their own benefit; and (4) the weaker party is not exercising independent judgement, so there is no genuine free and voluntary consent.
Certain recognised relationships automatically raise a presumption of undue influence, including parent and child, guardian and ward, trustee and beneficiary, doctor and patient, lawyer and client, and religious adviser and disciple (Allcard v Skinner). In other relationships, the party alleging undue influence must show that the other party exercised a dominance or controlling influence over them. The courts have recognised that this may arise between spouses, principal and agent, bank and client, accountant and client, employer and employee, and carer and patient (Johnson v Buttress). Once this is shown, the onus shifts to the stronger party to prove that the transaction was the "pure voluntary and well-understood act" of the weaker party.
In Johnson v Buttress, Mr Buttress, an elderly widower who was illiterate and of limited intelligence, became dependent on Mrs Johnson after the death of his wife and transferred his only significant asset, his home, to her without proper independent advice. The High Court held that there was a relationship of influence and dependence, that Mrs Johnson could not show the transfer was his free and well-understood act, and set the transfer aside.

Element (1) relationship — pick the ONE row that matches your facts

Accountant → client[X] is [Client]'s accountant. This is not a presumed relationship, but the courts have recognised that controlling influence can arise between accountant and client, so [Client] must prove [X] dominated [his] financial decisions.Johnson v Buttress

Issue 3: Misrepresentation

What this means: a misrepresentation = a false statement about a fact (something true or untrue right now, or in the past) that persuaded the client to sign. A puff = obvious sales exaggeration ("best deal ever"); a promise or a prediction about the future is also NOT a misrepresentation. There are 3 types depending on what the speaker knew: fraudulent (knew it was false, or didn't care), negligent (honest but careless, and should have checked), innocent (honestly believed it). The type matters because only fraudulent and negligent get damages (money); innocent gets the contract cancelled only.

Write — Issue

Can [Client] rescind the contract with [X] on the basis of misrepresentation?

Write — Law

A misrepresentation is a false statement of fact made by one party to another which induces the other party to enter into the contract, making the contract voidable. The elements are: (1) a false statement of fact made by the representor to the representee; (2) concerning an existing fact or past event, not a statement of future intention; (3) made before or at the time the contract is concluded; and (4) intended to induce, and in fact inducing, the other party to enter into the contract. Honest statements of opinion, puffs or exaggerated statements, promises and statements of future intention are not misrepresentations. Mere silence is generally not a misrepresentation, unless a failure to disclose turns a statement into a half-truth.
A fraudulent misrepresentation is made knowingly, without belief in its truth, or recklessly (Derry v Peek). In Derry v Peek, directors stated in a prospectus that their company had the right to run steam trams when it in fact needed government consent; as they honestly believed the statement, it was not fraudulent. A negligent misrepresentation is an honest but careless statement made where a special relationship gives rise to a duty of care, about a serious or business matter, where the maker knows the recipient will rely on it, reliance is reasonable and damage results. An innocent misrepresentation is made with no intention to deceive (Oscar Chess Ltd v Williams, where a seller honestly but wrongly described a car as a 1948 model based on its log book). Rescission is available for all three; damages are available for fraudulent and negligent misrepresentation, but not innocent misrepresentation.

Variants — pick the ONE row per statement that matches

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case to name
Fraudulent: the speaker knew it was false, or couldn't possibly have believed it (e.g. a salesperson describing their own company's contract)As [X's role, e.g. ES's sales representative handing over ES's own contract], [X] either knew the statement was false or was reckless as to whether it was true. Unlike the directors in Derry v Peek, who honestly believed their statement, [X] could not honestly believe [that a contract with a $4,000 exit fee was "risk-free"]. The misrepresentation is therefore fraudulent.Derry v Peek
Negligent: an expert/professional who honestly believed it but should have checkedIf [X] honestly believed the statement, it was at least negligent: [X] was in a position of specialist knowledge about a [business] matter, knew [Client] would rely on it, [Client]'s reliance was reasonable, and [Client] suffered [financial] loss.(elements only — no case on the slide)
Innocent: the speaker honestly believed it and had a reason to (e.g. relied on a document)[X] honestly believed the statement because [he relied on the log book]. Similar to Oscar Chess Ltd v Williams, where the seller honestly described the car as a 1948 model based on its log book, this is an innocent misrepresentation, so [Client] may rescind but cannot claim damages.Oscar Chess v Williams
NOT a misrep: puff, opinion, promise or prediction ("will cut your bills in half", "best deal ever")The statements that ["…"] relate to future outcomes and are likely puffs or promises rather than statements of existing fact, so they are not misrepresentations. [Client] may argue that the word ["guaranteed"] turns this into an assertion of fact about [the system's capability], but this is the weaker argument.(principle)

It was false: clause 10(a)(ii) imposes a $4,000 exit fee on Juan. It was made before Juan signed and induced him to sign, as he signed 'trusting Jake's assurances'. As ES's sales representative handing over ES's own contract, Jake either knew of the exit fee or was reckless; unlike the directors in Derry v Peek, he could not honestly believe it was risk-free, so it is fraudulent. The statements that the system would 'instantly cut his power bills in half' and 'provide guaranteed energy independence' are about future results and are likely puffs or promises."

Write — Conclusion

If the question says "Contract Law 3 only", add one line: "([Client] may also have statutory remedies for misleading or deceptive conduct under the ACL, but this question is limited to Contract Law 3.)" If it says "must not discuss the ACL", leave this out.

Issue 4: Mistake

What this means: a mistake = someone misunderstood a basic fact when they signed. The general rule is that a mistake does NOT get you out of a contract (caveat emptor = "let the buyer beware"). Only big mistakes count. Common mistake = both sides wrong about the same thing. Mutual mistake = each side meant something different. Unilateral mistake = only one side was wrong, and the other side knew and took advantage. Non est factum (Latin, "it is not my deed") = the person signed without being able to read it AND didn't even know what kind of document it was. If the other side caused the mistake by saying something false, that is misrepresentation instead.

Write — Issue

Is the contract between [Client] and [X] void for [unilateral mistake or non est factum / common mistake / mutual mistake]?

Write — Law (general)

As a general rule, a mistake by one party does not make a contract unenforceable (caveat emptor). Only in exceptional circumstances is a contract void for mistake: the mistake must exist prior to or at the time of contracting, must be fundamental, and must be a mistake rather than a misrepresentation.

Variants — pick the ONE row that matches, and add its Law sentences after the general Law

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences: Law, then Application)Case to name
Common: BOTH sides believed the same wrong thing (painting is genuine, item still exists)Law: A common mistake occurs where both parties make the same mistake about a fundamental fact; the contract is void if the agreement was conditional on the truth of a belief held by both parties and that belief was incorrect when the contract was formed. In Leaf v International Galleries, both buyer and seller believed a painting was by Constable when it was not; the buyer, who waited about five years, could not rescind. Application: Both [A] and [B] believed […]. Similar to Leaf, where both believed the painting was a Constable, their agreement was conditional on that shared belief, which was wrong when they contracted. [Unlike Leaf, Client acted within … so delay is not a problem.]Leaf v International Galleries
Mutual: each side was thinking of a DIFFERENT thing (two ships, two properties with the same name)Law: A mutual mistake occurs where each party believed it agreed to something different; there is no meeting of the minds and no binding contract. In Raffles v Wichelhaus, a contract for cotton "ex Peerless" failed because there were two ships called Peerless and each party meant a different one. Application: [A] meant […] while [B] meant […]. As in Raffles v Wichelhaus, the parties were never agreed on the same thing.Raffles v Wichelhaus
Unilateral: only the client was wrong (price, cost, key term), and the other side knew and took advantageLaw: A unilateral mistake will make a contract void or unenforceable where one party has made a serious mistake about a fundamental aspect of the contract, and the other party knew or ought to have known of the mistake and sought to take advantage of it (Taylor v Johnson). In that case, Mrs Johnson agreed to sell her land for $15,000 in total, believing it was $15,000 per acre; the buyer knew of her mistake and deliberately kept her from discovering it, and the High Court set the contract aside. Application: [Client] was mistaken about [the cost of leaving the contract]. [X], knowing [he could not read it], took advantage by [urging him to sign immediately], similar to the buyer in Taylor v Johnson who knew of and exploited the seller's mistake. [X] would argue a mistake about [an exit fee] is not fundamental.Taylor v Johnson
Non est factum: client couldn't read the document (blind, poor eyesight, illiterate, no English) and was wrong about what KIND of document it wasLaw: Unilateral mistake also includes the limited doctrine of non est factum, where one party is encouraged to sign a written contract without first reading or understanding it. The court may find the contract void where the mistaken party had a good reason for not reading it (for example, they are blind, illiterate or cannot read English) and the mistake is about the fundamental nature of the document. Application: [Client] had a good reason for not reading the contract: [poor eyesight and difficulty understanding English]. However, [he knew he was signing a solar contract; his mistake was about a particular term], so the mistake was not about the fundamental nature of the document, and non est factum is unlikely to apply. (If they really thought it was something else, e.g. "a quote, not a contract", say it DOES apply.)(principle — no case on the slide)
The client was wrong only because the other side SAID something falseThis is better treated as a misrepresentation rather than a mistake, as [Client]'s mistaken belief was caused by [X]'s false statement (see Issue [3]).—
