Example (2025 S1 Juan): "Juan had a good reason for not reading the contract: his poor eyesight and difficulty understanding English, both of which he told Jake about. However, non est factum requires a mistake about the fundamental nature of the document. Juan knew he was signing a contract for a solar package; his mistake was about a particular term, the exit fee, so non est factum is unlikely to succeed. Alternatively, Juan believed the contract carried no risk, Jake knew Juan could not read it, and similar to the buyer in Taylor v Johnson, Jake took advantage by urging him to sign immediately. ES would argue an exit fee is not fundamental to a solar contract."

Write — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, [non est factum is unlikely to succeed, but Client has an arguable case that the contract is unenforceable for unilateral mistake; this is weaker than his claim for unconscionable conduct].

Issue 5: Duress

What this means: duress = someone was forced to sign by a threat of harm: to their body or family (duress to the person), to their belongings (duress to goods), or to their business by unlawful pressure (economic duress). The threat only has to be ONE of the reasons they signed. Ordinary sales pressure ("sign now, limited-time offer") is NOT a threat, so in most exam questions you dismiss duress in one sentence.

Write — Issue

Can [Client] have the contract set aside on the basis of duress?

Write — Law

Duress is pressure exerted by one party to coerce or compel another to contract on particular terms, and makes the contract voidable. It requires that one party expressly or impliedly threatened the other with harm, and that the threat contributed to the decision to enter the contract. Duress may be to the person (Barton v Armstrong, where a threat to have Barton killed if he did not sign was enough, as it need only be one reason for signing), to goods or property (Hawker Pacific v Helicopter, where a helicopter was withheld until the owner agreed to pay more), or economic, which requires the pressure to be in some way unlawful (North Ocean Shipping v Hyundai, where a shipbuilder threatened not to deliver a tanker unless paid 10% more).

Variants — pick the ONE row that matches

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case to name
Threat of violence to the client or their family[X] threatened [to hurt Client / his family] unless [he] signed. This is duress to the person. Similar to Barton v Armstrong, where a threat to have Barton killed made the deal voidable, the threat here need only be one reason [Client] signed, even if [he also wanted the deal for business reasons].Barton v Armstrong
Holding the client's goods until they agree to pay more[X] refused to release [Client]'s [car / equipment] unless [he] agreed to [pay more]. This is duress to goods. Similar to Hawker Pacific v Helicopter, where the owner urgently needed the helicopter and signed an agreement to pay more, [Client] [needed … urgently] and signed only to get it back.Hawker Pacific v Helicopter
Threat to break the contract unless paid more (business pressure)[X] threatened not to [deliver] unless [Client] paid [more]. Similar to North Ocean Shipping v Hyundai, where a shipbuilder demanded 10% more under threat of not delivering the tanker, this is economic duress, as threatening to breach an existing contract is unlawful pressure. [However, Client waited … months without protest, so like the buyer in North Ocean, he may have affirmed the contract.]North Ocean Shipping v Hyundai
Only sales pressure / urgency, no threat (MOST exam questions)Duress is unlikely to apply: although [X] pressured [Client] to sign immediately, there was no express or implied threat of harm to [his] person, property or economic interests (cf Barton v Armstrong). (Write only this, then move on.)Barton v Armstrong (cf)

Write — Application

Threat. [paste your row] Contributed to the decision. The threat contributed to [Client]'s decision to sign, as [he signed only after the threat / said he would not otherwise have agreed].

Example (2025 S1 Juan — dismiss row): "Duress is also unlikely: although Jake pressured Juan to sign immediately, there was no threat of harm to Juan's person, property or economic interests (cf Barton v Armstrong)."

Write — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, the contract [is / is not] voidable for duress.

Issue 6: Frustration

What this means: frustration = after the contract was signed, something happened that nobody caused (a fire, flood, bushfire, cancelled event, new law) and it makes the contract impossible or completely different from what was agreed. It is not a "vitiating factor" (the agreement was fine at the start) but it IS in Contract Law 3. The result is discharge = the contract ends and both sides are released from future obligations. "Harder" or "more expensive" is NOT enough.

Write — Issue

Has the contract between [Client] and [X] been discharged by frustration as a result of the [bushfire]?

Write — Law

A contract may be discharged by operation of law through frustration, where something happens that is not the fault of either party and makes performance impossible or only possible in a way neither party anticipated. Performance must be substantially different, not merely more difficult or expensive. A contract is frustrated if: (1) a supervening event has made performance completely impossible or impossible in the way originally envisaged; (2) neither party caused the event; (3) the contract did not provide for the event, expressly or impliedly; and (4) it would be unjust to compel either party to proceed. In Taylor v Caldwell, a music hall hired for a series of concerts was destroyed by fire before the concerts, and the contract was frustrated.

Variants — pick the ONE row that matches what happened

If… (what happened after signing)Write this (full sentences)Case to name
Fire, flood, bushfire, storm destroyed the thing or the placeThe [bushfire] is a supervening event which [destroyed …] and made [performance] impossible, like the fire that destroyed the music hall in Taylor v Caldwell.Taylor v Caldwell
The event the contract was for was cancelledThe whole purpose of the contract was [to attend/watch …], which was cancelled. Similar to Krell v Henry, where a room was hired to watch the coronation procession and the procession was cancelled, the contract is frustrated.Krell v Henry
A new law makes it illegalAfter the contract was made, [a new law] made [performance] illegal. As in Esposito v Bowden, a change in the law that makes performance illegal frustrates the contract.Esposito v Bowden
Conditions of performance changed dramaticallyThe conditions under which [X] must perform have changed dramatically because […], so performance would be radically different from what was envisaged (Codelfa v State Rail).Codelfa v State Rail
Death or illness of the person who had to personally perform[Performer]'s [death / serious illness] makes this personal-services contract impossible to perform, and neither party caused it, so the contract is frustrated.(principle — no case named)
Only delay, harder or more expensivePerformance has become [more expensive / delayed by a few days], but it is not substantially different from what was envisaged, so the contract is not frustrated.(principle)

Write — Application

(1) Supervening event. [paste your row] (2) No fault. Neither [Client] nor [X] caused [the bushfire]. Against: [X] may argue performance is only delayed, not impossible. However, [the system was promised "within a week"; a delay of over a year in a six-year package, while Client keeps paying for nothing] is substantially different from what was envisaged. (3) Not provided for. The contract does not appear to provide for this event: [the exit-fee clause deals with the purchaser choosing to cancel, not a natural disaster] (it is assumed there is no force majeure clause, as none is mentioned). (4) Unjust. It would be unjust to compel [Client] to [keep paying or pay the exit fee for a service X cannot provide].

Example (2025 S1 Juan): "The bushfire is a supervening natural disaster, like the fire in Taylor v Caldwell, which destroyed power infrastructure and made installation impossible. Neither Juan nor ES caused it. ES may argue performance is only delayed; however, the system was promised 'within a week', and a delay of over a year in a six-year package while Juan makes monthly payments for nothing is substantially different. Clause 10(a)(ii) deals with the purchaser choosing to cancel, not a natural disaster, so the contract does not provide for this event. It would be unjust to compel Juan to keep paying or pay a $4,000 exit fee."

Write — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, the contract has been frustrated, releasing [Client] from [his] future obligations, including [the monthly payments and the exit fee].

Remedies

What this means: rescission = the court cancels the contract and puts everyone back where they started (money refunded, house transferred back). It is the main remedy for every vitiating factor. Bars to rescission = 6 situations where the court will refuse to cancel (e.g. the client waited too long, or the house has already been sold to an innocent buyer). You must check EACH bar against the facts. Damages = money compensation; for misrepresentation only if fraudulent or negligent. Mitigate = the client must take reasonable steps to keep their loss small.

Write — Law

The primary remedy for a vitiating factor is rescission, an equitable remedy by which the court declares the contract terminated or unenforceable and, as far as possible, restores the parties to their original positions. The right to rescind is lost if substantial restitution is impossible, the contract has been affirmed, there has been delay, third party rights have intervened, the contract has been executed, or the party seeking relief has acted unconscientiously. Damages, which aim to restore the innocent party to the position they would have been in had the misrepresentation not occurred, are available for fraudulent and negligent misrepresentation but not innocent misrepresentation; the plaintiff must mitigate their loss (Payzu Ltd v Saunders).

Bars to rescission — write ONE sentence for EACH row (say it applies or doesn't)

Bar (plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case to name
Restitution impossible (can't put things back)Restitution is possible because [only a $2,500 deposit has been paid / X still holds the house]. (or) Restitution is impossible because [the goods have been consumed / destroyed], so rescission is barred.—
Affirmed (the client carried on with the contract after finding out)[Client] has not affirmed the contract, as [the system was never installed / he complained as soon as he found out], unlike the buyer in North Ocean Shipping v Hyundai, who waited months without protest.North Ocean Shipping v Hyundai
Delay (waited too long)[Client] acted promptly, [two weeks later / as soon as the will was read], unlike the buyer in Leaf v International Galleries, who waited five years [or: unlike Miss Allcard in Allcard v Skinner, who waited years after leaving the sisterhood].Leaf v International Galleries / Allcard v Skinner
Third party rights (an innocent outsider now has it)No third party rights have intervened, assuming [X] has not sold [the apartment] to an innocent buyer; if [she] has, rescission may be barred.—

Write — Application

[Client] should seek rescission. [paste one sentence for each of the 6 bars] The court would order [the $2,500 deposit be repaid / the property be transferred back to the estate]. [paste the damages row] [If frustration: In any event, as the contract has been frustrated, Client is released from future obligations, including …]

Example (2025 S1 Juan): "None of the bars apply. Juan acted within two weeks, unlike the buyer in Leaf v International Galleries who waited five years; he has not affirmed the contract, as the system was never installed; restitution is simple because only a $2,500 deposit has been paid; no third party rights are involved; the contract has not been executed; and Juan has not acted unconscientiously. The court would rescind the contract and order ES to refund the $2,500 deposit, and Juan would not be liable for the $4,000 exit fee or further payments. As the misrepresentation was fraudulent, Juan may also claim damages for any further loss, subject to mitigation (Payzu Ltd v Saunders)."

Write — Conclusion

Therefore, [Client] is likely to be entitled to rescission of the [contract/transfer], restoring the parties to their original positions.

Overall conclusion

What this means: one sentence that directly answers the question asked ("Advise Juan…"), naming the strongest factor(s) and what the client gets.

Write — Conclusion

[Client] should be advised that the [contract/transfer/will] is likely to be set aside, primarily for [strongest factor] and also for [second factor], entitling [him] to rescission and [the refund / return of …][; in any event, the … has likely frustrated the contract, releasing him from further obligations].

Example (2025 S1 Juan): "Juan should be advised that the contract is likely to be set aside, primarily for unconscionable conduct and also for Jake's misrepresentation that it was 'risk-free', entitling him to rescission and a refund of his $2,500 deposit; in any event, the bushfire has likely frustrated the contract, releasing him from further payments and the exit fee."

Full mini-answer — 2025 Semester 1, Question 2: Juan and Evergreen Solar (25 marks)

Facts in one line: Jake (ES salesperson) visits Juan (85, Spanish immigrant, poor English, poor eyesight, depressed since his wife died, stepson away), says the 35-page contract is "risk-free" and will "cut his power bills in half", insists he sign now; Juan pays a $2,500 deposit; hidden clause 10(a)(ii) = $4,000 exit fee for Juan, clause 22(b)(vi) = ES can terminate free; two weeks later a bushfire makes installation impossible for over a year.

Whether Juan can have his contract with Evergreen Solar Pty Ltd (ES) set aside depends on whether his consent was vitiated by unconscionable conduct, misrepresentation or mistake, and whether the contract has been discharged by frustration. Each is considered in turn, followed by remedies. A contract will only be enforceable against a party who entered into it willingly, with free and voluntary consent. Factors that affect genuine consent are called vitiating factors. Where established, the contract may be void, or voidable at the option of the innocent party.

Issue 1: Unconscionable conduct

Issue. Can Juan have the contract with ES set aside on the basis of unconscionable conduct?

Law. Unconscionable conduct is conduct that is unfair, unjust and against good conscience. Equity intervenes where there is no "reasonable degree of equality between the contracting parties" (Longmate v Ledger), that is, where one party "is at a special disadvantage… because illness, ignorance, impaired faculties… affect his ability to conserve his own interests, and the other party unconscientiously takes advantage" (Kitto J, Blomley v Ryan). Under Commercial Bank of Australia v Amadio: (1) the weaker party must be under a special disadvantage; (2) the stronger party must know or ought to know of it; and (3) the stronger party must take unfair advantage of it. In Amadio, elderly Italian migrants with limited written English signed an unlimited guarantee for their son's company, believing it was limited; the bank manager knew of their poor English but did not explain the document, and the guarantee was set aside.

Application. Juan was under a serious special disadvantage: he is 85, an immigrant who "still has difficulty understanding English", has poor eyesight, and suffers from depression following his wife's death. These are the "illness, ignorance, impaired faculties" described in Blomley v Ryan. Similar to the Amadios, Juan is elderly with limited English, and his position is worse, as his eyesight meant he could not read the 35-page contract at all. Jake knew of this because Juan told him directly; like the bank manager in Amadio, Jake had actual knowledge. Jake took unfair advantage: instead of letting Juan wait for his stepson, he called it a "once-in-a-lifetime deal", insisted Juan sign immediately and said it was "risk-free". As in Amadio, he explained nothing, and the contract is one-sided: clause 10(a)(ii) imposes a $4,000 exit fee on Juan, while clause 22(b)(vi) lets ES terminate without penalty. ES may argue Juan was an adult who chose to sign; however, Jake's pressure after being told Juan could not read the contract was clearly unconscientious.

Conclusion. On the balance of probabilities, Juan can have the contract set aside for unconscionable conduct, as all three elements in Amadio are satisfied.

For completeness, undue influence is unlikely, as it requires a pre-existing relationship and Juan met Jake that afternoon. Duress is also unlikely: although Jake pressured Juan, there was no threat of harm (cf Barton v Armstrong).

Issue 2: Misrepresentation

Issue. Can Juan rescind the contract on the basis of misrepresentation?

Law. A misrepresentation is a false statement of existing fact, made before or at the time of contracting, which is intended to and does induce the other party to contract; it makes the contract voidable. Opinions, puffs, promises and statements of future intention are not misrepresentations. A misrepresentation is fraudulent if made knowingly, without belief in its truth, or recklessly (Derry v Peek, where directors who honestly believed their prospectus statement about steam trams were not fraudulent). An innocent misrepresentation is made without intention to deceive (Oscar Chess Ltd v Williams). Damages are available for fraudulent and negligent, but not innocent, misrepresentation.

Application. The statements that the system would "instantly cut his power bills in half" and "provide guaranteed energy independence" concern future results and are likely puffs or promises. By contrast, "risk-free" is a statement about an existing fact, the content of the contract Jake handed over. It was false, as clause 10(a)(ii) imposes a $4,000 exit fee. It was made before signing and induced Juan to sign "trusting Jake's assurances". As ES's representative presenting ES's own contract, Jake knew of the fee or was reckless; unlike the directors in Derry v Peek, he could not honestly believe it was risk-free.

Conclusion. On the balance of probabilities, the "risk-free" assurance was a fraudulent misrepresentation, entitling Juan to rescind and claim damages.

Issue 3: Mistake

Issue. Is the contract void for unilateral mistake or non est factum?

Law. Generally a mistake does not affect a contract (caveat emptor). A unilateral mistake makes a contract unenforceable where a party made a serious mistake about a fundamental aspect and the other party knew of it and took advantage (Taylor v Johnson, where a buyer who knew the seller thought the price was per acre had the contract set aside). Non est factum may apply where a person had a good reason for not reading a document and was mistaken about its fundamental nature.

Application. Juan had a good reason for not reading the contract, but he knew it was a solar contract; his mistake concerned one term, so non est factum fails. Unilateral mistake is arguable: similar to the buyer in Taylor v Johnson, Jake knew Juan could not read the contract and took advantage, though ES would say an exit fee is not fundamental.

Conclusion. Mistake is arguable but weaker than Issues 1 and 2.

Issue 4: Frustration

Issue. Has the contract been discharged by frustration as a result of the bushfire?

Law. A contract is frustrated where (1) a supervening event makes performance impossible or radically different, (2) neither party caused it, (3) the contract did not provide for it, and (4) it would be unjust to compel performance (Taylor v Caldwell, where a music hall hired for concerts burnt down).

Application. The bushfire, like the fire in Taylor v Caldwell, made installation impossible, and neither party caused it. ES may say performance is only delayed, but a delay of over a year for a system promised "within a week" is substantially different. Clause 10(a)(ii) covers voluntary cancellation, not natural disasters. It would be unjust to make Juan keep paying for nothing.

Conclusion. On the balance of probabilities, the contract has been frustrated.

Remedies

Rescission restores the parties to their original positions, but is barred by impossibility of restitution, affirmation, delay, third party rights, execution or unclean hands. None apply: Juan acted within two weeks, unlike the five-year wait in Leaf v International Galleries; nothing was installed, so there is no affirmation or execution; the $2,500 deposit can be returned; no third party is involved; and Juan acted honestly. The court would order the deposit refunded, with no exit fee payable. Juan may also claim damages for the fraudulent misrepresentation, subject to mitigation (Payzu Ltd v Saunders).

Overall conclusion

Juan should be advised that the contract is likely to be set aside, primarily for unconscionable conduct and also for Jake's "risk-free" misrepresentation, entitling him to rescission and a refund of his $2,500 deposit; in any event, the bushfire has likely frustrated the contract.

≈1,250 words. If short on time, cut Issue 3 to two sentences.

Glossary

TermPlain English

VoidableA valid contract until the innocent person chooses to cancel it.

Undue influenceUsing a relationship of trust/dependence to dominate someone's decision.
Presumption / presumedThe court automatically assumes it, unless the other side proves otherwise.

"Pure voluntary and well-understood act"The weaker person's own free choice, made understanding what it meant.

W4 Exact words: Agency & partnership

Use this page when…

  • Someone signed, bought or sold something "on behalf of" someone else (a firm, a band, a home owner), and the people behind them now say "he had no authority" / "we won't pay" / "we want a refund".
  • Two or more people run a business together — split profits, joint bank account, "no formal agreement", "introduced as part of the team", one is called an "employee" but shares profits.
  • A third party (supplier, buyer, store owner) asks who they can sue to get paid.
  • A principal asks whether their agent ignored instructions, took a secret commission, or sold too cheap (fiduciary duties).
THE ORDER — your booklet headings
  1. Opener (1–2 lines)
  2. Issue 1: Is there a partnership? (skip only if it is clearly one owner + one agent, e.g. home owner + real estate agent)
  3. Issue 2: Did [A] have authority? — express, implied, apparent (one issue PER purchase/contract)
  4. Issue 2 (partners): Apparent authority of a partner — s 8 PA, four requirements
  5. Issue 3: Who is liable? — principal / agent personally / all partners
  6. Issue 4: Fiduciary duties (only if the question is from the principal's side or a partner misbehaved)
  7. Remedies
  8. Overall conclusion

Opener

What this means: one or two sentences at the top that tell the marker which issues you'll answer, in order. Principal (P) = the person/firm the agent acts for. Agent (A) = the person who signs or buys for them. Third party (3P) = the outsider who dealt with the agent (the supplier, the buyer, the store).

Whether [3P] can recover [$X / the price of the …] depends upon three issues: first, whether [A, B and C] carry on [business name] in partnership; second, whether [A] had authority to bind [the firm / P] when [signing / purchasing the …]; and third, if not, whether [A] is personally liable to [3P].

If the business is outside Queensland, add: "As [business] operates in [NSW], that State's partnership legislation would technically apply; this answer applies the Partnership Act 1891 (Qld) ('PA'), assuming the relevant provisions are equivalent." If someone says "company card"/"company" but nothing says it was registered, add: "There are no facts suggesting [business] has been incorporated under the Corporations Act 2001 (Cth); I assume it has not."

Example (2025 S2 StageCorp): Whether StageCorp Pty Ltd ('StageCorp') can recover the remaining $162,000 depends upon three issues: first, whether Callum, Tessa and Robert carry on CT Events in partnership; second, whether Robert had authority to bind CT Events and its partners to the stage contract; and third, if he did not, whether Robert is personally liable to StageCorp.

Issue 1: Is there a partnership?

What this means: a partnership is two or more people running a business together to make money. It does NOT need a written agreement or registration — if the 4 requirements are met, it exists, even if they never called it one. Why it matters: in a partnership every partner is the agent of the others, so what one partner signs can bind all of them, and each partner is personally liable for the firm's debts (they can lose their own house/savings = unlimited personal liability).

WRITE — Issue

Were [A, B and C] carrying on [business name] in partnership under the Partnership Act 1891 (Qld), such that each was the agent of the others?

WRITE — Law (copy all four requirement sentences)

A partnership is "the relation which subsists between persons carrying on a business in common with a view of profit": Partnership Act 1891 (Qld) ('PA') s 5(1). No registration or formalities are required, and the partnership agreement may be written, oral or wholly implied from the parties' conduct; a partnership exists whenever the four requirements are satisfied, regardless of the parties' intention. Each partner is both a principal and an agent of the other partners, and each has unlimited personal liability for the firm's debts: Kendall v Hamilton (1879).

First, there must be at least two persons, and generally no more than 20: Corporations Act 2001 (Cth) s 115.
Second, they must be carrying on a business, which includes any trade, occupation or profession (PA s 3) and requires some continuity or repetition of activity; a one-off project is usually not a partnership: Smith v Anderson (1880). A partnership begins from the date the parties begin working together to establish an identifiable business: Khan v Miah [2000].
Third, the business must be carried on in common, meaning each person acts on behalf of the others as well as themselves. An employer and employee carry on business together but are not partners, because the employer does not act on behalf of the employee: Degiorgio v Dunn (2004). Even silent partners count and are liable.
Fourth, the business must be carried on with a view of profit; it remains a partnership even if a loss is expected or made: Minter v Minter (2000).

Indicators — pick every row that matches your facts

Joint bank account, shared office, equal profit split, majority vote, "introduced as part of the team"They [share an office / manage finances through a joint account / split profits equally / decide by majority vote / were introduced to clients as part of the team], which shows each acting on behalf of the others as well as themselves, so the business is carried on in common.PA s 5(1)
Someone is called an "employee" or gets a salary — BUT also shares profits / can spend from joint account / votes[X] may argue that [Y] is merely an employee, like the employee in Degiorgio v Dunn, because [Y] receives [a salary]. However, this is unlikely to succeed because [Y] [receives an equal share of profits / can spend from the shared account / has an equal vote] — rights an employee would not have. The [salary] merely compensates [extra responsibilities].Degiorgio v Dunn

Someone does their own freelance work on the sideOccasional individual freelance work does not change the character of the [business] itself (although it may raise a separate issue of competing with the firm under s 33 PA).PA s 33

(If loss:) It does not matter that the business [made a loss], because it was still seeking profit: Minter v Minter.Minter v Minter

WRITE — Application (one sentence per requirement, in order)

Persons. There are [number] persons — [names] — well within the limit of 20. This requirement is satisfied.
Carrying on a business. [Business] has operated as [type of business] since [date], with [facts showing continuity]. Unlike Smith v Anderson, this is a continuing trade, not a single project. [Late joiner] joined in [date]; consistent with Khan v Miah, [he/she] became a partner from the time [he/she] began working in the business.
In common (for). They [shared facts: office / joint account / equal profits / majority vote / "part of the team"], which shows each acting on behalf of the others.
In common (against). [X] may argue that [Y] was merely an employee because [salary / freelance work]. However, unlike the employee in Degiorgio v Dunn, [Y] receives [an equal share of profits, has access to the joint account and has an equal vote] — none of which is consistent with employment.
View of profit. [Profit facts], so the business is clearly carried on with a view of profit (Minter v Minter).

Example (2025 S2 StageCorp): There are three persons — Callum, Tessa and Robert. CT Events has operated since mid-2022 with a website and growing client base; unlike Smith v Anderson, this is a continuing trade. Robert joined in early 2023; consistent with Khan v Miah, he became a partner from when he began working in the business. They share an office, a joint account, split profits equally and decide by majority vote, and Robert was introduced to clients "as part of the team". Callum and Tessa may argue Robert's freelance work shows they were not truly in business together; however, unlike the employee in Degiorgio v Dunn, Robert shares profits equally, uses the joint account and has an equal vote. Profits are split equally, so there is a view of profit (Minter v Minter).

WRITE — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, [A, B and C] are partners in [business] under s 5(1) PA, so [A] is an agent of the firm and of [B and C].

Issue 2: Did [A] have authority to make the [purchase / contract]?

What this means: authority = permission to make deals that bind the principal. If the agent had authority, the deal is between the principal and the third party (the principal pays; the agent doesn't). There are 3 kinds — always go through ALL THREE in this order: express actual (P actually told A "you may do this", in writing or out loud), implied actual (things that naturally come with the job P gave A), apparent (P made the outsider reasonably believe A had permission, even if A didn't). Two purchases = do this issue twice (usually one is authorised and one isn't).

WRITE — Issue

Is [P / the firm and its partners] bound by the contract [A] made with [3P] for [the item], on the basis that [A] had authority under the law of agency?

WRITE — Law

Agency is a legal relationship in which one person (the agent) is authorised to act on behalf of another (the principal) in dealings with a third party. If the agent acts within the scope of their authority, the resulting contract is between the principal and the third party, and the agent incurs no personal liability. Authority may be actual — express or implied — or apparent.

Actual authority "is a legal relationship between principal and agent created by a consensual agreement to which they alone are parties": Freeman & Lockyer v Buckhurst Park Properties Ltd [1964]. Express actual authority is given by the principal in writing (including by deed, such as a power of attorney) or orally. Implied actual authority extends to all things necessarily incidental to carrying out the express instructions, or needed to give them business efficacy; it is not found merely because it would be convenient: Peterson v Moloney (1951).

Apparent authority arises where the principal leads a third party to reasonably believe the agent has authority; the principal is then bound even if the agent had no actual authority, and even if the principal expressly prohibited the act. Three requirements must be met: (1) the third party did not know the agent lacked actual authority; (2) the principal held the agent out as having authority, for example by appointing them to a position or allowing them to act in the past; and (3) the third party reasonably relied on that representation. In Panorama v Fidelis Furnishing Fabrics (1971), a company secretary hired cars in the company's name, partly for personal use; the company was bound because hiring cars fell within a secretary's usual, and therefore apparent, authority. The representation must come from the principal, not the agent: in Essington v Regency (2004) there was no apparent authority to vary a contract because the principal had done nothing to represent that the agent could do so.

Partnership question? Add this sentence to the Law: "Each partner has implied authority to do the usual things necessary to carry on the firm's business, such as buying goods of the kind usually employed in that business, borrowing money and incurring debts; but implied authority can be overridden by the partnership agreement or express instructions to the contrary: Construction v Hexyl (1985)." Then for apparent authority use the s 8 four requirements in the next heading.

The 3 kinds of authority — write a paragraph for EACH, in this order

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
1. Express actual — P told A exactly what they could do (in writing or out loud). Check: did the deal fit inside what P said? Was any condition (permission, price, spending limit) met?Express actual authority. [P] expressly authorised [A], [in writing / orally], to [instruction, e.g. sell to the first buyer over $1 million / spend up to $50,000 on musical equipment with another member's permission]. The [purchase / sale] [fell within / fell outside] this because [reason, e.g. no permission was obtained / the price was below $1 million]. (If nothing was said:) There is no evidence that [P] expressly authorised [A] to [act]; indeed [P] was ["furious" / "outraged"] on learning of it. There was no express authority.Freeman & Lockyer v Buckhurst Park Properties
2. Implied actual — not said out loud, but a natural part of the job P gave A (real estate agent → show the house, give information; partner → buy the usual goods of the business). Fails if an express rule/practice says otherwise.Implied actual authority. [3P] will argue that [A] had implied authority because [buying event equipment / answering buyers' enquiries] is [necessarily incidental to the task / a usual thing necessary to carry on the firm's business]. However, [the firm only ever hired equipment / decisions were made by majority vote / the agreement required another member's permission], and as in Construction v Hexyl, implied authority is overridden by that express arrangement. As in Peterson v Moloney, implied authority is unlikely to be found.

Write one sentence per element.Apparent authority. First, [3P] did not know of [the limit / restriction] — [A said nothing about it]. Second, [P] held [A] out as having authority by [appointing A as selling agent / a sign directing all enquiries to A / allowing A to buy from 3P in the past]. Similar to Panorama v Fidelis Furnishing Fabrics, where the company was bound by car hire within the usual authority of the secretary's position, [the deal] fell within what someone in [A]'s position would usually do. Third, [3P] reasonably relied on that representation because [saw the sign and dealt with the agent P appointed].Panorama v Fidelis Furnishing Fabrics

Extra situations — add the matching row inside your apparent-authority paragraph

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
A told 3P "I'm authorised…" but P never said so[P] will rely on Essington v Regency to argue that [A]'s statement that ["quote"] came only from the agent and cannot create authority. (If P ALSO did something:) That is correct as far as it goes, but [3P] does not rely on [A]'s words alone; [P]'s own [appointment of A / sign] is a representation by the principal. (If P did nothing:) As in Essington v Regency, where the principal had done nothing to represent the agent could vary the contract, [P] made no representation, so there is no apparent authority.Essington v Regency
A was sacked / P withdrew A's authority, but A kept dealing with old suppliers who weren't toldEnding [A]'s actual authority does not end apparent authority; [P] had to inform [3P] that [A] was no longer authorised. Similar to Summers v Solomon, where the supplier did not know the agent's employment had ended and the principal was still bound, [3P] was never told, so [P] remains bound. The key question is whether [3P] knew the authority had been withdrawn: Tooth v Laws.Summers v Solomon; Tooth v Laws
A is a company officer (director, MD/CEO, secretary)The more senior the role, the more implied authority: Giltrap City Ltd v Commerce Commission [2004]. A single director alone generally has none; an MD/CEO may make contracts in the ordinary course of business; a company secretary may make administrative contracts only, which in Panorama v Fidelis Furnishing Fabrics included hiring cars. Here [A] is [role] and [the contract] [is / is not] of that kind.Giltrap City; Panorama
Emergency — A has P's goods, they're under threat, P can't be contacted (rare)An agency of necessity arises if: (1) [A] has possession of [P]'s property; (2) the property is under threat; (3) [A] cannot contact [P] for instructions; and (4) [A] acts in good faith. Unlike Springer v Great Western Railway, where the agent could have contacted the principal and failed requirement 3, here [facts].Springer v Great Western Railway

WRITE — Application (template order)

A relationship of principal and agent exists: [P] is the principal, [A] is the agent and [3P] is the third party. (Partnership: As established above, [A] is a partner and therefore an agent of the firm and of [B and C].)
Express actual authority. [Row 1 sentences].
Implied actual authority. [Row 2 sentences]. Therefore, [A] acted outside [his/her] actual authority.
Apparent authority. [Row 3 sentences, or the s 8 four requirements below if partners].

Example (Week 12 Cristina/Davo): Cristina gave Davo express authority, in writing, to sell to the first buyer offering over $1 million. He also had implied authority to show Patrizia through the house and give her information. However, in accepting $850,000 he acted outside his actual authority. As to apparent authority, Patrizia did not know of the $1 million limit; Cristina held Davo out by appointing him and erecting a sign saying "All enquiries to David Barnes". Cristina will rely on Essington v Regency to argue Davo's claim that he was "authorized … to accept any offer that I determine to be reasonable" cannot create authority, but Patrizia relies on Cristina's own sign and appointment. Similar to Panorama, her reliance was reasonable.

WRITE — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, [A] [had / did not have] [actual / apparent] authority, so [P / the firm and partners B and C] [is / are / is not / are not] bound to pay [3P] for [item].

Issue 2 (partners): Apparent authority of a partner — s 8 PA

What this means: when the agent is a partner, apparent authority is tested using the Partnership Act s 8, which has four requirements (use these instead of the general three). Even if the other partners never gave permission, the firm is bound if the deal was the normal kind of deal for that business, done in the normal way, with someone who thought they were dealing with a partner and didn't know about any lack of permission. A secret internal rule (e.g. "needs another partner's OK") only binds the partners, not outsiders — unless the outsider knew about it.

WRITE — Law

Under s 8 PA, every partner is an agent of the firm, and acts done "for carrying on in the usual way business of the kind carried on by the firm" bind the firm and the partners, unless the partner had no authority and the third party knew this, or did not know or believe them to be a partner. Accordingly, where a partner lacks actual authority, the firm is still bound if four requirements are met.
First, the partner was carrying on business of the kind carried on by the firm, judged by what the firm's usual business actually is and what transactions a firm of that kind would usually engage in: Mercantile v Garrod (1962).
Second, the business was carried on in the usual way, because otherwise the third party should suspect a lack of authority: Goldberg v Jenkins (1889).
Third, the third party knew or believed the person was a partner.
Fourth, the third party did not know or suspect the lack of authority, relying in good faith and without notice: Crouch v IPG (2013).
The partnership agreement binds only the partners, not third parties.

Pick the ONE row per requirement that matches your facts

Req 1 MET — the item is exactly what this business uses (drum set for a band)First, [item] is exactly the type of goods [a touring band] uses, so similar to Panorama, the purchase falls within what a partner of such a firm would usually do; it is business of the kind carried on by the firm.Mercantile v Garrod
Req 1 NOT met — a new direction, much bigger scale, or a personal item (stage for concerts when firm does small events; a painting for a band)First, applying Mercantile v Garrod, the court looks at what [firm]'s business actually is: [small functions under 100 guests]. [Item] was [for large-scale concerts and festivals / a personal item] — a different kind of business — so this requirement is probably not met.Mercantile v Garrod

Req 2 NOT met — firm usually hires not buys; unusually large; sent to a home addressSecond, [firm]'s usual way was to [hire equipment], so [a $180,000 purchase by one partner / delivery to a home address] was not the usual way. Req 3 — 3P knew A was a partner (signed "on behalf of", introduced as part of the team, past dealings)Third, [3P] knew or believed [A] was a partner: [A signed "on behalf of the firm" / paid from the joint account], and [B and C] themselves held [A] out by [introducing him to clients as part of the team / allowing past purchases]. Example (2023 S2 drum set): A drum set is exactly what a touring band uses, bought for upcoming Christmas performances. Roxanne "regularly buys band equipment" from Bob on the band's card and sent it to the band's studio — the usual way. Maxine and Kevin held her out by allowing past purchases; similar to Panorama, her established role made the purchase look authorised. There is no evidence Bob knew of the permission rule, which binds only the members. (Painting: a personal item sent to her home address — not the kind of business (Mercantile v Garrod), not the usual way (Goldberg v Jenkins) → no authority.)

WRITE — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, [A] [had / did not have] apparent authority under s 8 PA, so [the firm and partners B and C] [are / are not] bound by the [contract].

Issue 3: Who is liable?

What this means: after authority, say who actually has to pay. If there WAS authority → the principal pays (in a partnership, all partners together, from their own pockets). If there was NO authority → the principal walks away, but the outsider can sue the agent personally for breach of warranty of authority = the agent effectively promised "I'm allowed to do this" and that promise was false.

WRITE — Issue (only for a deal with NO authority)

Can [3P] hold [A] personally liable for breach of warranty of authority?

WRITE — Law

Where an agent lacks authority, the agent is personally liable to the third party for breach of warranty of authority if: (1) the agent claimed to make the contract on behalf of the principal; (2) the agent did not in fact have authority; and (3) the third party relied on the representation and would not have contracted without it: Oliana Foods Pty Ltd v Culinary Co [2020]. The agent may also be liable in deceit or negligence, or for misleading conduct under s 18 of the Australian Consumer Law.

Issue 4: Did [A] breach fiduciary duties owed to [P / the other partners]?

What this means: fiduciary = a relationship of trust where one person must put the other's interests first. An agent owes these duties to the principal; partners owe them to each other. Use this heading when the principal is your client (e.g. "advise Cristina") or a partner misbehaved. These duties come from equity (judge-made fairness rules) and are often also terms of the agency contract — so a breach is usually a breach of contract too.

WRITE — Issue

Did [A] breach the fiduciary duties [he/she] owed to [P / the other partners]?

WRITE — Law (name only the duties you will use)

Agents owe fiduciary duties to their principal as a result of the relationship of trust and confidence between them; these duties arise in equity and are often also terms of the contract of agency. They include the duty to [duties from the table below, each with its case].

Remedies

What this means: say exactly who sues whom for how much. Damages = money to make up for the loss. Indemnity = being paid back for costs. An agent has no automatic right to be paid (remuneration) unless agreed, and gets no indemnity for things done outside instructions.

Full mini-answer — 2025 S2 Q2: StageCorp v CT Events (all blanks filled)

Whether StageCorp Pty Ltd ('StageCorp') can recover the remaining $162,000 depends upon three issues: first, whether Callum, Tessa and Robert carry on CT Events in partnership; second, whether Robert had authority to bind CT Events and its partners to the stage contract; and third, if not, whether Robert is personally liable to StageCorp. As CT Events operates in Sydney, NSW legislation would technically apply; this answer applies the Partnership Act 1891 (Qld) ('PA'), assuming the provisions are equivalent.

Issue 1: Partnership

Issue: Were Callum, Tessa and Robert carrying on CT Events in partnership under the PA, such that each was the agent of the others?

Law: A partnership is "the relation which subsists between persons carrying on a business in common with a view of profit": PA s 5(1). No formalities are required and the agreement may be implied from conduct. Each partner is principal and agent of the others, with unlimited personal liability: Kendall v Hamilton (1879). First, there must be at least two persons and generally no more than 20: Corporations Act 2001 (Cth) s 115. Second, they must be carrying on a business with continuity; a one-off project is usually not enough: Smith v Anderson (1880); a partnership begins when the parties begin working together in an identifiable business: Khan v Miah [2000]. Third, the business must be carried on in common; an employer and employee are not partners: Degiorgio v Dunn (2004). Fourth, there must be a view of profit: Minter v Minter (2000).

Application: There are three persons, within the limit of 20. CT Events has operated since mid-2022 with a website, social media presence and expanding client base; unlike Smith v Anderson, this is a continuing trade. Consistent with Khan v Miah, Robert became a partner when he began working in the business in early 2023, despite no formal agreement. The three share an office, a joint account, split profits equally and decide by majority vote, and Robert was introduced to clients "as part of the team" — each acting on behalf of the others. Callum and Tessa may argue that each person's freelance work shows they were not truly in business together; however, unlike the employee in Degiorgio v Dunn, Robert shares profits equally, uses the joint account and has an equal vote. Profits are split equally, so there is a view of profit (Minter v Minter).

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, Callum, Tessa and Robert are partners under s 5(1) PA, so Robert is an agent of the firm and of Callum and Tessa.

Issue 2: Robert's authority to buy the stage

Issue: Are CT Events and its partners bound by the $180,000 contract Robert signed with StageCorp, on the basis that Robert had authority under the law of agency?

Law: Agency is a relationship in which an agent is authorised to act for a principal in dealings with a third party; if the agent acts within authority, the contract is between principal and third party. Actual authority arises from a consensual agreement between principal and agent: Freeman & Lockyer v Buckhurst Park Properties Ltd [1964]. Partners have implied authority to do the usual things needed to carry on the firm's business, but this may be overridden by the partnership agreement or express instructions: Construction v Hexyl (1985); implied authority is not always found: Peterson v Moloney (1951). Under s 8 PA, acts "for carrying on in the usual way business of the kind carried on by the firm" bind the firm. The firm is bound if: (1) the business was of the kind carried on by the firm: Mercantile v Garrod (1962); (2) it was done in the usual way: Goldberg v Jenkins (1889); (3) the third party knew or believed the person was a partner; and (4) the third party did not know or suspect the lack of authority: Crouch v IPG (2013). The representation must come from the principals: Essington v Regency (2004).

Application: Express. There is no evidence Callum or Tessa expressly authorised the purchase; they were "outraged", and Robert admitted he still needed to "convince Callum and Tessa". Implied. StageCorp will argue buying event equipment is usual for an events business. However, CT Events deliberately hired rather than bought equipment and decided matters by majority vote; as in Construction v Hexyl, that practice overrides any implied authority, and as in Peterson v Moloney, none is likely to be found. Kind of business. Applying Mercantile v Garrod, CT Events' business is small functions under 100 guests; a custom stage for "large-scale concerts and festivals" is a different kind of business. Usual way. The usual way was to hire, and a $180,000 purchase by one partner is out of proportion; as in Goldberg v Jenkins, StageCorp should have suspected a lack of authority. Believed a partner. Satisfied: Robert signed "on behalf of CT Events", paid from the joint account, and Callum and Tessa held him out as part of the team — unlike Essington v Regency, the holding out came from the principals. No notice. Robert said the purchase was his "long-term goal", "provided he could convince Callum and Tessa", so StageCorp was on notice and did not rely in good faith without notice (Crouch v IPG).

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, Robert had no actual or apparent authority, so CT Events, Callum and Tessa are not bound.

Issue 3: Robert's personal liability

Issue: Can StageCorp hold Robert personally liable for breach of warranty of authority?

Law: An agent lacking authority is personally liable if: (1) the agent claimed to contract on behalf of the principal; (2) the agent had no authority; and (3) the third party relied on that representation and would not otherwise have contracted: Oliana Foods Pty Ltd v Culinary Co [2020].

Application: First, Robert signed "on behalf of CT Events" and paid from the joint account. Second, he had no authority. Third, StageCorp would not have built a $180,000 custom stage for Robert personally. Robert may argue StageCorp did not rely on any claim of authority because he disclosed he needed his partners' support; however, by signing a binding contract in the firm's name and paying a deposit from its account, he represented that the firm was committed.

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, Robert is personally liable to StageCorp for breach of warranty of authority.

Remedies

StageCorp's best course is to claim damages from Robert personally, up to the $162,000 balance less what it recovers by taking back and reselling the stage. In the alternative, StageCorp may argue Robert had apparent authority under s 8 PA; if accepted, Callum, Tessa and Robert would be jointly and personally liable for $162,000 (Kendall v Hamilton), as a partnership is not a separate legal entity — but given Robert's statement, this is weaker. Separately, Callum and Tessa may pursue Robert for breach of his duty to disclose matters affecting the firm (s 31 PA) to recover the $18,000 deposit.

Glossary

TermPlain English
AgencyA relationship where one person (agent) is allowed to make deals for another (principal) with outsiders.
Principal (P)The person/firm the agent acts for — the one who ends up bound by the deal if there was authority.
Agent (A)The person who signs/buys/sells for the principal.
Third party (3P)The outsider who dealt with the agent (supplier, buyer, store).
AuthorityPermission to make deals that bind the principal.
Express actual authorityPermission P actually gave A, in writing or out loud.
Implied actual authorityPermission that naturally comes with the job P gave A (things "necessarily incidental").
Apparent (ostensible) authorityP's own words/conduct made the outsider reasonably believe A had permission — P is bound even though A didn't really have it.
Holding outP presenting A to the world as having authority (appointing them, a sign, letting them buy before).
Business efficacyWhat is needed to make the arrangement work in practice.
Partnership2+ people running a business together for profit (s 5(1) PA); no paperwork needed.
In commonEach partner acts for the others as well as themselves (unlike an employee).
With a view of profitTrying to make money (even if they actually lose money).
Silent partnerA partner who doesn't take part in running the business — still a partner, still liable.
Unlimited personal liabilityThe partners' own assets (house, savings) can be taken to pay the firm's debts.
Jointly liableAll partners are responsible together for the whole debt.
Separate legal entityA company is its own "person" in law (Salomon); a partnership is NOT.
On noticeThe outsider was told or should have realised something was wrong.
Good faithHonestly, without knowing of any problem.
Breach of warranty of authorityThe agent falsely represented "I'm allowed to do this", so the agent personally pays the outsider.
Fiduciary dutyA duty of trust to put the other person's interests first (agent → principal; partner → partners).
EquityJudge-made rules of fairness, separate from contract.
Secret commission / profitMoney the agent takes on the side without the principal knowing.
RemunerationPayment for the agent's work — only if agreed.
IndemnityBeing paid back for costs/liabilities incurred doing P's instructions.
LienThe agent's right to hold on to P's property until paid.
Vicarious liabilityAn employer being liable for the wrongs its employee commits at work.
Agency of necessityAuthority created by an emergency where P can't be contacted (rare).
Balance of probabilitiesMore likely than not (the civil standard of proof).
W5 Exact words: Contract 1–2

Use this page when…

  • The question says "Contract Law 1" or "Contract Law 2", "was a contract formed?", "is the promise enforceable?", "enforceability of the statements", or "can [business] rely on the disclaimer?"
  • Formation facts: haggling ("How about $120,000?"), an ad / eBay listing / shop display, "That's a deal", family members making a deal, or a promise made after the deal was done.
  • Terms facts: someone said something before signing ("I assure you it handles 10,000 users") and the written contract doesn't mention it; sales talk ("best on the market").
  • Disclaimer facts: a sign, ticket, locker notice or T&Cs saying "not liable for any loss however caused", and something went wrong (item stolen, person hurt).

What this means: one sentence at the top that lists the issues you are about to answer, so the marker sees your plan. Pick the version for your question type.

If your question is…Write this

B. TermsWhether [Claimant] can enforce the statements made by [A] and [B] depends upon: (1) whether each statement is a term of the contract or a mere representation; (2) whether the parol evidence rule excludes it, or a collateral contract arises; (3) whether [B]'s statements are mere puffery; and (4) whether the written clause has been breached. C. DisclaimerWhether a disclaimer will effectively protect [Business] from liability for breach of contract depends upon two separate issues: (1) whether the disclaimer is in fact a term of the contract; and (2) whether the disclaimer will be interpreted as applying to the breach in question.

Example (2024 S2 GlobalCorp): "Whether GlobalCorp can enforce the statements made by Alex and Damon depends upon three issues: (1) whether Alex's assurance is a term of the contract, or is otherwise enforceable despite the parol evidence rule as a collateral contract; (2) whether Damon's statements are terms or mere sales puffery; and (3) whether the written clause requiring 'industry-standard performance capabilities' has been breached. The available remedies are then considered."

1. Agreement (offer, counter-offer, acceptance)

What this means: a contract needs 3 things: agreement, intention and consideration. Agreement = an offer (a firm proposal: "you can buy it for $140,000") plus an acceptance (a clear "yes" to exactly those terms). An invitation to treat = an invitation to start negotiating, not an offer (ads, shop shelves, eBay listings, price lists). A counter-offer = a reply that changes the terms ("How about $120,000?"); it kills the first offer and becomes a new offer. Go through the facts in time order and label each statement.

Write — Issue

Was a contract formed between [A] and [B] for [the sale of the painting]?

Write — Law

A contract is a legally binding promise or agreement. A simple contract may be made verbally, in writing or by conduct, and requires three elements: (1) agreement; (2) an intention to create legal relations; and (3) consideration.
Agreement consists of an offer plus an acceptance. An offer is a proposal by one party to another to immediately enter into a legally binding contract, assessed objectively by asking whether a reasonable person in the recipient's position would believe it to be an offer. An offer must be distinguished from an invitation to treat, which merely invites others to deal; advertisements and price lists are usually invitations to treat, although an advertisement can be an offer to the world at large where it shows a willingness to be bound immediately (Carlill v Carbolic Smoke Ball Co, where an advertisement promising £100 to anyone who caught influenza after using the smoke ball, backed by £1,000 deposited in the bank, was an offer). Acceptance is a final and unqualified assent to all the terms of the offer, made by words or conduct and judged objectively. If acceptance is qualified, it is a counter-offer, which rejects the original offer and replaces it with a new offer. Acceptance must generally be communicated, and silence is not acceptance (Felthouse v Bindley, where an uncle's statement "if I hear no more… I consider the horse mine" did not create a contract when the nephew said nothing).

Variants — label EACH statement in your facts with ONE of these rows (in time order)

2. Intention to create legal relations

What this means: the parties must have meant the deal to be legally binding (enforceable in court), not just a friendly arrangement. In the past courts assumed business deals were binding and family/social deals were not. Now there are no presumptions: you look at all the circumstances (how much money, the words used, the setting, the relationship). Onus = the person saying there is a contract has to prove it. Family members CAN have a binding contract if the deal is serious.

Write — Law

The parties must intend to create legal relations, and the onus is on the party alleging the contract. Traditionally, courts presumed that commercial agreements were intended to be binding and that social or domestic agreements were not, though either presumption could be rebutted (Wakeling v Ripley). In that case, Ripley persuaded his sister and brother-in-law to give up their life in England and move to Sydney to care for him, promising they could live in his house and inherit it. Despite the family relationship, the court found an intention to be bound because of the seriousness of the promises and the sacrifices made in reliance on them. The current approach is to consider the agreement on its own merits, without presumptions, by assessing objectively all the circumstances: the parties' conduct, the context, the language used, the value of any consideration and the nature of the relationship (Ermogenous v Greek Orthodox Community of SA).

Variants — pick the ONE row that matches the relationship

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case to name
Business / strangers / commercial dealThe agreement between [A] and [B] was made in a commercial context: [two companies / a shop and a customer] dealing at arm's length for [$…]. Considering all the circumstances (Ermogenous), including the commercial language and the value involved, the parties clearly intended to be legally bound.Ermogenous
Family / friends, but the deal is serious (big money, haggling, cheque, delivery)[B] may argue that, as [siblings chatting over coffee at home], they did not intend legal consequences. However, the nature of the relationship is no longer decisive (Ermogenous). As was the case in Wakeling v Ripley, where family members intended to be bound because the arrangement was serious, the parties here intended to be legally bound. The following facts support this: (1) [the value was very large, $120,000]; (2) [genuine price negotiation]; (3) [commercial language: "That's a deal"]; (4) [a cheque for the full price]; (5) [a delivery arrangement].Wakeling v Ripley; Ermogenous
Family / friends, casual, small, no money or formality ("I'll drive you to work")Considering all the circumstances (Ermogenous), the arrangement was [a casual promise between friends, with no money involved and no formal steps]. Unlike Wakeling v Ripley, where the Wakelings gave up their life in England in reliance on serious promises, nothing here suggests the parties intended legal consequences, so there is no intention to create legal relations.Wakeling v Ripley (contrast)

However, the nature of the relationship is no longer decisive (Ermogenous). As in Wakeling v Ripley, the parties clearly intended to be bound: the price was $120,000; they negotiated from $140,000 down to $120,000; Mary said 'That's a deal'; Jack wrote a cheque immediately; they arranged delivery; and the painting was already listed on eBay."

Write — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, [A] and [B] [intended / did not intend] to create legal relations.

3. Consideration (including past consideration)

What this means: consideration = what each side gives in exchange for the other's promise (money, goods, a service, or a promise to do one of these). It doesn't have to be a fair price (need not be adequate), just something of value. But some things don't count (not sufficient): a vague promise, doing what you already had to do, and past consideration = something already given BEFORE the promise was made. If someone makes an extra promise after the deal is done ("I'll re-frame it for you") and gets nothing new for it, that promise can't be enforced. Treat each promise as a separate issue.

Write — Issue (for a later promise, write it as a separate issue)

Is [B]'s later promise to [re-frame the painting] enforceable by [A]?

Write — Law

For an agreement to be binding, the promisee must provide consideration for the promise received: the price paid, or promised, for the other party's promise. It may be money, goods, services or a promise to provide them (Carlill, where using the smoke ball as directed was consideration). It need not be adequate, only of some legal value (Thomas v Thomas, where £1 a year rent was enough). However, consideration must be sufficient, and past consideration is not good consideration: an act or payment completed before the promise is made cannot be the price of that promise (Roscorla v Thomas). In that case, after a horse had been sold, the seller promised it was sound and free from vice. When it proved vicious, the buyer could not enforce the promise because the only consideration, the purchase price, had been given before the promise was made.

This is very similar to Roscorla v Thomas: just as the promise about the horse's soundness was made only after the sale and payment, Mary's promise was made only after the $120,000 was handed over. [B]'s promise to [re-frame the painting] cannot be enforced, as past consideration is not sufficient consideration. (or, for the main deal:) This constitutes sufficient consideration.

4. Promissory estoppel

What this means: promissory estoppel = even if there is no contract (or no consideration), a court can stop someone going back on a clear promise if the other person relied on it, changed their position, and would be badly hurt. Estopped = not allowed to deny. Detriment = real harm or loss. The lecturer warned this is narrow and often misused: only use it if there is a clear promise, real reliance and real loss, AND no contract/consideration. If the question says "Contract Law 1 only", don't use it.

Write — Issue

Is [Promisor] estopped from going back on its promise to [Promisee] that […]?

Write — Law

A promise is normally enforceable only if it is contained in a contract supported by consideration. However, equity developed promissory estoppel so that a person who, by words or conduct, has led another to believe in a particular state of affairs will not be allowed to go back on it where it would be unjust or inequitable to do so (Moorgate v Twitchings). In Waltons Stores v Maher, Waltons led the Mahers to believe a lease would be completed; the Mahers demolished their building and began building a new one to Waltons' plans while Waltons secretly told its solicitors to "go slow", then withdrew. The High Court held Waltons was estopped from denying a binding agreement. The elements are: (1) a legal relationship exists or is anticipated; (2) a clear and unambiguous promise; (3) reliance by the promisee through a change of circumstances; (4) material detriment if the promise is not kept; and (5) it would be unconscionable for the promisor not to keep it. The court may order specific performance or damages (Giumelli v Giumelli).

Variants — pick the ONE row that matches

If…Write this (full sentences)Case to name
Clear promise + they spent money / built / moved / gave something up because of it(1) [A] and [B] anticipated a legal relationship, as they were [negotiating a lease]. (2) [Promisor] clearly promised that […]. (3) [Promisee] relied on it by […]. (4) [Promisee] will suffer material detriment, namely [$… spent], if the promise is not kept. (5) Similar to Waltons Stores v Maher, where the Mahers began building in reliance on Waltons' conduct, it would be unconscionable for [Promisor] to go back on its promise.Waltons Stores v Maher
Vague promise, or no real loss (e.g. they just felt disappointed)Unlike Waltons Stores v Maher, where the Mahers demolished and rebuilt in reliance on Waltons' conduct, [there was no clear promise / Promisee suffered no real detriment], so promissory estoppel does not apply.Waltons Stores v Maher (contrast)
Question says "Contract Law 1 only" or a valid contract already existsDon't write a promissory estoppel issue.—

Example: no past exam question has needed promissory estoppel; the Tutorial 5 Jack & Mary question excluded it ("Contract Law 1 only"), and in any case Jack suffered no real detriment in relying on Mary's re-frame promise.

5. Term or representation?

What this means: a term = a promise that is part of the contract, so if it is broken you can sue for breach of contract. A representation = something said during the deal that is NOT part of the contract, so you can't sue for breach of contract on it. To decide, the court asks what a reasonable person would think the parties meant, using 5 factors: language, context, timing, who said it (their expertise), and importance (did the other side rely on it?). Write one sentence per factor using the facts.

Write — Issue

Is [A]'s assurance that [the software could handle at least 10,000 concurrent users] an enforceable term of the contract between [Claimant] and [Defendant]?

Write — Law

Contract Law 2 is concerned with identifying the content of the bargain. A term is a promise that forms part of the contract and has promissory effect, so its breach is a breach of contract. A representation is a statement made in connection with the contract, which may be true or false, but which does not form part of it and has no promissory effect. To distinguish them, the court asks what a reasonable person would believe the parties intended about the contractual nature of the statement, considering: (1) the language used, since a strong statement such as "I assure you" or "I guarantee" is more likely to be a term; (2) the context in which it was made; (3) the timing, since a statement made immediately before the contract is more likely to be a term; (4) the maker of the statement and their relative knowledge and expertise; and (5) the importance of the statement and whether the other party obviously relied on it. A precise, measurable claim, such as "this machine can process 500 units per hour", is more likely to be a term, while an opinion such as "this machine should be perfect for your business" is a representation.

No case is given in the lecture for term vs representation: argue the 5 factors and use the lecture examples.

Variants — pick the ONE row that best describes the statement

If… (the statement is)Write this (full sentences)Authority
Strong, specific, measurable, said by an expert close to signing ("I assure you… at least 10,000 users", "I guarantee only 40,000 km")The statement is strong, specific and measurable, very like the lecture example "this machine can process 500 units per hour", and unlike a vague opinion such as "this machine should be perfect for your business". It was made [one week] before signing by [A], who as [head of technology] had expertise [Claimant] lacked. It is therefore likely to be a term.Lecture examples (term)
Opinion, vague, "I think", "should be", "lovely atmosphere", "one of our most popular"The statement that ["…"] is a vague opinion, like the lecture examples "this machine should be perfect for your business" and "I think you'll be very happy with it", so it is a representation rather than a term.Lecture examples (representation)
A factual claim that could go either way ("only one previous owner")The statement that ["…"] could be either a term or a representation, like the lecture example "this car has only had one previous owner", so the factors decide it: […].Lecture examples (either)

Write — Application (one sentence per factor)

Language: [A] "[assured]" [Claimant] that […], a [strong, specific and measurable] statement, like the lecture example "this machine can process 500 units per hour".
Context: the statement was made in the course of [lengthy commercial negotiations for a customised program, where scalability was a central concern].
Timing: it was made [one week] before the contract was signed, i.e. close to formation, which points to a term.
Maker: [A] is [head of the technology department] and based the statement on [similar projects]; [he] had expert knowledge that [Claimant] lacked, so [Claimant] was entitled to rely on it.
Importance: [Claimant] obviously relied on it: it "[provided GlobalCorp with confidence in the software's scalability]", and [capacity was critical to its business].
Against: [Defendant] may argue the statement was only an estimate based on [past projects], and that the parties did not put it in the written contract, suggesting it was not intended to be contractual. However, the strength of the words, [A]'s expertise and [Claimant]'s obvious reliance outweigh this.

Example (2024 S2 GlobalCorp): "Alex 'assured' GlobalCorp that the software would handle 'at least 10,000 concurrent users', a strong, specific and measurable statement, very like 'this machine can process 500 units per hour'. It was made in lengthy commercial negotiations for a customised program, one week before signing. Alex is head of Technovate's technology department and based it on similar projects, so GlobalCorp was entitled to rely on him. It 'provided GlobalCorp with confidence in the software's scalability'. Technovate may argue it was only an estimate and was not written into the contract; however, the strength of the words, Alex's expertise and GlobalCorp's reliance outweigh this."

Write — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, [A]'s assurance is more likely to be a term than a mere representation, subject to the parol evidence rule.

6. Parol evidence rule and collateral contract

What this means: parol evidence rule ("parol" = spoken) = if the contract is written down and looks complete, you normally can't add things that were said out loud before signing. There are 2 ways around it: (1) the contract was not meant to be wholly in writing (it is partly written, partly spoken), or (2) a collateral contract = a separate mini-contract: "I promise X, if you sign the main contract". Signing the main contract is what you "pay" for that promise. Breaking a collateral contract gets you damages only (you can't cancel the main contract).

Write — Issue

Does the parol evidence rule prevent [Claimant] from relying on [A]'s oral assurance, and if so, can [Claimant] enforce it as a collateral contract?

Write — Law

Under the parol evidence rule, where a contract is reduced to writing and appears to be entire, it is presumed that the writing contains all the terms, and evidence of any previous or contemporaneous oral agreement will not be admitted to add to or vary it (Mercantile Bank of Sydney v Taylor). The rule applies only where the written contract appears to be entire. There are two main exceptions. First, where the contract was not intended to be wholly in writing, oral evidence may establish further oral terms. Second, the oral statement may form a collateral contract.
A collateral contract requires: (1) an oral statement and a main written contract; (2) that the statement was intended as a promise, made to induce entry into the main contract; (3) that it is not inconsistent with the main written contract; and (4) that entry into the main contract is the consideration for the promise (De Lassalle v Guildford). In that case, before the tenant handed over the signed lease, the landlord orally assured him that the drains were in good order. The lease said nothing about drains, which proved defective, and the assurance was enforced as a collateral contract. Because it is the collateral contract that is breached, the innocent party cannot terminate the main contract but is entitled to damages.

Variants — pick the row(s) that match

The parol evidence rule therefore prevents [Claimant] from adding [A]'s oral statement as a term (Mercantile Bank of Sydney v Taylor), and [Claimant] must rely on a collateral contract.Mercantile Bank v Taylor

Given [six months of negotiations], the parties did not intend the contract to be wholly in writing, so [A]'s assurance may be proved as an oral term.Mercantile Bank v Taylor (exception)

7. Puffery (sales talk)

What this means: a mere puff = sales exaggeration that nobody is meant to take literally ("the best on the market", "will revolutionise your business", "Red Bull gives you wings"). It is not a term, so it can't be enforced. A statement stops being a puff if the speaker did something to show they were serious (like the £1,000 bank deposit in Carlill).

Write — Issue

Are [B]'s statements that [the software was "the best on the market" and would "revolutionise" the business] enforceable terms of the contract?

Write — Law

A "mere puff" is a statement that is clearly not intended to be taken seriously by the receiver, often found in advertising (for example, "Red Bull gives you wings"), and it does not create contractual obligations. In Carlill v Carbolic Smoke Ball Co, the company argued its advertisement was a mere puff, but the court held it was a serious promise because the company had deposited £1,000 in the bank "showing our sincerity". Vague statements of opinion such as "I think you'll be very happy with it" are representations, not terms. The five factors used to distinguish terms from representations also apply.

Unlike Carlill, where the £1,000 deposit showed sincerity, [B] offered [no research or statistics].Carlill (contrast)
Tentative opinion about the future ("likely to", "should", "I think")The statement that the [software] would "[likely enhance productivity]" is tentative ("likely") and an opinion about the future, similar to "I think you'll be very happy with it", so it is at most a representation.Lecture examples

Example (2024 S2 GlobalCorp): "Damon's claims that the software was 'the best on the market' and would 'revolutionise' GlobalCorp's operations are exaggerated, unquantifiable sales talk. Unlike Carlill, Damon offered no research or statistics. 'Likely enhance productivity' is tentative and an opinion about the future.
