[B]'s statements are mere puffery, or at most non-contractual representations, and are not enforceable terms of the contract.

8. Express written term: condition or warranty?

What this means: an express term = a term actually stated (written or spoken). If you signed it, you're bound by it even if you didn't read it. Once something is a term, decide how important it is: a condition = a major term at the heart of the deal; if broken, you can terminate (end the contract) AND get damages. A warranty = a minor term; if broken, damages (money) only. Test: would the client have signed without this promise? If not, it's a condition. (Implied term = a term the court reads in even though nobody said it — only if the contract is silent on something obvious.)

Write — Issue

Has [Defendant] breached the express term requiring ["industry-standard performance capabilities"], and is that term a condition or a warranty?

Write — Law

Express terms are those explicitly agreed by the parties, orally or in writing, and a party who signs a written contract is bound by its terms whether or not they have read them (L'Estrange v Graucob). Once a term is identified, the court determines its importance. A condition is fundamental to the contract, and its breach entitles the innocent party to terminate and claim damages (Poussard v Spiers, where a lead singer's absence from the opening performances allowed the producers to terminate). A warranty is a minor term whose breach gives damages only (Bettini v Gye, where a singer missing some rehearsals gave damages only). The test of essentiality asks whether the promise was so important to the innocent party that it would not have entered the contract without it (Associated Newspapers v Bancks, where a promise to publish a cartoonist's comic on the front page of the comic section was a condition).

Variants — pick the ONE row that matches

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case to name
The broken promise goes to the heart of the deal[Capacity] was so important to [Claimant] that it would not have entered the contract without it. Like the front-page placement in Associated Newspapers v Bancks and the lead singer's absence on opening night in Poussard v Spiers, this goes to the heart of the bargain, so the term is a condition, and [Claimant] may terminate and claim damages.Bancks; Poussard v Spiers

[Claimant] should treat this as a supporting argument rather than its main claim.L'Estrange v Graucob

Here, a term that […] is [necessary for the contract to work / so obvious it goes without saying], so it is likely to be implied.Codelfa v State Rail

Write — Application

[paste row: vague clause first if relevant, then condition or warranty]

Example (2024 S2 GlobalCorp): "The clause is an express written term binding on Technovate, but it is vague; whether 5,000 users falls below 'industry-standard performance capabilities' needs evidence the facts do not supply. If Alex's assurance is a term, 10,000-user capacity was essential: like the front-page placement in Bancks and the opening performances in Poussard, it goes to the heart of the bargain, as software that cannot handle GlobalCorp's users is 'not suitable' for its business. It is likely a condition, not a minor warranty as in Bettini v Gye."

Write — Conclusion

The written clause is a term, but proving its breach is uncertain. The [capacity] promise, if it is a term, is likely a condition.

9. Disclaimer Issue 1: is it a term?

What this means: a disclaimer (also called an exclusion clause) = a term saying "we're not responsible if X goes wrong". It only protects the business if it is actually part of the contract. There are 3 ways it gets in: (a) it was in a document the customer signed; (b) the customer was given reasonable notice of it BEFORE the contract was made (a sign seen after paying is too late); or (c) prior dealings = the customer had done the same deal many times before and should have known about it. First work out exactly WHEN and WHERE the contract was made.

Write — Issue

Is the disclaimer a term of the contract between [Customer] and [Business]?

Write — Law

A disclaimer is a term of a contract stating that one of the parties will not be in breach despite failing to perform one or more of their contractual obligations. Disclaimers are also known as exclusion, exemption or limitation of liability clauses. A disclaimer will be a term of the contract if it is: (a) incorporated into a written contract signed by the parties; (b) brought to the other party's attention by reasonable notice before the contract is formed; or (c) implied into the contract as a result of prior dealings between the parties.
Signed documents. A party who signs a written contract is bound by its terms whether or not they have read them (L'Estrange v Graucob, where Miss L'Estrange was bound by an exclusion clause in small print in an order form for a vending machine that she signed without reading).
Reasonable notice. With an unsigned document, a disclaimer is binding only if it was brought to the other party's attention by reasonable notice before the contract was entered into (Olley v Marlborough Court, where a disclaimer on a notice in a hotel bedroom was not a term because the contract had already been formed at the reception desk). Similarly, in Thornton v Shoe Lane Parking, conditions referred to on a car-park ticket came too late, because the contract was formed at the ticket machine. The court asks whether the document is one a reasonable person would expect to contain contractual terms, and whether the business did what was reasonable to bring the terms to the customer's notice.
Prior dealings. If the parties have made similar contracts in the past containing the disclaimer, it can be implied into the present contract (Balmain New Ferry Co v Robertson). In that case the notice was only visible after the customer paid for the ferry, but it was still a term because Robertson had travelled on the ferry on many previous occasions.

Variants — go through (a), (b), (c) in order; pick the matching sentence for each

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case to name
(a) Disclaimer is in the document the customer SIGNED[Customer] signed the [contract] containing the disclaimer, so [she] is bound by it. Similar to L'Estrange v Graucob, [Customer]'s failure to read the terms ("[I don't need to read this]") does not help [her].L'Estrange v Graucob
(a) Customer signed something, but the disclaimer was on a SEPARATE unsigned page[Business] will argue that the separate terms and conditions formed part of the contract [Customer] signed, so [she] is bound as in L'Estrange v Graucob. [Customer] may argue the disclaimer was not in the document [she] actually signed. The facts do not say whether the signed contract referred to the separate terms; I assume it did.L'Estrange v Graucob
(b) Customer was handed/shown the terms BEFORE paying or agreeingThe disclaimer was brought to [Customer]'s attention by reasonable notice before the contract was formed: [the attendant handed her the terms before she signed and paid], and the disclaimer was ["clearly visible"]. A document headed [terms and conditions] is exactly the kind a reasonable person would expect to contain contractual terms. This is unlike Olley and Thornton, where the terms were seen only after the contract was formed.Olley; Thornton (contrast)
(b) Notice only seen AFTER the contract was made (sign in the change room/bedroom; after paying)The disclaimer was not brought to [Customer]'s attention before the contract was formed, as the first time [she] could see it was [in the change room]. Like the bedroom notice in Olley v Marlborough Court, it came only after the contract was made at [the front counter], so it is not a term by notice.Olley v Marlborough Court
(b) Terms on a ticket from a machine / referred to on a ticketThe contract was formed when [Customer] [drove up to the ticket machine]. Similar to Thornton v Shoe Lane Parking, where the conditions referred to on a car-park ticket came too late, the disclaimer here came after the contract was formed and is not a term.Thornton v Shoe Lane Parking
(c) Customer has done this many times beforeHowever, [Business] can argue the disclaimer is a term because of prior dealings. Similar to Balmain New Ferry v Robertson, where Robertson had travelled on the ferry on many previous occasions, [Customer] has [been there 20 times in the past year], so had plenty of opportunity to read the disclaimer and ought to have known of it. Against: [only one or two visits / the clause has changed / she never saw it].Balmain New Ferry v Robertson
(c) First visit / no historyThere is no evidence that [Customer] had [used the cloakroom] before, so prior dealings (Balmain New Ferry v Robertson) [cannot be relied upon / do not need to be relied upon].Balmain New Ferry
Online: clicked "I accept" (clickwrap) vs terms only behind a link (browsewrap)Clickwrap: [Customer] clicked "I accept", which is generally binding even if the terms were unread, especially as [she had to scroll/tick a box]. Browsewrap: the terms were only behind a hyperlink with no active step; there is no clear Australian authority, and arguably there was neither acceptance nor reasonable notice.(principle — no case)

Write — Application

(a) [paste signed row] (b) [paste notice row] (c) [paste prior dealings row]

Example (2021 S1 Melissa): "Melissa signed the cloakroom contract, but the disclaimer was on a separate page of terms and conditions. BCC will argue that page formed part of the contract she signed, so as in L'Estrange v Graucob her refusal to read it ('I don't need to read this') does not help her. Even if it is unsigned, the attendant handed her the terms before she signed and paid, and the disclaimer was 'clearly visible', unlike Olley and Thornton where the terms came after the contract was formed. There is no evidence Melissa had used the cloakroom before, so prior dealings do not need to be relied upon."

Write — Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, the stronger argument is that the disclaimer [is / is not] a term of the contract, due to [the signed contract / reasonable notice / prior dealings / lack of reasonable notice].

10. Disclaimer Issue 2: does it cover this breach?

What this means: even if the disclaimer is a term, the court reads it carefully. Contra proferentem (Latin, "against the one who put it forward") = if a disclaimer in a business-vs-consumer contract is unclear, the court picks the meaning that is WORST for the business, so general words like "any loss" usually don't cover the business's own carelessness (negligence). Between two businesses, the clause is read neutrally. Scope = a disclaimer never covers things the business did that were completely outside what the contract was for (e.g. a "secure" cloakroom leaving the key on the desk).

Write — Issue

Will the disclaimer protect [Business] from liability for [the theft of Customer's coat]?

Write — Law

Where a disclaimer is contained in a contract between two businesses, the court presumes equal bargaining power and interprets it neutrally. However, if the disclaimer is uncertain or ambiguous and is contained in a contract between a business and a consumer, the court will interpret it contra proferentem, that is, against the party seeking to rely upon it. If there is more than one way of interpreting the disclaimer, the court will choose the interpretation that favours the consumer and does not apply to the breach (White v John Warwick). There, a clause stating that "nothing in this agreement shall render the owners liable for any personal injuries" to hirers of tricycles was read as excluding only contractual liability, not liability for the owners' negligence when the seat tipped forward and injured the hirer.
Disclaimers will also not exclude liability for acts occurring outside the scope of the contract (Sydney City Council v West). There, a car-park attendant negligently allowed a person to take a car without producing proof of a ticket. Despite a widely drafted clause excluding responsibility for loss "however… caused", the council was liable, because the attendant's act was outside the scope of the contract.

Variants — pick the row(s) that match

If… (situation in plain words)Write this (full sentences)Case to name
Business vs businessThis is a contract between two businesses, [A] and [B], so the court presumes equal bargaining power and will read the disclaimer neutrally, according to its ordinary meaning. On that reading, the words "[…]" [do / do not] cover [the loss].(principle)
Business vs consumer, and the wording is general ("any loss", "however arising") — doesn't mention negligenceThis is a contract between a business, [Business], and a consumer, [Customer], so the disclaimer will be read against [Business]. It is ambiguous: it is unclear whether "[any loss or damage… however that loss or damage may arise]" covers loss caused by customers themselves or by unavoidable events, or extends to loss caused by [Business]'s own negligence. As there is more than one interpretation, the court will choose the one that goes against [Business]. Similar to White v John Warwick, where general words did not exclude the owners' negligence, [Business] remains liable for loss caused by its own fault: [leaving the key on the desk].White v John Warwick
The business did the exact opposite of what the contract was for (key left out, gave goods to the wrong person, no one on duty)[Customer] will argue that the whole purpose of the contract was [the secure storage of her belongings]. This is very similar to Sydney City Council v West, where the purpose of the car park was that only the customer could retrieve the car on producing a ticket. The wording clearly and specifically covers this exact loss (e.g. expressly mentions "negligence of our staff")The disclaimer expressly refers to ["loss caused by the negligence of our staff"], so it is not ambiguous and contra proferentem does not help [Customer]. The stronger argument is that, although the disclaimer is a term of the contract, it does not protect [Business] from liability for its own negligent conduct, which was outside the scope of the contract. Therefore [Customer] can sue [Business] for [the loss of her coat].

Remedies

What this means: damages = money to put the client where they would have been if the contract had been kept. Terminate (also "rescind" here) = end the contract; only allowed for breach of a condition. Specific performance = court order to actually do what was promised; injunction = court order to stop doing something (both only if money isn't enough). Mitigate = the client must take reasonable steps to keep the loss small. Pick the row(s) that match what you found above.

Write — Law

The purpose of damages is to restore [Claimant] to the position it would have been in had the contract been properly performed. Damages are available for any breach of contract and for breach of a collateral contract. If the term breached is a condition, the innocent party may also terminate the contract (Poussard v Spiers); if it is a warranty, damages only (Bettini v Gye). The plaintiff must take reasonable steps to mitigate its loss (Payzu Ltd v Saunders). Where damages are inadequate, the court may order specific performance or an injunction.

Full mini-answer — 2024 Semester 2, Question 2: GlobalCorp v Technovate (25 marks)

Facts in one line: after 6 months of negotiations, Technovate contracts to build software for GlobalCorp; one week before signing, Alex (head of technology) "assured" it would handle at least 10,000 concurrent users; early on, Damon (junior sales rep) said it would "likely enhance productivity", was "the best on the market" and would "revolutionise" operations; it handles only 5,000 users and GlobalCorp loses $250,000; the poorly written contract only says "industry-standard performance capabilities".

Whether GlobalCorp can enforce the statements made by Alex and Damon depends upon three issues: (1) whether Alex's assurance is a term of the contract, or is otherwise enforceable despite the parol evidence rule as a collateral contract; (2) whether Damon's statements are terms or mere puffery; and (3) whether the written "industry-standard" clause has been breached. Remedies are then considered.

Issue 1: Alex's assurance

Issue. Is Alex's assurance that the software could handle at least 10,000 concurrent users an enforceable term of the contract between GlobalCorp and Technovate?

Law. A term is a promise that forms part of the contract, so its breach is a breach of contract; a representation is a statement made in connection with the contract that does not form part of it. The court asks what a reasonable person would believe the parties intended, considering: (1) the language used; (2) the context; (3) the timing; (4) the maker's relative knowledge and expertise; and (5) the importance of the statement and whether it was relied on. A precise claim such as "this machine can process 500 units per hour" is likely a term.

Under the parol evidence rule, where a contract is in writing and appears to be entire, evidence of prior oral agreements cannot be admitted to add to it (Mercantile Bank of Sydney v Taylor). The rule does not apply where the contract was not intended to be wholly in writing, or where the oral statement forms a collateral contract. A collateral contract requires: (1) an oral statement and a main written contract; (2) a promise made to induce entry into the main contract; (3) consistency with the main contract; and (4) entry into the main contract as consideration (De Lassalle v Guildford, where a landlord's oral assurance that the drains were in good order, given before the tenant handed over a lease silent on drains, was enforced). Breach of a collateral contract gives damages only.

Application. Language: Alex "assured" GlobalCorp of "at least 10,000 concurrent users", a strong, specific, measurable statement, very like "this machine can process 500 units per hour". Context: it was made in lengthy commercial negotiations for a customised program where scalability was central. Timing: it was made one week before signing. Maker: Alex is head of Technovate's technology department and relied on similar projects; GlobalCorp lacked this expertise. Importance: it "provided GlobalCorp with confidence in the software's scalability", and a 5,000-user limit was "not suitable" for its operations. Technovate may argue it was only an estimate and was not written into the contract; however, the strength of the words, Alex's expertise and GlobalCorp's reliance outweigh this.

Technovate will argue the parol evidence rule excludes the assurance. However, the contract "lacks detail and is very poorly written", does not address Technovate's points and has no entire-agreement clause, so after six months of negotiations it was not intended to be wholly in writing. Alternatively, there is a collateral contract: Alex's oral promise induced GlobalCorp to sign; it is consistent with the "industry-standard" clause; and GlobalCorp gave consideration by entering the main contract. Similar to De Lassalle v Guildford, where the drains assurance induced the tenant to sign a lease silent on drains, Alex's assurance induced GlobalCorp to sign a contract silent on capacity. The software handles only 5,000 users, so the promise is broken.

Conclusion. On the balance of probabilities, Alex's assurance is enforceable as a term or, alternatively, as a collateral contract, and Technovate is in breach.

Issue 2: Damon's statements

Issue. Are Damon's statements enforceable terms of the contract?

Law. A mere puff is a statement clearly not intended to be taken seriously, such as "Red Bull gives you wings", and creates no contractual obligation. In Carlill v Carbolic Smoke Ball Co, an advertisement was held not to be a puff because £1,000 had been deposited "showing our sincerity". Opinions such as "I think you'll be very happy with it" are representations, not terms.

Application. "The best on the market" and "revolutionise GlobalCorp's business operations" are exaggerated, unquantifiable sales talk. Unlike Carlill, Damon offered no research or statistics. "Likely enhance productivity" is a tentative opinion about the future. The statements were made at the start of negotiations, about six months before signing, by a junior sales representative. GlobalCorp may say productivity mattered to it, but the phrase is too vague to measure.

Conclusion. Damon's statements are mere puffery and are not enforceable terms.

Issue 3: The "industry-standard" clause

Issue. Has Technovate breached the express term requiring "industry-standard performance capabilities", and is the capacity promise a condition or a warranty?

Law. A party is bound by the terms of a written contract it signs (L'Estrange v Graucob). Breach of a condition allows termination and damages (Poussard v Spiers, where a lead singer missed the opening performances); breach of a warranty gives damages only (Bettini v Gye, missed rehearsals). A term is a condition if the innocent party would not have contracted without it (Associated Newspapers v Bancks, front-page placement).

Application. The clause is an express term but vague; whether 5,000 users is below industry standard needs expert evidence the facts do not supply, so it is a supporting argument only. The 10,000-user promise, like the front-page placement in Bancks, goes to the heart of the bargain, as the software is "not suitable" without it, so it is likely a condition.

Conclusion. The clause is a term but breach is uncertain; the capacity promise is likely a condition.

Remedies

Damages aim to put GlobalCorp where it would have been had the contract been performed. If Alex's assurance is a term and a condition, GlobalCorp may terminate and claim its $250,000 loss. If enforced only as a collateral contract, it cannot terminate the main contract but may recover the $250,000 in damages. GlobalCorp must mitigate its loss, for example by promptly seeking an upgrade or replacement (Payzu Ltd v Saunders). No remedy is available for Damon's statements.

Overall conclusion

The most appropriate course of action is for GlobalCorp to sue Technovate for breach of Alex's assurance, as a term or, alternatively, as a collateral contract, and claim damages of $250,000. It should not rely on Damon's statements.

≈1,150 words.

Short mini-answer — 2021 Semester 1: Melissa v Brisbane Convention Centre (cloakroom disclaimer)

Facts in one line: Melissa pays $10 and signs a cloakroom contract for her fur coat; before signing she is handed a separate page of T&Cs ("I don't need to read this") containing a "clearly visible" disclaimer: BCC "accepts no responsibility for any loss or damage… however that loss or damage may arise"; the attendant leaves the cloakroom unattended with the Locker 5 key on the desk and a thief steals the coat. Assume a contract was formed.

Whether the disclaimer protects the BCC depends upon two issues: (1) whether it is a term of the cloakroom contract; and (2) whether it applies to the breach in question. It is assumed a contract was formed when Melissa paid $10.

Issue 1

Issue. Is the disclaimer a term of the cloakroom contract between Melissa and the BCC?

Law. A disclaimer is a term stating that a party will not be in breach despite failing to perform its obligations. It is a term if it is in a signed contract (L'Estrange v Graucob, where an unread exclusion clause in a signed order form bound Miss L'Estrange), brought to the other party's attention by reasonable notice before the contract is formed (Olley v Marlborough Court, where a bedroom notice seen after booking at reception was too late), or implied by prior dealings (Balmain New Ferry v Robertson).

Application. BCC will argue the terms page formed part of the contract Melissa signed, so as in L'Estrange her refusal to read it does not help her. Even if unsigned, it was handed to her before she signed and paid, and the disclaimer was "clearly visible", unlike Olley, where notice came after the contract was formed. Prior dealings need not be relied on.

Conclusion. On the balance of probabilities, the disclaimer is a term of the contract.

Issue 2

Issue. Will the disclaimer protect the BCC from liability for the theft of Melissa's coat?

Law. In a business–consumer contract, an ambiguous disclaimer is read contra proferentem, against the party relying on it (White v John Warwick, where a clause excluding liability for "any personal injuries" did not cover the owners' negligence). A disclaimer also does not cover acts outside the scope of the contract (Sydney City Council v West, where a car-park attendant let someone take a car without a ticket and a "however caused" clause did not protect the council).

Application. This is a business–consumer contract. "Any loss… however that loss or damage may arise" does not mention negligence, so read against the BCC, it does not cover its own carelessness, as in White. The cloakroom's purpose was secure storage; leaving it unattended with the Locker 5 key on the desk is, as in West, outside the scope of the contract.

Conclusion. The disclaimer does not protect the BCC, which is liable for the coat.

Remedies

Melissa may claim damages equal to the coat's value, proved by evidence of its market or replacement value, and must mitigate her loss, e.g. by cooperating with police (Payzu Ltd v Saunders).

≈480 words.

Glossary

TermPlain English
Contract (simple / formal)A legally binding agreement (spoken, written or by conduct / must be in writing, e.g. land).
OfferA firm proposal to make a deal right now on stated terms.
Offeror / offereeThe person making the offer / the person it is made to.
Invitation to treatAn invitation to start negotiating (ads, shelves, price lists) — not an offer.
Offer to the worldAn offer anyone can accept by doing what it asks (e.g. a reward).
AcceptanceA clear, unconditional "yes" to all the terms of the offer.
Counter-offerA reply that changes the terms; it kills the first offer and becomes a new offer.
RejectionA plain "no", with no new terms.
RevocationThe offeror withdrawing the offer before it is accepted.
LapseAn offer expiring because too much time passed.
Postal ruleAn acceptance by post counts from the moment it is posted.
Unilateral contractA contract accepted by doing the task, not by saying yes.
Objective testWhat a reasonable outsider would think, not what someone secretly meant.
Intention to create legal relationsMeaning the deal to be enforceable in court.
Presumption / rebuttedAn automatic starting assumption / disproved by evidence.
OnusWho has to prove something.
ConsiderationWhat each side gives in exchange for the other's promise.
Promisor / promiseeThe person who makes the promise / the person it is made to.
Adequate / sufficientA fair price (not needed) / something the law recognises as having value (needed).
Past considerationSomething already given before the promise was made — doesn't count.
Existing dutySomething you were already obliged to do — doesn't count as new consideration.
Practical benefitA real advantage the promisor gets, which can make the promise enforceable.
Promissory estoppel / estoppedEquity stops someone breaking a clear promise the other relied on to their detriment / not allowed to deny.
DetrimentReal harm or loss.
Equity / unconscionableCourt rules based on fairness / grossly unfair.
TermA promise that is part of the contract.
RepresentationA statement made during the deal that is not part of the contract.
Express term / implied termA term actually stated / a term the court reads in.
Business efficacyWhat is needed for the contract to work at all.
Condition / warrantyA major term (breach → terminate + damages) / a minor term (breach → damages only).
Test of essentialityWould they have signed without this promise? If not, it's a condition.
Parol evidence ruleYou can't add spoken terms to a written contract that looks complete.
Entire-agreement clauseA clause saying the written document is the whole deal.
Collateral contractA side-promise that persuaded someone to sign the main contract; breach → damages only.
InducePersuade; be a reason for signing.
Mere puff / pufferySales exaggeration nobody takes literally.
Disclaimer / exclusion / exemption / limitation clauseA term saying "we are not responsible if this goes wrong".
IncorporatedMade part of the contract.
Reasonable noticeThe business did what was reasonable to show the customer the terms before the deal.
Prior dealingsThe same deal done many times before, so the customer should know the terms.
Clickwrap / browsewrapOnline terms accepted by clicking "I accept" / terms only behind a link with no click.
Contra proferentemAn unclear clause is read against the business that wrote it.
Negligence / negligentCarelessness.
Scope of the contractWhat the contract was for; acts completely outside it aren't covered by a disclaimer.
B2B / B2CBusiness-to-business / business-to-consumer.
DamagesMoney compensation.
Terminate / rescind / repudiationEnd the contract / (repudiation = a breach so serious the other side may end it).
Specific performance / injunctionCourt order to do what was promised / court order to stop doing something.
MitigateTake reasonable steps to keep your loss small.
On the balance of probabilitiesMore likely than not.
W6 Exact words: Intellectual property

Use this page when…

  • Someone records, photocopies, reposts, uploads or sells copies of a lecture, song, film, photo, book, manual or website → copyright (headings 1–5).
  • The question says "explore protection, ownership and any defences" → copyright, all five headings.
  • A similar business name, logo, colours, cup/bottle shape; customers think two businesses are linked; a celebrity's image used in an ad → trade mark (6) + passing off (7).
  • An employee leaves with a recipe, customer list, business plan, or an idea pitched in confidence is used → breach of confidence (8).

  3. Copyright Issue 2: Ownership
  4. Copyright Issue 3: Infringement (3 requirements)
  5. Copyright Issue 4: Defences (fair dealing)
  6. Copyright Remedies
  7. Trade mark infringement (only if a name/logo/shape is involved)
  8. Passing off (always run with trade mark; alone if unregistered)
  9. Breach of confidence (only if secret information)
  10. Overall conclusion

Opener

What this means: one sentence listing your issues. Intellectual property (IP) = things created by the mind (writing, music, a logo, an invention) that the law lets the owner stop others copying.

Whether [D1 and D2] have committed an intellectual property breach depends on whether copyright protects [the work], who owns that copyright, whether [D1 and D2] infringed it, and whether any defence applies.

Trade mark version: "[P]'s claims against [D] depend on two issues: first, whether [D] has infringed [P]'s registered trade marks; and second, whether [D] has committed the tort of passing off."

Example (2021 S1 Alex and Travis): Whether Alex and Travis have committed an intellectual property breach depends on whether copyright protects the lecture, who owns that copyright, whether Alex and Travis infringed it, and whether any defence applies.

Copyright Issue 1: Protection

What this means: copyright = the right to stop people copying the expression of an idea (the actual words, notes, picture) — never the idea itself. It is automatic: no registration needed. Something is protected if it is (1) a "work" (or other protected thing like a recording), (2) original (the author made it themselves — it doesn't need to be new or clever), and (3) in material form (written down, saved or recorded somewhere).

WRITE — Issue

Is [the lecture / photo / manual / song] protected by copyright under the Copyright Act 1968 (Cth)?

WRITE — Law

Intellectual property is an intangible product of intellectual effort, and IP law gives its owner the right to prevent others from copying, using or exploiting it without permission. Copyright is the legal right to prevent unauthorised copying of the expression of an idea; it does not protect the idea itself. Copyright is regulated by the Copyright Act 1968 (Cth) ('CA') and arises automatically, without registration. A creation is protected if: (1) it is a 'work' or 'subject matter other than works'; (2) it is original; and (3) it is expressed in a material form.

Work. Section 32 protects original literary, dramatic, musical and artistic works. A literary work is the expression of an idea in text, such as a book, article, email, manual or written information: Mirror Newspapers v Queensland Newspapers [1982]. The CA also separately protects 'subject matter other than works', including sound recordings (s 89), films (s 90), broadcasts (s 91) and published editions (s 92), so several layers of copyright may exist in the one product.
Original. A work is original if it originates from the author through independent intellectual effort rather than being copied; it need not be new or unique, and the threshold is low (s 32).
Material form. Copyright protects the expression of an idea, not ideas or facts themselves: John Fairfax & Sons v Australian Consolidated Press (1960). 'Material form' means 'any form (whether visible or not) of storage' (s 10). In Donoghue v Allied Newspapers (1938), a jockey told his stories to a journalist, who wrote them up; the journalist, who created the particular expression, was the author, and the jockey had no copyright merely because he supplied the ideas orally.

Example (2021 S1 Alex and Travis): Professor Butler read out comprehensive teaching notes he prepared before class; they are written text, so a literary work (s 32), original because he prepared them himself, and in material form because they were written and stored before the lecture (s 10). Charley's talk was unscripted: arguably never stored, but Travis's recording stored it (s 10), and applying Donoghue, Charley — who chose the words — is the author, not Travis, who merely pressed record.

Copyright Issue 2: Ownership

What this means: who owns the copyright — not always the person who made it. Owning the physical thing (a DVD, a printed photo) is NOT owning the copyright. Default: the author (creator) owns it. But an employee's work done as part of their job belongs to the employer. An independent contractor (hired for a job, not an employee) keeps it unless agreed.

WRITE — Issue

Who owns the copyright in [the work]?

WRITE — Law

Ownership of copyright is separate from ownership of the physical object. The author is generally the first owner (s 35(2)). However, where a work is created by an employee in pursuance of the terms of their employment, the employer generally owns the copyright (s 35(6)); the creator must be an employee rather than an independent contractor, and the work must be made under their employment duties: Redrock Holdings v Hinkley (2001). An independent contractor generally keeps copyright unless otherwise agreed; a person who commissions a work for a private or domestic purpose is generally the first owner (s 35(5)); and these default rules may be changed by agreement (s 35(3)). University IP policies may allow academics to keep copyright in certain scholarly works.

Employee, but the work might be outside their duties (argue both sides)[Doing X] is arguably within [his/her] duties, in which case [employer] owns it under s 35(6). Example (2021 S1): Professor Butler is UB's "usual lecturer", so a UB employee; teaching notes are central to his duties, so under s 35(6) UB likely owns them — unless a UB IP policy lets him keep them (s 35(3)). Either way, the owner is not Alex or Travis.

WRITE — Conclusion

[Owner] is likely the owner of the copyright in [the work], unless [an IP policy or agreement provides otherwise].

Copyright Issue 3: Infringement

What this means: infringement = breaking copyright, by doing one of the owner's exclusive rights (things only the owner may do: copy, publish, perform, share online, adapt) without permission. Three requirements: a substantial part was copied (judged by importance, not amount — even a few bars can count), the copy is objectively similar (looks/sounds like the original), and there's a causal connection (the copy came from the original, not made independently).

WRITE — Issue

Have [D1 and D2] infringed the copyright in [the work]?

WRITE — Law

Under s 31 of the CA, the owner has the exclusive rights to reproduce the work in a material form, publish it, perform it in public, communicate it to the public and make an adaptation. Infringement occurs when a person exercises any of these rights without permission, and requires: (1) a substantial part was copied; (2) objective similarity; and (3) a causal connection.
Substantial part is qualitative, looking at the importance of what was copied rather than its amount. In Hawkes & Son v Paramount Film Service (1934), a short but distinctive part of a march in a newsreel was substantial; in EMI Songs Australia v Larrikin Music (2011), two bars of "Kookaburra" in the flute riff of "Down Under" were substantial.
Objective similarity compares the particular expression, not the general idea. In Zeccola v Universal Studios (1982), "Great White" copied the characters, situations and sequence of events of "Jaws", not just the idea of a killer shark, and so infringed.
Causal connection requires that the copy be derived from the original rather than created independently; it may be inferred from access plus similarity, and need not be conscious: in Bright Tunes v Harrisongs Music (1976), George Harrison subconsciously copied "He's So Fine" in "My Sweet Lord".

Which exclusive right did each defendant use? Pick every row that applies

Sold or handed out copiesBy [selling copies to other students], [D] published the work without permission.s 31 (publish)
Uploaded, posted online, emailed widely, streamedBy [uploading it to …], [D] communicated the work to the public without permission.s 31 (communicate)
Played/performed it in public (a gig, a shop, an event)By [playing the song at …], [D] performed the work in public without permission.s 31 (perform)
Changed it into another version (translation, remix, script → film)By [translating / turning it into …], [D] made an adaptation without permission.s 31 (adapt)
Asked someone else to do the copying[D2] asked [D1] to make the [recording], and then [sold it], so [D2] also exercised the owner's exclusive rights.s 31

Example (2021 S1): Travis reproduced the lecture by recording it, without UB's permission; Alex asked him to and then sold copies, publishing it. The whole two-hour lecture was copied — if two bars were substantial in EMI v Larrikin, the entire lecture clearly is. A sound recording is an exact copy, unlike Zeccola. Travis recorded it directly and deliberately — stronger than Bright Tunes.

WRITE — Conclusion

Therefore, [D1] has likely infringed copyright by [reproducing the work], and [D2] by [procuring and selling it], unless a defence applies.

Copyright Issue 4: Defences

What this means: a defence is an excuse the law accepts. The main one is fair dealing: you may use copyright material without permission IF it's for one of the listed purposes (study, review, parody, news, legal advice) AND the use is fair (not too much, not for selling). Both must be true.

WRITE — Issue

Can [D1 or D2] rely on the defence of fair dealing?

WRITE — Law

A defendant may argue that no substantial part was copied, or rely on an exception under the CA. The fair dealing provisions (ss 40–43, 103A–104) permit use of copyright material without permission where the dealing is fair and is for a recognised purpose: research or study; criticism or review; parody or satire; reporting news; or judicial proceedings or professional advice. Whether a dealing is fair depends on the circumstances, including the purpose and extent of the use.

Pick the ONE purpose row that matches, then ALWAYS add the "is it fair?" row

If… (D's reason)Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
Student studying / research[D] may argue that [the copy] was made so [he/she] could study [the lecture he missed], which is a purpose of research or study. In favour of fairness, [he is an enrolled student entitled to attend].CA ss 40–43, 103A–104
A review or criticism[D] used [extract] in [a review of …], which is for the purpose of criticism or review.same
A joke / parody / satire[D]'s [video] mocks […], which is for the purpose of parody or satire.same
News reporting[D] used [clip] to report [news event], which is for the purpose of reporting news.same

Sold for moneyIn any event, [D]'s sale of [copies] is a commercial purpose, not [research or study], and so falls outside fair dealing altogether.same
Only a tiny unimportant bit copied[D] may argue no substantial part was copied, because […]. (If the whole work was copied:) The "no substantial part" defence fails, because the whole [work] was copied.Hawkes; EMI v Larrikin

Example (2021 S1): No substantial part fails — the whole lecture was copied. Alex may argue it was for study of a lecture he missed. Against: the whole lecture was copied, secretly, in breach of UB's policy, and Travis made it for someone else. Alex's sales are commercial and fall outside fair dealing altogether.

WRITE — Conclusion

Fair dealing might arguably cover [D's private study], but it will not protect [the secret copying], and it clearly cannot excuse [the sales].

Copyright Remedies

What this means: what the owner can get from the court. Injunction = court order to stop. Damages = money for the owner's loss. Account of profits = hand over the money the defendant made from the copying (choose damages OR account of profits).

[Owner] may seek an injunction to stop further [copying and sales] (s 115(2)). It may also seek either damages for any loss caused (s 115(2)–(3)) or an account of the profits [D] made from [selling the recording] (s 115(2)–(3)).

Example (2021 S1): UB (or Charley, for his part, if he is the owner) may seek an injunction to stop further copying and sales (s 115(2)), and either damages or an account of the profits Alex made from selling the recording (s 115(2)–(3)).

Trade mark infringement

What this means: a trade mark = a name, logo, shape, colour or sound that tells customers which business a product comes from. Trade marks MUST be registered to be protected under the Act. Infringement = someone uses the same or a deceptively similar mark (close enough to confuse customers) on the same/similar goods. If it isn't registered → skip to passing off.

WRITE — Issue

Can [P] bring an action against [D] for infringement of its registered trade mark under the Trade Marks Act 1995 (Cth)?

WRITE — Law

A trade mark is a distinctive word, phrase or symbol used in commercial dealings to show a connection between a business and its product (s 17 TMA). It may be letters, a word, a name, a device, packaging, a shape, a colour, a sound or a scent: in Coca-Cola v All-Fect (1999), Coca-Cola's registered contour bottle shape stopped All-Fect selling lollies in the same shape. Trade marks must be registered to be protected under the TMA. A registered trade mark is infringed if: (1) the owner's mark is registered under the TMA; (2) the defendant uses a mark substantially identical with or deceptively similar to it; and (3) on goods or services the same as or closely related to those it is registered for. A mark is deceptively similar if it nearly resembles the registered mark and is likely to mislead or cause confusion (s 44): in Torpedoes v Thorpedo (2003), 'THORPEDO' was deceptively similar to the registered 'TORPEDOES' for clothing. Protection is limited to the registered classes, unless the mark is well known in Australia: Eastman v John Griffiths (1898); Virgin Enterprises v Klapsas (2001).

Pick the matching row for each element

If…Write this (full sentences)Case/section to name
Registered[P] registered ['MARK'] in [year] for [goods/services], so the first element is satisfied.TMA
NOT registered[The cup design] is not registered, so trade mark infringement fails at the first element, and [P] must rely on passing off.—
One letter different / sounds the same / same picture and colours['D MARK'] differs from ['MARK'] by [a single letter], and [when spoken they are almost indistinguishable]. Similar to Torpedoes v Thorpedo, where a minor spelling change did not prevent deceptive similarity, the change here does not alter the overall impression. Evidence of actual confusion — [customers believing it was a new branch] — shows it is likely to mislead or cause confusion. [D] may argue [the words differ / the image is common]; however, [the combination of features] makes this weak.s 44; Torpedoes v Thorpedo
Same goods/services[D] uses the mark for [a coffee shop], exactly the [services] for which [P]'s mark is registered.TMA
Different goods — but P's mark is famousAlthough [D]'s goods are outside [P]'s registered classes, ['MARK'] is well known in Australia, so, as in Eastman v John Griffiths (KODAK) and Virgin Enterprises v Klapsas, use on unrelated goods can be stopped.Eastman; Virgin v Klapsas

WRITE — Conclusion + Remedies

Therefore, [D] has likely infringed [P]'s registered trade mark. Under the TMA, [P] may obtain an injunction to prevent further use of [the name and logo], and either damages or an account of profits.

Example (practice MoonBean v Ruby): "MoonBeam" differs from the registered "MOONBEAN" by one letter and sounds almost identical; similar to Torpedoes v Thorpedo, the change doesn't alter the overall impression, and customers posted "Didn't know MoonBean opened a new store!".

Passing off

What this means: a tort (civil wrong) where a business pretends — openly or by hints like copying the look — that it is connected with, or approved by, another business or person. Works even if nothing is registered. Three elements: a misrepresentation of connection, made in trade, intended to deceive buyers.

WRITE — Issue

Can [P] bring an action against [D] for the tort of passing off?

WRITE — Law

Where a trade mark or get-up is unregistered, the owner may still prevent its misuse through the tort of passing off. A defendant commits passing off if they misrepresent themselves or their product as having some kind of connection with the plaintiff. The plaintiff must show that: (1) the defendant made a misrepresentation, express or implied, that its goods or services are connected with the plaintiff or have the plaintiff's endorsement or approval; (2) the misrepresentation was made in the course of trade; and (3) the misrepresentation was intended to deceive potential purchasers. In Pacific Dunlop v Hogan (1989), a shoe advertisement parodying the knife scene from Crocodile Dundee was passing off, because viewers would assume Paul Hogan had endorsed or was connected with the shoes.

WRITE — Application (one sentence-set per element)

Misrepresentation. [D] never expressly claimed to be part of [P]. However, by using [a near-identical name / the same logo / the same cups], [D] impliedly represented that [its café] is connected with [P]. As in Pacific Dunlop v Hogan, where viewers inferred an endorsement that was never stated, [customers here inferred a connection: "quote"]. Against: [D] may argue some customers were merely confused, and confusion alone is not enough (McWilliam's Wines v McDonald's (1980)); but [several customers positively believed there was a connection].
In the course of trade. [The name/get-up] is used in selling [goods] to the public, so this element is clearly satisfied.
Intention to deceive. [D] knew of [P]'s business because [she worked there / it is nearby], and copied [several features at once], so an intention to deceive can be inferred. Against: [D] says ["innocent reason"]; however, that does not explain […].

Example (MoonBean v Ruby): Ruby used a name one letter off, the same moon logo and identical navy-and-gold cups; customers posted that MoonBean had "opened a new store", as in Pacific Dunlop v Hogan. She worked at MoonBean for two years and opened 500 m away, so intention to deceive can be inferred; "I just like the moon" doesn't explain the cups.

WRITE — Conclusion + Remedies

On the balance of probabilities, [D] has committed the tort of passing off. [P] may seek damages to restore it to the position it would have been in had the tort not been committed, and an injunction restraining [D] from continuing to use [the name, logo and get-up].

Breach of confidence

What this means: a tort for misusing secret information you were trusted with (a recipe, customer list, business plan, an idea pitched in private). Unlike copyright, it can protect an idea that was only spoken. Three elements: the info is confidential, it was given under an obligation of confidence (said or implied, e.g. employee), and it was used without permission.

WRITE — Issue

Can [P] bring an action against [D] for the tort of breach of confidence?

WRITE — Law

Breach of confidence is a tort committed when a person uses or discloses information given to them on a confidential basis without the owner's consent. It requires: (1) the information is confidential in nature, not trivial or public knowledge; (2) it was given in circumstances where an obligation of confidence was expressly made known and accepted, or implied from the context, such as an employer/employee relationship: NP Generations v Feneley (2001); and (3) there has been an unauthorised use or threatened use. The information need not be in material form: in Fraser v Thames Television (1984), an idea for a TV series pitched verbally was protected. A confidentiality agreement or a reasonable restraint of trade clause in an employment contract may also give a separate contractual claim.

WRITE — Application

Confidential. [The recipe / customer list] is not public knowledge and is valuable to [P].
Obligation. [D] received it [as an employee / under a confidentiality agreement / in a private pitch], so an obligation of confidence is [implied / express], as in NP Generations v Feneley. (Idea only spoken:) Similar to Fraser v Thames Television, it does not matter that [the idea] was only communicated verbally.
Unauthorised use. [D] has used it to [start a rival business] without [P]'s consent.

WRITE — Conclusion + Remedies

Therefore, [D] has likely committed breach of confidence; [P] may seek an injunction and damages, plus a contract claim if [a confidentiality / restraint of trade clause] was breached.

Overall conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, [D1 and D2] have infringed [the copyright in the work / P's trade mark], which is most likely owned by [owner], and [D2's sales] attract the greatest liability.
Full mini-answer — 2021 S1 Q2: Alex and Travis (all blanks filled)

Whether Alex and Travis have committed an intellectual property breach depends on whether copyright protects the lecture, who owns that copyright, whether Alex and Travis infringed it, and whether any defence applies.

Issue 1: Protection

Issue: Is the lecture given by Professor Butler and Charley protected by copyright under the Copyright Act 1968 (Cth)?

Law: Copyright is the right to prevent unauthorised copying of the expression of an idea; it does not protect the idea itself. It arises automatically under the Copyright Act 1968 (Cth) ('CA'). A creation is protected if: (1) it is a 'work' or 'subject matter other than works'; (2) it is original; and (3) it is expressed in material form. Section 32 protects literary works, being text such as written information (Mirror Newspapers v Queensland Newspapers [1982]); sound recordings are separately protected (s 89). A work is original if it comes from the author's independent intellectual effort; the threshold is low. 'Material form' means 'any form (whether visible or not) of storage' (s 10). In Donoghue v Allied Newspapers (1938), a journalist who wrote up a jockey's stories was the author, because he created the expression.

Application: Professor Butler read out comprehensive teaching notes he prepared before class. They are written text, so a literary work; original, because he prepared them himself; and in material form, being written and stored (s 10). Charley's talk was unscripted, so material form is arguable. On one view it was never stored by Charley and so is unprotected; on the other, Travis's recording stored it (s 10). Applying Donoghue, the author is whoever created the expression — Charley, who chose the words, not Travis, who merely pressed record. Travis's recording itself is a sound recording (s 89), but that gives him no right to the lecture content.

Conclusion: Copyright likely subsists in Professor Butler's notes, and probably in Charley's words once recorded.

Issue 2: Ownership

Issue: Who owns the copyright in Professor Butler's and Charley's material?

Law: The author is generally the first owner (s 35(2)), but an employee's work made in pursuance of their employment belongs to the employer (s 35(6)); it must be made under their duties: Redrock Holdings v Hinkley (2001). Agreements and university IP policies may change this (s 35(3)).

Application: Professor Butler is UB's "usual lecturer", so an employee, and teaching notes are central to his duties; under s 35(6) UB likely owns them, unless a UB policy lets him keep them (s 35(3)). Charley is "another academic at UB"; a guest talk is arguably within his duties (UB owns), but it drew on his separate work as a corporate lawyer, so it may fall outside them (Charley owns under s 35(2)). As the facts are silent, I assume it was part of his duties.

Conclusion: UB is likely the owner of both sessions — in any event, not Alex or Travis.

Issue 3: Infringement

Issue: Have Travis and Alex infringed the copyright in the lecture?

Law: Under s 31 the owner has exclusive rights to reproduce, publish, perform in public, communicate to the public and adapt the work. Infringement requires: (1) a substantial part copied — qualitative: two bars of "Kookaburra" were substantial in EMI Songs Australia v Larrikin Music (2011), and a short distinctive extract in Hawkes & Son v Paramount Film Service (1934); (2) objective similarity, comparing expression not ideas: Zeccola v Universal Studios (1982); and (3) a causal connection, which may even be subconscious: Bright Tunes v Harrisongs Music (1976).

Application: Travis reproduced the lecture in a material form by recording it, without the permission of UB, which has a no-recording policy. Alex procured the recording and then sold it, publishing it. The whole two-hour lecture was copied; if two bars were substantial in EMI v Larrikin, the entire lecture clearly is. A recording is an exact reproduction; unlike Zeccola, no comparison of plot is needed. Travis recorded it directly and deliberately, a stronger case than Bright Tunes.

Conclusion: Travis likely infringed by reproducing the lecture, and Alex by procuring and selling it, unless a defence applies.

Issue 4: Defences

Issue: Can Travis or Alex rely on fair dealing?

Law: Fair dealing (CA ss 40–43, 103A–104) permits use that is fair and for research or study, criticism or review, parody or satire, reporting news, or judicial proceedings or professional advice. Fairness depends on purpose and extent.

Application: "No substantial part" fails, as the whole lecture was copied. Alex may argue the recording was for studying a lecture he missed. Against: the whole lecture was copied, "secretly", in breach of UB's policy, and Travis made it for someone else, so it was not fair. Alex's sales are commercial and fall outside fair dealing altogether.

Conclusion: Fair dealing might arguably cover Alex's private listening, but not Travis's secret recording, and clearly not the sales.

Remedies

UB (or Charley for his part, if he owns it) may seek an injunction to stop further copying and sales (s 115(2)), and either damages or an account of the profits Alex made from selling the recording (s 115(2)–(3)). On the balance of probabilities, both Travis and Alex have infringed copyright in the lecture, and Alex's sales attract the greatest liability.

TermPlain English
Intellectual property (IP)Creations of the mind (writing, music, logos, inventions) the law lets the owner control.
CopyrightRight to stop others copying the expression (actual words/notes/image) of an idea. Automatic.
Expression vs ideaThe idea ("a story about a shark") is free; the particular words/pictures are protected.
Work (literary/musical/dramatic/artistic)Text / music / dance-script / art-photo-design — the four kinds of "works" in s 32.
Subject matter other than worksSound recordings, films, broadcasts, published editions — also protected (ss 89–92).
OriginalMade by the author's own effort, not copied (doesn't need to be new).
Material formStored somewhere — written, saved, recorded (s 10).
AuthorThe person who created the expression.
Independent contractorSomeone hired for a job who isn't an employee (keeps copyright unless agreed).
CommissionedPaid someone to create it for you (e.g. wedding photos).
Exclusive rightsThings only the owner can do: reproduce, publish, perform, communicate, adapt (s 31).
InfringementDoing one of the owner's exclusive rights without permission.
Substantial partAn important part — judged by quality, not quantity.
Objective similarityThe copy actually looks/sounds like the original.
Causal connectionThe copy came from the original (not made independently).
A fortiori"All the more so" — if a small part counts, the whole thing definitely does.

Deceptively similarClose enough to the registered mark to confuse or mislead customers.
Class (of goods/services)The category of products the mark is registered for.

Question names the CCA / ACCC / Part IV.
